Part I

Introduction

=== [PDF page 14] ===
CHAPTER 1

Introduction to the problem

1.1 Background

For centuries, turbulent flows over three-dimensional bluff bodies have been chal-
lenging issues in fluid dynamic research. Leonardo da Vinci was already fascinated
by the vortex structure in flows over prismatic obstacles. His sketched observations
of flow visualisation experiments are well known and are frequently used to illus-
trate the complexity of such kind of bluff-body flows. One of his sketches is shown in
Fig. 1.1, taken over from Frisch [52]. The sketch represents the flow past a prismatic
vertical wall-mounted object. The drawing clearly shows the turbulent nature of the
flow, particularly in the wake of the obstacle. Da Vinci was probably the first who
identified the so-called horseshoe vortex, which is induced in front of the obstacle
and wraps around its base.

When such a bluff body is internally heated, it will be convectively cooled by the
fluid flow. The rate of convective heat transfer, usually expressed in terms of the heat
transfer coefficient, is proportional to the local fluid velocity and the temperature
difference between the body surface and the fluid. However, in complex situations
the heat transfer rate is also dependent on the immediate flow structure around
the obstacles. Such vortical structures will lead to steep spatial gradients in the
distributions of the local time-averaged convective heat transfer. In addition to this,
flow instabilities may lead to periodic flow phenomena (like the well-known vortex
shedding at the sides of the obstacle) which affect the time-dependent convective
heat transfer.

Turbulent flow around and heat transfer to or from bluff bodies can be found
in various practical situations encountered in engineering. For instance in wind-
engineering applications, possible aerodynamic interaction between two bluff bod-
ies, such as the pylons of bridges or tall buildings in closely populated areas, is of
crucial importance for their dynamical and mechanical characteristics, since flow-
induced excitations can result in severe material damage. It is therefore of major
importance to understand the flow and vortical structures around these bluff bodies.
Further, examples can be found in steam and gas turbines, steam generators and
heat exchangers, where the structural integrity of the equipment depends on the lo-
cal flow structures. An illustrating example is the convective heat transfer from the
high-temperature combustion gases to the blades in a turbine which, if insufficient,
may lead to overheating of the blade material and, therefore, to irreversible damage
of the turbine blades which shortens the life time of the machinery. To prevent
the turbine blades from overheating, cold air is usually recirculated through cooling
passages inside the blade. These internal passages are equipped with wall-mounted
protruding ribs to enhance the overall convective heat transfer. For optimum de-
sign of the cooling passages in turbine blades, detailed knowledge of the local wall

il

heat transfer and the associated local flow properties is required. Several studies
were focussed on this subject [17, 31, 70, 71, 72, 73]. In the present work, we are
concerned with another important application, i.e. the thermal performance of elec-
tronic printed circuit boards. The research described in this thesis was sponsored
by Philips, Nederlandse Philips Bedrijven B.V., in order to get answers to questions
related to local overheating problems of integrated circuits (chips) and electronic
components. Wrong predictions of the heat transfer may lead to critical local hot
spots which obviously reduce the lifetime of an electronic apparatus.

1.2 Electronics cooling application

Severe local overheating of crucial components, leading to component damage, is an
important cause of technical failure of electronic equipment [87]. In order to increase
the life-time and the product reliability of an apparatus, the thermal performance of
such systems during operation needs to be well known. It is the task of an engineer
to adequately design the electronic system according to the thermal requirements.
Despite extensive research in this topic for decades, the problem of local overheating
is still actual. This is mainly due to the miniaturisation of electronic circuitry

=== [PDF page 16] ===
1.2. Electronics cooling application 13

and the ever increasing power dissipation in printed circuit boards which impose
more and more stringent demands for an efficient thermal management. To keep
the manufacturing costs and the noise level of the apparatus as low as possible,
designers solve the problem with optimum design rather than with additional cooling
capacity provided by extra fans. The various topics related to thermal management
of electronic parts and systems is elaborately discussed in an overview by Lasance
[87].

Printed circuit boards are characterised by a complex composition of a range of
different bluff-shaped electronic components. The components have several different
sizes and geometries which make the usage of one characteristic length scale for the
heat transfer characterisation impossible. The complex configuration and the strong
concentrated power dissipation within the elements result in a high non-uniform
convective and radiative heat transfer. An example of a typical surface temperature
distribution of a powered printed circuit board, recorded with an infrared camera, is
given in Fig. 1.2. The image shows clearly regions with hot spots (the dark areas).

The convective heat transfer depends strongly on the flow field characteristics.
Hence, an accurate solution of the temperature field depends on the accuracy of
the flow solution. The flow in the devices is usually classified as a low Reynolds
number turbulent flow. While the Reynolds numbers remain low because of the small
velocities and length scales encountered, turbulence may be generated locally by the
protruding electronic components mounted on the printed circuit boards. Depending
on the local Reynolds number, turbulence may decay locally or may persist until
the next downstream protrusion where it may be enhanced. In electronic equipment
turbulence often remains isolated in small patches in the shear layers around the
protruding elements, while the flow above the protrusions remains more or less
laminar.

This outline of the problem highlights that both the heat transfer and the flow
structures on printed circuit boards are very complex. A reliable prognosis and
avoidance of hot spots and critical temperatures in a variety of complex printed
circuit boards can be achieved only by employing a technique which involves an
accurate evaluation of the local surface heat transfer and temperature on and around
the components.

Therefore, the well-known Nusselt-Reynolds number relations, which correlate
a Nusselt number (the dimensionless heat transfer coefficient h) with a Reynolds
number (a measure for the flow rate), are highly unsuited for the description of the
convective heat transfer from densely packed printed circuit boards. These relations
are usually applicable for a selective class of heat transfer problems in which only
one characteristic length is involved and for which the boundary conditions are well
defined. Further, the definition of the local reference temperature, encountered on
printed circuit boards and used in the definition of h, is not trivial.

Computational fluid dynamics (CFD) codes are more and more used as design
tools to predict the thermal processes encountered in electronic circuitry. CFD
codes can easily cope with a high level of complexity (component, board and system
level) which makes them attractive to use in the design process of printed circuit
boards. With large workstations, an entire electronic cabinet can be considered

=== [PDF page 17] ===
14 Chapter 1. Introduction to the problem

with many details. An accurate laminar solution for low-Reynolds number flows
can be obtained provided that the mesh is fine enough and that the solvers and
discretisations techniques are well chosen. But, as argued earlier, it is unlikely
that the fow remains laminar. Therefore, sophisticated turbulence models and/or
other solution techniques, like large eddy simulations (LES) and direct numerical
simulations (DNS), may be required to obtain accurate and reliable solutions for
the transitional and turbulent flows. A drawback for both LES and DNS is that the
computational capacity of currently available workstations is far from that needed
to accurately solve both the kinematic and the non-uniform heat transfer problems
as encountered in electronic devices.

A better prediction and understanding of the heat transfer and kinematic pro-
cesses encountered on printed circuit boards can be achieved by a balanced combi-
nation of both experimental and numerical effort. Although computational research
will soon or later be the dominant contribution in the design cycle of an electronic
apparatus, experimental research will, in essence, always be needed for the validation
of numerical simulations.

1.3 Goals of this study

The previous section highlighted the context in which the research was performed.
This thesis will be focussed on the experimental approach of the problem which
involves the accurate analysis of the local convective heat transfer in idealised models
of realistic electronic printed circuit boards. Some of the major goals of the research
are itemised as follows:

- The primary purpose of this experimental study is to investigate the physical
mechanisms which lead to the intensification or degradation of the local convective
heat transfer. This objective involves a thorough evaluation of the time-averaged
convective heat transfer and its associated flow structure. In some cases, additional
time-dependent convective heat flux measurements were performed to support the
interpretation of the results.

- A next objective was to derive quantifiable correlations between the distributions of
the local time-averaged heat transfer coefficient and the local flow field parameters
for idealised configurations of (multiple) wall-mounted protrusions in a channel
flow. These configurations serve as a diagnostic case for the cooling of realistic
electronic circuitry.

- A third major goal of the research was to provide accurate and reliable exper-
imental heat transfer and flow field data for benchmarking of commercial and
home-made CFD codes and, in particular, of turbulence models used for the ther-
mal design of printed circuit boards. The experimental data needed to be very
accurate to serve as a reference. This implies that significant effort had to be put
in a thorough accuracy analysis and in the validation of the obtained experimental
results.

=== [PDF page 18] ===
1.4. Implementation of approach 15

1.4 Implementation of approach

The investigation was primarily focussed on the flow structures and the turbulent
heat transfer in flows over surface-mounted obstacles in a turbulent channel flow.
The electronic components were modelled as a cube for the following reasons:

- A cube is the most basic three-dimensional geometry which involves most generic
flow phenomena encountered in real electronic devices: flow separation, recircula-
tion and reattachment. Yet the simplicity of the geometry and the fixed separation
lines (at the cube edges) make this flow suitable for experimental analysis. The
different types of flow will have a significant impact on the local heat transfer.
Although in real electronics applications the sizes of the protruding components
are somewhat different (more flat-pack like geometries) the physical phenomena
encountered in the cube case are more or less representative. For these reasons,
the cube is considered to be a suitable test model for an electronic component.

- Several researchers studied the cube-averaged heat transfer from configurations of
cubes (particularly isolated cubes or regular matrices of cubes) in a channel flow.
These data served for comparison and verification of the results obtained in this
research.

- The method to determine distributions of the time-averaged local convective heat
transfer from the obstacles required a sufficiently large obstacle. This determined,
among others, the size and the geometry of the adopted element (see the descrip-
tion of the experimental method, part I).

An arrangement of these cubes mounted on a wall is then representative for a printed
circuit board geometry. While an isolated wall-mounted cube can be considered as
the most basic configuration of such a printed circuit board, a configuration of
multiple cubes models a densely populated board and can be used to study the
interaction between components.

Four basic configurations were selected to investigate the local convective heat
transfer and the local flow structure. All four test-cases provided basic data but
each configuration had different and complementary features. Figure 1.3 shows

the side and top view of a typical configuration of cubes studied in this thesis.
The cube height is indicated with H, S, and S, denote the streamwise and the

=== [PDF page 19] ===
16 Chapter 1. Introduction to the problem

spanwise centre-to-centre distance between the cubes and D is the channel height.
The configurations discussed in this thesis are as follows:

- A single cube, placed in a developing turbulent channel flow was considered to
be the most basic configuration. For this test case, no thermal and aerodynamic
interactions with other heated obstacles were present: this allowed a very detailed
analysis of the flow structures and the local convective heat transfer. Further,
much research was already devoted to the flow and the vortex structure around a
single cube in either a boundary layer flow or a fully developed channel flow with
much higher Reynolds numbers. Additional data of the convective heat transfer
will, therefore, complement the existing knowledge. The flow field around the
single cube contains more or less the most basic features and this case is therefore
well suited to study the correlation between surface heat transfer and local flow
structure. Experiments were performed for different Reynolds numbers and the
effect of the upstream boundary condition was incorporated.

- The in-line row of cubes with a streamwise distance of S,/H = 2 and placed in
a developing channel flow was the second test case considered. The experiments
were performed with all cubes heated in order to investigate the so-called adiabatic
heat transfer. Tests were conducted for different Reynolds numbers. This is the
only test case for which mixed convection phenomena were noticed for the lowest
Reynolds number studied.

- A tandem arrangement of two cubes was a suitable model to investigate the aero-
dynamic and thermal interaction between two wall-mounted obstacles. Most of
the experimental work was performed for tandem arrangements placed in a fully
developed turbulent channel flow at the fixed Reynolds number of Rey = 3900.
Some additional measurements were carried out in a developing boundary layer
flow for higher Reynolds numbers. Flow field and heat transfer measurements were
performed for various streamwise and spanwise distances, S,/H and S,/H respec-
tively. The arrangements with S,/H = 0 are denoted as in-line arrangements,
those with S,/H different from 0 are denoted as off-axis tandem arrangements.
Additional measurements of the time-dependent convective heat transfer were per-
formed for the in-line tandem.

- The last studied test case is the in-line matrix of wall-mounted cubes with equal
streamwise and spanwise distances, i.e. S,/H = S,/H = 4. The total number of
allocated cubes (250 in total) caused a spatially periodic turbulent channel flow
far downstream in the matrix. The most important objective for this case was the
detailed and accurate description of the flow field and heat transfer characteristics
of a cube in the matrix sufficiently far downstream so that the flow could be
regarded as fully developed. This situation allowed the use of periodic inlet and
outlet flow conditions in the numerical computations. The dataset was used at two
ERCOFTAC workshops (June 6-7, 1997, Delft University of Technology, Delft [62],
and May 28-29, 1998, UMIST, Manchester [88]) for the validation of turbulence
models and numerical solution methods.

=== [PDF page 20] ===
1.5. Outline of thesis 17

These four configurations have been considered separately and the results will
be presented in separate chapters.

1.5 Outline of thesis

This thesis is organised in four clusters of chapters. Part I started with introduc-
tory words to define the problem and to address the objectives of the investigation
(chapter 1). A brief summary of the theoretical background is given in chapter 2.
We limit the discussion to the theory needed for the interpretation of the experi-
mental results. Further, a review of relevant literature is given in chapter 2 aimed
at indicating the current state of knowledge in this area. Part II is dedicated to the
description of the experiment. The test-configuration and the experimental meth-
ods to evaluate local heat transfer coefficients are described in chapter 3. Chapter 4
addresses the diagnostic measurement techniques to acquire convective heat transfer
and velocity data. This chapter covers, besides other used measurement techniques,
in fact two major techniques, namely infrared thermography and the Laser Doppler
Anemometry method. Part III of the thesis presents the results for the four test
cases investigated. The most basic case, a single cube in a turbulent channel flow,
is discussed in chapter 5. Chapter 6 presents the results for the in-line row of 9
cubes. The tandem of two cubes is discussed in chapter 7 and finally, results for
the spatially periodic matrix flow are given in chapter 8. The thesis is finished
with concluding remarks in part IV. A discussion of the major findings is given
in chapter 9 while conclusions and future directions are given in chapter 10. The
experimental evaluation of the thermal conductivity of the epoxy substrate, used in
the measurement cubes, is given in appendix A.

18

Chapter 1. Introduction to the problem

=== [PDF page 22] ===
CHAPTER 2

Theoretical background and literature review

In the past decades, much research was already devoted to the thermal management
of electronic systems since product reliability is an important strategy and sales argu-
ment. Most of this published material is pragmatic in nature and is mainly focussed
on the average convective heat transfer or the thermal resistance of representative
electronic components. Entire international conferences are dedicated to the prob-
lem of thermal management of electronic systems (e.g. Thermal Management of
Electronic Systems (Eurotherm), Semitherm and ITHERM (USA), etc.). The ap-
proach adapted in this thesis is different and is based on the local flow structure
and its relation to the local convective heat transfer for idealised models of printed
circuit boards. The chapter starts with a section on general considerations to briefly
outline some basic theoretical background information needed for the interpretation
of the flow and heat transfer results. Further, the flow over a single wall-mounted
cube is discussed to illustrate the generic flow types encountered in the test cases
under investigation. The discussion then proceeds with an overview of the most rel-
evant, literature in this area, which includes the flow and heat transfer characteristics
of single and multiple two-dimensional and three-dimensional objects.

2.1 General considerations

The general flow and energy equations are discussed first, followed by the concepts of
convective heat transfer. The statistical description of turbulence is an important
issue in this thesis since the observer, the experimenter in this case, deals with
continuous signals (velocity, temperature) which are acquired in discretized form.
The autocorrelation functions and spectra were mainly used to determine frequencies
of vortex shedding in the wake flow of the cubes.

2.1.1 Flow equations

When fluid is considered to be a continuum, the laws of mechanics and thermo-
mechanics can be applied to a control volume to derive the conservation laws of
mass, momentum and energy. In Euler’s approach, the control volume is fixed
relatively to the coordinate system while the fluid is passing through it. Only
Newtonian viscous fluids will be considered. The formulas given here were obtained
from the well-known literature [66, 166, 63, 52].

Conservation of mass describes the balance between the density change at a fixed
point and the change in the mass velocity vector pu. The difference of mass flow
into and out of the control volume must equal the rate of change in mass contained
in the volume (a repetition of the suffix in a term implies a summation over all three
values of the suffix):

2.1.2 Convective heat transfer

Convective heat transfer refers to heat exchange between a heated/cooled surface
and the fluid flowing over it. Traditionally, the heat transfer coefficient is defined
as [42, 83]:

Qeonw

where Qconv is the convective heat flow, A is the total area and 7,,,, and T,. f are
the averaged surface and reference temperatures, respectively. The temperature
difference (Tsur — Tre) is the driving force for the convective transport. The heat
transfer coefficient is usually non-dimensionalised with a characteristic length L and
the thermal conductivity of the fluid A, resulting in the Nusselt number:

Prep L

In some cases, a better insight is gained by the Stanton number St, defined as:

where p is the fluid density, c, is the specific heat and U;ey is a reference velocity.
A dimensionless parameter for the flow is the Reynolds number Re, which is

defined as: UL

where U,.f is a reference velocity, L is again the characteristic length and v is the

Convective heat transfer is directly related to air flow and, therefore, the Nusselt
number is a function of many properties and parameters. Nusselt numbers are
usually related to Reynolds numbers according to:

Nu= ARe? Pr" f(geometry), (2.18)

where Pr is the Prandt] number (Pr = v/a, where a is the thermal diffusivity of
the fluid), A is a constant, n and m are correlation coefficients and f(geometry) is a
geometry-dependent function. Such a correlation is doomed to fail when the situa-
tion is not represented by one characteristic length scale and one reference velocity.
Farther, such a correlation, expressed in the constant A and the coefficients n and
m, is dependent on the geometry and thermal and kinematic boundary conditions.
This means that congruence scaling rules need to be strictly applied as well as the
boundary conditions involved if such a correlation is used to estimate/predict the
heat transfer in a given flow situation. Nu-Re relations generally give the correct
order of magnitude of the convective heat transfer in simple flow/wall configura-
tions such as flat walls, etc. However, such correlations are usually over-simplistic
for complex geometries.

In this thesis it is, therefore, attempted to present the results in their basic
form, i.e. in terms of the local heat transfer coefficient for each particular geometry
without attempting to involve any similarity or scaling. In some cases, the heat
transfer and bulk velocity of the channel flow is scaled to enable a comparison with
literature date.

the same physical mechanism. The classical relation between the heat and momen-
tum transfer reads:

analogy are to be expected.

2.1.3 Statistical description of turbulence

In laboratory experiments, it is common practise to define a time average of the
function f(t):

= lim 3 J roa (2.21)

where T is the period in which the signal is recorded. This integral can be approx-

1

where At; is the time interval between two consecutive realisations. In reality, it
is impossible to take an infinite number of samples to obtain the time average.
There exist tabulated criteria to which the number N should satisfy in order to
neglect statistical error. Another often useful definition is the ensemble average
which averages the function over different individual events of the experiment each
time at a time ¢, after the run was started:

1 &
R y f (to). (2.23)

This average can be interpreted as repeating the experiment R times when the
values are recorded at time ¢,. The phase average is a special ensemble average. For
example, if vortex shedding is present in the flow, each time when a shed vortex
passes the monitor point, a sample is taken. Averaging over R samples then gives
the average at a certain phase of the shedding cycle. In this manner, it is possible to
detect coherent structures in flows provided that a time reference point is available.

It is assumed that the process becomes uncorrelated for large time delays:
f(t)f(i+r) + 0 if 7 > oo. This implies that the time scale T,, describes the period
over which the signal is still correlated with itself (in fact it is a measure for the
macroscopic time scale). If the signal is measured in the period T which is much
larger than the time scale T,, the ensemble average can be replaced by the time
average [166].

The skewness and kurtosis factors, S and K, respectively, are defined as the third
and fourth order moments normalised with the standard deviation: S = f3/ oF and
K = f"/ oF. The skewness indicates the symmetry or asymmetry of the histogram
while the kurtosis is a measure for the peakedness of the distribution. It can be
shown for Gaussian distributions that the skewness equals S = 0 and the kurtosis
k = 3. Turbulence is a non-linear phenomenon and, therefore, its statistics will not
resemble Gaussian behaviour. For example, strong negative peaks in the velocity
distributions will lead to strong negative values of the skewness factor.

2.2. Generic flow features in bluff-body flows: the cube 25

2.2 Generic flow features in bluff-body flows: the cube

The literature review of Schofield [145] showed that a consensus exists for the general
flow features around a single wall-mounted cube. The features include the horseshoe
vortex, the wake vortex and the top and the side vortices, which are indicated in the
three-dimensional ’artist’s impression’ in Fig. 2.1. These flow features are not only
observed for a single cube in turbulent channel flows, but are also representative for
configurations of multiple cubes. In a time-averaged sense, the oncoming main flow
is separated at the sharp leading side and top edges of the cubes. The separated
side shear layer caused a high vorticity recirculation at the side face close to the
leading edge with its origin at the channel floor. This side vortex is indicated with
A in Fig. 2.1. The separation of the top shear layer resulted in a bound recirculation
(‘top vortex’, indicated with B) located at the top face and oriented perpendicularly
to the direction of the main flow. The flow field in front of the cube is characterised
by the horseshoe vortex, indicated with C, which originates at the base of the
windward stagnation region and extends downstream along the channel floor past
the sides of the cube. An arc-shaped vortex was typically found in the wake of the
obstacle (‘wake vortex’). This wake vortex, indicated with D, was confined by the
solid walls and the fluid flowing along the side and top faces. The instantaneous

FIGURE 2.1: ’Artist’s impression’ of the flow structure around the single cube in a turbulent
channel flow.

flow field differs significantly from the time-averaged field as sketched in Fig. 2.1.
Vortex shedding at the side faces of the cube leads to asymmetric instantaneous flow
patterns (only in a time-averaged sense one can speak of a symmetric flow pattern).
The shear layer which separates at the leading side edge of the obstacle is subject to
Kelvin-Helmholtz instabilities [66]. These instabilities are amplified and force the

|

=== [PDF page 29] ===
26 Chapter 2. Theoretical background and literature review

flow to alternate between two preferable energy states. In one state, the angle of
attack of the main flow is more directed towards the left side of the obstacle, causing
a vortex to be shed from the right trailing side edge. In the other state, a vortex is
shed at the opposite trailing side. The alternation between both states results in a
street of vortices, the so called Von Karman vortex street, which interact with each
other. The dynamics of vortex shedding is a well documented phenomenon which
occurs behind the side faces of bluff bodies [66, 174, 58).

2.3 Flow field around wall-mounted rectangular protrusions

Hunt and co-workers [68] gave a detailed description of the application of topology
to oil-film visualisations to deduce the flow field around single wall-mounted pris-
matic obstacles. They provided details of the kinematic and topological principles
necessary to infer the flow field from the critical points (nodes and saddles) iden-
tified on the surfaces which bound the flow. Dependent on the number of saddles
and nodal points found, they derived a topologically consistent flow field which con-
tained additional fine details besides the structures given in Fig. 2.1. For example,
it was deduced that the primary horseshoe vortex is accompanied by an additional
system of smaller counter-rotating vortices upstream of the primary one.

Martinuzzi and co-workers [101, 102] applied the topological concepts to doc-
ument the mean flow structure around surface-mounted blocks with square cross-
sections placed in a fully developed, turbulent channel flow at a Reynolds number of
Rey = 40000. The Reynolds number was based on the bulk velocity and the obsta-
cle height. The channel-height-to-cube-height ratio was 2. The purpose of the study
was to highlight the differences between nominally and fully three-dimensional flows.
The results showed that two distinct flow regimes existed which depended on the
obstacle span-to-height ratio. For the larger ratios, the tall obstacles, the mid region
was nominally two-dimensional but the alternating saddle and nodal points in front
of the obstacle suggested that three-dimensional cellular flow structures caused the
transport of fluid over the top of the obstacle. For the smaller ratios, including the
single cube, it was found that the flow upstream of the obstacle alternates between
two energetic states. In one state, a high-inertia jet of fluid is directed upstream
against the pressure gradient. In the other state, this jet rolls up to form a system of
four vortices, making the horseshoe vortex system. This hypothesis was supported
with laser-light sheet experiments and LDA-velocity measurements [86, 101]. Due
to the alternation between the two states, the velocity distributions had bimodal
shapes. Despite the vigourous variation of the velocity field, it was found that the
alternation did not involve periodicity.

Such a bimodal behaviour was also observed in the horseshoe vortex structure up-
stream of a wing-body junction by Devenport and Simpson [38]. They observed that
the time-averaged flow patterns obtained using oil-film visualisations differed from
those observed from velocity measurements. These inconsistencies were attributed
to the dynamic, bimodal behaviour of the flow: in a certain region upstream of the
wing-body junction, the probability density functions of the streamwise and normal
velocity component became significantly bimodal. In an attempt to elucidate the

=== [PDF page 30] ===
2.3. Flow field around wall-mounted rectangular protrusions 27

mechanisms involved, the histograms were decomposed in two major peaks each cor-
responding to a state. From this decomposed histograms, the time-averaged velocity
was calculated for each state. Such a conditional decomposition and analysis of two
main velocities was followed by Martinuzzi and co-workers [86, 101]. Time-averaged
vector plots obtained in this manner clearly displayed the two different flow states.

Martinuzzi et al. [101, 103, 104, 69] investigated the flow over a cube in a fully
developed channel flow using Laser Doppler Anemometry (Rey = 40000, channel-
height-to-cube-height ratio was 2). The rotation of the setup allowed the measure-
ment of all three velocity components. Long-term averaged velocities and higher
order moments, like the variance, skewness and kurtosis were directly measured.
The instantaneous measurement of pairs of the velocity components u and v and
pairs of u and w allowed the measurement of correlations, or the Reynolds stresses,
between them. The detailed measurements were complemented with oil-film and
smoke visualisations. They documented the three-dimensional flow structure around
the cube in great detail which displays all features given in Fig. 2.1. Further, they
also provided information about the flow dynamics in front of the cube, as well as
in the wake of the obstacle. A comparison with these results will be made in this
thesis in the paragraphs in question.

The flow over a surface-mounted cube in a uniform flow and in a boundary layer
flow was experimentally studied by Castro and Robins [30]. For both cases, the
cube was placed on a flat plate in an open-circuit wind tunnel. The cube size was
60 mm. For the uniform oncoming flow condition, the cube was placed 20 mm
from the leading edge, giving a boundary layer thickness of less than 1.5 mm which
is significantly smaller than the cube height. In the boundary layer type of flow,
the boundary layer thickness was 10 times the cube size. Hot-wire anemometry
was used to measure profiles of the mean velocities and turbulence intensities. The
results showed that shear layer reattachment depends on the relative thickness of the
oncoming boundary layer: shear layer reattachment was observed for the boundary
layer flow, contrary to the cube in the uniform oncoming flow. Further, the extent of
the wake recirculation appeared to be also a strong function of the relative boundary
layer thickness of the oncoming flow.

The flow around wall-mounted vertical cylinders and wing-body junctions (a
tapered, streamlined cylinder) is also characterised by a horseshoe vortex system,
induced at front of the cylinder and deflected along the sides [13, 136, 48, 175, 178,
176]. In addition to the flow field analysis, Fisher and Eibeck [48] studied the effect
of the horseshoe vortex on the surface heat transfer from the base plate downstream
of the obstacle. The Reynolds numbers considered were in the range 77,000 < Rep
< 130,000. The Reynolds number was based on the cylinder width and the bulk
velocity. The results indicated that the heat transfer was augmented up to 50% at
the centreline of the vortex extensions.

The flow structure around multiple-obstacle configurations in a channel flow
received little attention in the literature. Okamoto et al. [126] studied the flow field
in a matrix of surface-mounted square blocks of width D = 23 mm and height H
= 5 mm placed in a boundary layer flow. The face-to-face distance S (in both the
streamwise and the spanwise direction) was varied as S/H = 2, 3, 5, 7, 10 and 13 to

=== [PDF page 31] ===
28 Chapter 2. Theoretical background and literature review

investigate the different flow regimes as imposed by the different distances between
the elements. The Reynolds number was constant for all configurations studied, Rey
= U,,H/v = 990, where U,. denotes the freestream velocity. They used a two-colour
Laser Doppler Anemometer to measure the time-mean velocities and turbulence
intensities around the protruding obstacles. The study showed that for S/H <5
the from the obstacle top edge separated flow did not reattach at the channel floor
in the wake of the obstacle but the inter-obstacle space was fully covered by a large
flow recirculation. Reattachment at the channel floor of the separated shear layer
was detected for S/H > 7. Further, the configuration of blocks with spacing S/H
= 7 had maximum turbulence intensities around the blocks.

The fully developed matrix flow was one of the test cases of the ERCOFTAC
workshop held at the Delft University of Technology, June 6-7, 1997 [62] and at
UMIST, Manchester, May 28-29, 1998 [88]). One of the reasons for its selection as
benchmark flow was the flow periodicity, which eliminates uncertainties associated
with the proper definition of the inlet and outlet boundary conditions. Further, new
data of both the flow structure and the convective heat transfer were available (the
results of chapter 8). In total 9 contributions were submitted, including steady-state
RANS (Reynolds Averaged Navier Stokes) computations with different turbulence
models and quasi-DNS simulations. Only one contribution involved the local con-
vective heat transfer (Bornoff and Parry [21]). They performed simulations with
the commercial CFD code Flotherm [49, 50] and supplied only the face averaged
and cube-averaged convective heat fluxes. The results were in reasonable agreement
with the experimental data although they did not correctly predict the flow struc-
ture around the cubes. The quasi-DNS data, supplied by Verstappen and Veldman
[170, 171], were performed for two different grids. The DNS-method was based
on a high-order, spectro-consistent discretisation method. The results, including
profiles of the mean velocity and turbulent fluctuations, were in good agreement
with the experimental data, particularly for the finer grid (100° grid points). The
turbulence models (RANS) predicted the mean velocities reasonably well while the
calculated Reynolds stresses deviated significantly from the experimental data. Fur-
ther, Mathey et al. [106] performed large eddy simulations (LES). The calculated
mean velocities and second order moments were also in very good agreement with
the experiments.

2.4 Vortex shedding past rectangular obstacles

Much experimental work was already devoted to the vortex shedding past square
or rectangular cross-sectioned cylinders suspended in a uniform flow. These two-
dimensional configurations display the mechanisms of vortex shedding well and can
serve as reference cases for studies of separated flows.

Okajima [125] found for rectangular two-dimensional cylinders suspended in a
uniform flow that the shedding frequency, presented as the dimensionless Strouhal
number St, is a strong function of the width-to-height ratio (B/H) in the Reynolds
number range of 70 < Rey < 2 x 10*. For the larger ratios, B/H > 1, a second
peak appeared in the power density spectrum, caused by motion induced excitations

=== [PDF page 32] ===
2.4. Vortex shedding past rectangular obstacles 29

(MIE, which are the eigen-vibrations of systems exposed to fluid flow). For the
square cylinder, only Von Karman type of vortex shedding was observed and St
varied between 0.10 and 0.15 for 70 < Rey < 2 x 101.

Shirashi and Matsumoto [153] presented a fundamental study of the mechanisms
responsible for the vortex-induced oscillations of two-dimensional structures. They
found that three different kinds of vortices are responsible for the vortex induced
oscillations of bluff sections which are: separated vortices from the leading edge,
secondary vortices at and separated vortices from the trailing edge.

The effect of the variation of the distance between a solid wall and a square
cylinder on the flow around the cylinder was investigated by Martinuzzi and Wu
[177, 105]. The results showed that the vortex shedding frequency increases sharply
for gaps less than one obstacle height H. This behaviour is accompanied by the
appearance of local wall flow recirculations and a decrease in the obstacle drag
coefficient. It was noted that no vortex shedding activity could be detected for
separations of less than 0.3H.

Lyn and co-workers [94, 95] studied the vortex shedding past a square cylinder
suspended in a uniform flow which had a turbulence intensity of 2% and a Reynolds
number of Rep = Uj,D/v = 22,000. The Strouhal number found was St = fD/[Vin
=0.132 + 0.004. In a time-averaged sense, the streamlines were symmetric and
showed two strong recirculations in the wake of the cylinder. A méthod was devel-
oped to perform a phase averaging in order to reveal the coherent structures in the
wake of the cylinder. An ordinary LDA system was used to measure the velocity
field, yielding consecutive velocity data at different stations. This means that the
definition of a fixed reference point in time was necessary to synchronise the mea-
surements. This was done by a pressure transducer at one of the side faces of the
square cylinder. The measured pressure revealed also a sinusoidal periodicity. By
triggering the Laser Doppler system at different time delays in the reference signals,
the phase averaging was performed at different phases which led to the detection of
large coherent structures in the wake flow.

In an additional study by Lee [89] and Vickery [173], the vortex shedding is
discussed in terms of the surface pressure field and drag/lift forces on the cylinder.
They did not give values for the dimensionless frequencies.

Balachandar et al. [14] considered the wake recirculation in separated flows of
two-dimensional geometries with circular, elliptical and square cross-sections and
normal plates. For the Reynolds number range considered, they observed that a
strong interaction existed between both separated shear layers for all cases, which
resulted in Von Karman vortex streets. They found that the contribution of the
Reynolds normal stresses to the force balance of the wake vortex was significant.
Further, they made a conditional decomposition to separate the incoherent contri-
butions from the coherent or organised structures.

For the freely suspended two-dimensional square prism, the consensus of exper-
imental data yields a Strouhal number of St = 0.133, based on the oncoming free
stream velocity and the width of the square cylinder.

Vortex shedding from the lateral faces of a wall-mounted cube is less pronounced
due to the suppression by the base channel wall underneath it. Directly behind the

=== [PDF page 33] ===
30 Chapter 2. Theoretical background and literature review

obstacle, the organised vortices are rather strong and most of the kinetic energy is
stored at the largest scales (the energy is concentrated around the vortex shedding
frequency). Further downstream, about 1-2H distances, the large scale structures
have more or less decayed, among others due to the viscous interaction with the
channel wall, and the energy associated with these coherent structures cascades to
the higher frequencies (smaller scales). This results in more intense turbulence in the
far obstacle wake. When the oncoming flow is already turbulent, similar shedding
phenomena occur but the coherent structures are not very distinct: instead of a
periodic alternation, the vortex shedding occurs in a quasi-periodic manner.

Experimental investigation of the vortex shedding from single wall-mounted rect-
angular obstacles was reported by several authors. Martinuzzi et al. {101, 102]
presented Strouhal numbers (St = fH/ug, where ug is the bulk velocity, f is the
predominant frequency and H is the obstacle size) for bluff-bodies of square cross-
section with different span-width-to-height ratio’s (W/H) in a fully developed chan-
nel flow. The channel-height-to-obstacle-height ratio (h/H) was 2. The Reynolds
number Rey, based on the bulk velocity and the obstacle height, was consider-
ably higher than those considered in this study, namely Rey = 40.000. It was
observed that the dominant frequency associated with vortex shedding decreased
with increasing span. They measured a Strouhal number of St = 0.145 for a single
wall-mounted cube. For the larger aspect ratios, (W/H > 1), a second peak in the
power density spectra was also observed which corresponded to the MIE type of
oscillations. It was observed that the dominant frequency associated with vortex
shedding decreased with increasing span of the obstacle.

Shah and Ferziger [151] presented results of the time-dependent velocity field
with use of large eddy simulations (LES). The time-averaged flow field, resolved from
the time-dependent solutions, showed the characteristic vortices usually found in the
proximity of an isolated cube (i.e. horseshoe, top, side and wake vortices). These
time-averaged results were in good agreement with those measured by Martinuzzi
et al. [101, 102]. But the instantaneous flow field was very different from the
time-averaged one. For example, the arch-shaped wake vortex did not exist in
an instantaneous sense. Although instantaneous vortices were observed, they were
asymmetric on the sides of the cube.

The aerodynamic interaction between two rectangular cylinders of infinite length
in a tandem arrangement was studied by Takeuchi [163] and Takeuchi and Mat-
sumoto [164, 165]. The results showed that vortex shedding and the motion induced
excitations of the obstacles (MIE, which are obstacle excitations induced by flow in-
stabilities) both depend on the ratio S,/H, where S, is the in-line centre-to-centre
distance and H is the obstacle width.

Devarakonda and Humphrey [37] experimentally studied the turbulent flow in
the wake of a tandem arrangement of two square cylinders of infinite length using
Laser Doppler Anemometry at a Reynolds number of 10,000, based on the averaged
inlet velocity and the size of the downstream prism (D). They varied parameters
such as the streamwise centre-to-centre distance between the cylinders (\ = S,/D)
and the cylinder-height ratio (6 = d/D = 0.25, 0.5 and 1.0; d is the width of the
leading cylinder). Strouhal numbers were measured behind the second prism. A

=== [PDF page 34] ===
2.5. Time-averaged convective heat transfer from rectangular objects 31

maximum in the Strouhal number was found between 6 = 0.25 and 1.0 for 4 = 1.0.
For 6 = 1.0, a minimum in the Strouhal number occurred between \ = 1.0 and
4.0. They also postulated that the leading upstream cylinder may drive the second
cylinder although the presented data contained insufficient detail to support this
hypothesis.

The investigation of Luo and Teng [93] of the aerodynamic forces acting on the
downstream square cylinder of an in-line tandem arrangement showed the existence
of a critical spacing of S/H = 3 (5 is the centre-to-centre distance, H is the width
of the cylinder). The Strouhal number was minimum for this distance. For smaller
distances, the shear layer which separated from the leading cylinder reattached on
the second cylinder and vortex shedding was detected only downstream of the second
cylinder. For larger spacings, vortex shedding was detected behind both cylinders.

Similar results were reported by Oka et al. [124] and Ishigai et al. [79] for two
circular cylinders, of diameter D, in an in-line tandem arrangement as discussed in
the review of Zdravkovich [179].

de Souza et al. [156] experimentally studied the vortex structure in the near
wake of a circular cylinder suspended in a turbulent boundary layer flow. They
used Hot-wire anemometry to measure velocity time series to which three different
identification techniques were applied to identify coherent structures in the flow.
Two methods were based on the filtering of the instantaneous velocity data with
a spectral and a time-frequency (wavelet) decomposition while the third technique
made use of the relation between sudden variations in the velocity and large-scale
structures in the flow.

2.5 Time-averaged convective heat transfer from rectangu-
lar objects

2.5.1 Local convective heat transfer

The effect of flow separation, recirculation and reattachment on the time-averaged
heat transfer was studied in the past for mainly two-dimensional configurations.
Less attention was given to the local convective heat transfer for three-dimensional
cases because of the difficulty of measuring local heat fluxes in such a situation.

Two-dimensional situations

Several studies in the accessible literature were focussed on distributions of the local
heat transfer coefficient for two-dimensional configurations. Igarashi and Yamasaki
[76] studied the local convective heat transfer from a wall-mounted rectangular rib
of 10 and 20 mm height and 30 mm depth placed transversal in a turbulent bound-
ary layer flow at velocities of the undisturbed flow between 4 and 18 m/s. They
determined the local convective heat transfer coefficient (h) from the uniform heat
flux, provided by an electrically powered stainless steel sheet, and surface temper-
atures measured with thermocouples buried in the material. A large variation in
the local heat transfer coefficient was observed, in particular at the top face of the
rectangular rib, which was attributed to flow separation and reattachment.

=== [PDF page 35] ===
32 Chapter 2. Theoretical background and literature review

The convective heat transfer from an array of multiple wall-mounted ribs in a
fully developed channel flow was reported by Aliaga et al. [5, 6]. They determined
the local heat transfer coefficients from.a uniform heat flux, generated by an electri-
cally powered metal foil, and the surface temperature distribution, measured with
an infrared camera. The Reynolds numbers considered, based on the centreline ve-
locity and the hydraulic diameter of the channel, ranged between 0.5 x 10° and 6
x 10°. Results for a similar configuration were recently reported by Lorenz et al.
[91]. They also used a heated foil and infrared thermography to determine heat
transfer coefficients. The Reynolds numbers in the latter study, also based on the
hydraulic diameter and further on the mean velocity, ranged from 104 to 105. Both
studies illustrated the difficulties in determining accurately the local heat transfer,
and indicated possible causes of temperature inaccuracy when infrared thermogra-
phy is used for the measurement of surface temperature distributions. From these
studies it also appeared that, as expected, flow separation and reattachment dictate
the local convective heat transfer phenomena to a great extent.

In other work, Igarashi [74, 75] reported on studies of the local convective heat
transfer from a vertical rectangular prism placed in a uniform flow with free stream
velocities ranging from 6 to 20 m/s. The investigation included variation of the
width-to-height ratio and the flow Reynolds number. Again, a substantial variation
in the convective heat transfer was found. The local wall heat transfer from ribbed
surfaces in stationary and rotating ducts was also studied by Iacovidis et al. (31, 70,
71, 72, 73]. Ribbed ducts are usually applied in cooling passages for hot-temperature
gas turbine blades. The results showed also strong variation in the local convective
heat transfer.

Other examples include the heat transfer downstream of a step in the surface
of a plate [149], the heat transfer in the recirculation region behind a double step
[47] and the heat transfer in the recirculation region on a blunt flat plate [131]. The
studies showed also that for these two-dimensional geometries flow impingement and
separation had a significant impact on the local heat transfer coefficient. Regions
of flow separation were typically characterised by low heat transfer coefficients,
while regions were the flow reattached exposed maxima in the local heat transfer
coefficient.

Three-dimensional objects

To our knowledge, no results have been published on the direct measurement of
the distributions of the local convective heat transfer for all faces of a single cube
which are exposed to convective cooling. One of the reasons for this deficiency is
the difficulty of measuring local convective heat fluxes for three-dimensional objects.
A possible way to measure local heat fluxes, besides the method developed in the
work described in this thesis, is by using thin metal foils. The latter method, already
mentioned in the previous text, is based on the electrical power dissipation due to
the Ohmic resistance of the thin metal foil. The resistance is directly proportional to
the inverse of the thickness: R = ppl/A [0], where pz [2 m] is the specific resistance
of the metal used, / is the length of the sheet and A is the cross-section area of the

=== [PDF page 36] ===
2.5. Time-averaged convective heat transfer from rectangular objects 33

sheet. When the thickness is uniform and the electrical field is directed in parallel
through the sheet, a uniform dissipation results which leads to a uniform convective
heat flux when the surface is convectively cooled. The complicated shape of cubical
obstacles prevents appropriate wiring of the foils and special care should be taken
at the corners of the obstacles. It is much easier to equip a two-dimensional obstacle
with such a metal sheet.

The constant heat flux method was used by Olsen et al. (127] to measure heat
transfer coefficients at two faces of a heated cube. The cubical geometry complicated
the coverage of the entire cube surface with these metal foils. The heat transfer
was also strongly non-uniform in their case, in particular at the side face of the
cube where the side vortex, enveloping the entire side face, was responsible for a
monotonic increase in the heat transfer coefficient in the streamwise direction.

A different approach was followed by Natarajan and Chyu [123, 33] who in-
vestigated the local convective heat transfer from a single wall-mounted cube in a
boundary layer flow with the naphthalene sublimation method which is based on a
mass-heat transfer analogy. They placed a cube in a boundary layer flow for which
the boundary layer thickness 5 was about 1/4 of the cube height H. The Reynolds
numbers, based on the cube height and the free stream velocity, ranged between
3.1 x 10* and 1.1 x 10°. They observed significant differences in the mass transfer
rates for the different cube faces.

Recently, Chyu and Natarajan [34] performed a comparative study of the local
mass transfer characteristics on the base surface of three-dimensional basic geome-
tries (cylinder, cube, diamond, pyramid and hemisphere) placed in a boundary layer
flow at a Reynolds number of 17,000 (the Reynolds number was based on the object
height and the freestream velocity). The results showed a mass transfer enhance-
ment especially in vicinity of the horseshoe vortex and wake vortices downstream of
the protrusions.

2.5.2 Averaged convective heat transfer
Single objects

Much more research was devoted to the element-averaged convective heat transfer
from isothermal obstacles. A study of the average convective heat transfer for an
isolated single protrusion in channel flow was performed by Roeller et al. [139, 140]
for Reynolds numbers between 500 and 10000. The Reynolds number was based on
the channel height. The study included variation of obstacle dimensions, geometry
and blockage ratio. The results showed that the average heat transfer coefficient
increased with increasing blockage ratio mainly due to local flow acceleration.
Nakayama [121] studied the conjugate (conjugate refers to the interaction be-
tween the three modes of heat transfer: convection, conduction and radiation) heat
transfer from a wall-mounted block in a channel flow both experimentally and nu-
merically for Reynolds numbers between 2200 to 16000 for two different situations:
a heated copper block on an adiabatic wall and an adiabatic block on a heated wall.
They found that the conjugate effects were strong and that the local convective heat
transfer from the channel wall was strongly related to the three-dimensional flow

=== [PDF page 37] ===
34 Chapter 2. Theoretical background and literature review

field around the protrusion.

Morris and Garimella [116] studied the thermal wake downstream of a three-
dimensional obstacle in a water tunnel flow for uniform inlet conditions and Reynolds
numbers between 500-1000 (laminar regime) and 10000-25000 (turbulent regime).
Long-term mean flow patterns were deduced from flow visualisation experiments.
It was shown, as expected, that buoyancy affected the thermal wake structure only
for the lower Reynolds number cases.

Multiple objects

Moffat and Anderson [7, 8, 9, 10, 113] dealt with a matrix of aluminium cubes and
flat packs mounted on an insulating channel wall. The concept of the adiabatic heat
transfer coefficient was successfully applied aimed at eliminating the influence of
the upstream thermal boundary conditions. Different parameters were varied: the
channel height with respect to the obstacle height, the obstacle sizes (cubes and flat
packs), the spanwise and the streamwise separation between the components, and
the Reynolds number. The studies provided much insight in the convective heat
transfer for these kind of multiple-element configurations. It was shown that a fully
developed heat transfer regimes was obtained after four or five rows of cubes.

Garimella et al. [117, 53, 54, 55] also focussed on the average convective heat
transfer from isothermal copper cuboids on a flat plate in a water channel. Their
studies included variation of different geometric parameters (e.g. the channel height,
the streamwise and spanwise separation between the obstacles, in-line and staggered
arrangements) and the flow Reynolds number. The range of Reynolds numbers con-
sidered covered laminar, transitional and turbulent channel flows. They observed
that the convective heat transfer is significantly dependent on the streamwise sepa-
ration, the channel height and the Reynolds number.

2.6 Time-dependent convective heat transfer

The vortex shedding from obstacles suspended in a cross-flow or from wall-mounted
protrusions causes large-scale coherent structures in the wake flow of the obstacle in
question. These periodic oscillations affect not only the surface heat transfer from
the objects itself or, in the case of wall-mounted protrusions, from the base plate on
which the obstacle is mounted, but it will also affect the turbulent heat flux in the
boundary layer flow.

The coupling between the surface heat transfer from circular cylinders in cross-
flow and the vortex shedding has been experimentally investigated by Scholten and
Murray [146, 147, 148] for both low and high freestream turbulence flows. Time-
dependent heat fluxes were measured around the circumference of the cylinder with a
hot film sensor; velocities were measured with a Laser Doppler Anemometry system.
Due to the strong aerodynamic coupling with the pressure field, the heat flux at the
front of the cylinder varied periodically with the frequency of vortex shedding.

Wroblewski et al. [175, 176, 178] studied the effect of flow instabilities, caused by
vortex shedding, on the turbulent heat flux in the wake of wall-mounted cylinders

=== [PDF page 38] ===
2.6. Time-dependent convective heat transfer 35

and wing-body junctions. Wroblewski and Eibeck [175] performed simultaneous
measurements of the fluid velocity and temperatures at different stations in the
wake of the streamlined cylinders. The Reynolds numbers considered were Rep
= 24,700, 36,000 and 970,000, where Rep is based on the root diameter of the
cylinder. The boundary layer in the wake of the obstacle was heated by means of
a uniform heat flux foil, fully covering the base on which the cylinder was mounted
(the cylinder was insulated from the base plate in order to prevent its heating).
Cross-spectra of the fluctuating velocity and temperature, u’6’, showed peaks at
frequencies associated with vortex shedding for the measurements in the middle-
wake region. This suggested that the turbulent heat transfer in the boundary layer
flow was augmented by vortex shedding. Spectra of the turbulent heat flux in the
inner-wake region showed maxima at a much broader frequency range. Since the flow
periodicities were dominantly present in the spanwise direction, the turbulent heat
fluxes showed anisotropies. While the turbulent heat flux in the flow was enhanced
by vortex shedding, an effect on the total surface heat transfer was not observed.
They argued that the turbulence mixing in the wake due to flow instabilities and
noted increase in the eddy diffusivity was the dominant mechanism for heat transfer
enhancement in the boundary layer flow.

The effect of large-scale periodic unsteadiness in the wake of a streamlined cylin-
der, attributable to vortex shedding, on the heat transfer in the boundary layer was
studied by Wroblewski [176]. He used a triple-wire to measure simultaneously the
temperature and the velocity in the wake. By using a triple decomposition and
conditional sampling technique, the heat fluxes associated with the coherent struc-
tures in the wake flow were separated from the turbulent heat fluxes. The results
showed that up to 50 % of the spanwise heat flux was attributed to the coherent
contribution, while this was about 30 % for the vertical component of the heat flux.

Xie and Wroblewski [178] studied the effect of large-scale periodicities on the
surface heat transfer from the base plate in the wake of a circular cylinder. They
measured the time-resolved wall heat flux and turbulent heat fluxes in the boundary
layer. In addition to the measurement techniques described in [176], they used a
thin film sensor to measure the time-dependent wall heat flux. They found that the
surface heat flux in the wake of the cylinder was significantly increased by the large-
scale coherent structures in the flow (vortex shedding). This effect was largest in the
unstable region, directly downstream of the vortex streets. They postulated that
the thickening and the thinning of the near-wall thermal boundary layer, induced
by the flow periodicity, caused the augmentation of the surface heat transfer. The
strong correlation between the ensemble-averaged velocity, temperature, and wall
heat flux confirmed this hypothesis. The fluctuations in the convective heat fluxes
were up to 25 %.

Swisher et al. [158] used a fast-response heat flux sensor, similar to the one de-
scribed in Hager et al. [59] and Simmons et al. [155], to measure the time-dependent
heat flux in the horseshoe vortex structure in front of a wing-body junction at its
centreline. Velocities were acquired simultaneously. A strong correlation was de-
tected between the heat flux and the velocity, particularly at the location where the
flow separates from the wall upstream from the main horseshoe vortex.

=== [PDF page 39] ===
36 Chapter 2. Theoretical background and literature review

Suzuki et al. {82, 160, 161, 162] studied the surface heat transfer in a bound-
ary layer flow disturbed by an inserted cylinder. The cylinder was placed normal
to the flow direction. They reported on a dissimilarity between the surface heat
transfer and the friction factor. At locations where the latter became minimum,
a significant enhancement in the local surface heat transfer was found. This is in
contradiction with the similarity rule (Reynolds’ analogy) which prescribes a pro-
portionality between the heat transfer coefficient and the wall shear stress. The
argued that the dissimilarity was directly related to the vortex shedding from the
suspended cylinder. Entrainment of cold air occurred at the location where the vor-
tex was directed downward to the surface while hot fluid was ejected from the wall
where the flow separated. Temperature effects associated with this so-called washing
action caused the mentioned dissimilarity. Their hypothesis was recently supported
by time-dependent numerical simulations [77] which showed a dissimilarity between
the instantaneous wall friction and the surface heat transfer.

Part IT

Description of the experiment

37

=== [PDF page 41] ===
CHAPTER 3

Experimental set-up and measurement methods

The test configuration in which the experimental work was performed is described in
this chapter. A closed wind-tunnel was constructed in a way that the channel section
in which all the measurement were performed had well-defined two-dimensional
flow conditions. The configurations of cubes were mounted on one of the channel
walls. Two methods were explored to measure the convective heat transfer from
the cubes. A time-averaged method was developed to evaluate distributions of the
local heat transfer coefficient from the surface temperature distributions. For this
method, an advanced measurement assembly was designed. In addition to the time-
averaged method, a transient heat flux technique was developed to measure the
time-dependent convective heat transfer from the cubes. The method used a sensor
of finite size and had, therefore, a limited spatial resolution. Both methods are
evaluated with an accuracy analysis to support the reliability of the convective heat
transfer measurements.

3.1 Test configuration

The experimental work was conducted in a blowing-type low-Reynolds number
closed wind tunnel. The flow was recirculated by means of two fans. Two heat
exchangers were used to thermostat the air to a constant temperature. Honeycombs
and fine grids were used to eliminate the large turbulence scales and to straighten
the flow. Approximately 2 m upstream of the test-section, the channel contracted
to a rectangular channel of height D = 51 mm and of 60 cm width. The sufficiently
large width-to-height ratio (12) ensured a two-dimensional flow at the centre of the
test-section. A sketch of the test-section with its global right-turning Cartesian co-
ordinate system are given in Fig. 3.1. The z- and y-axis refer to the streamwise
and the normal direction. The z-axis points out of the image. A photograph of
the test-section with the matrix of wall-mounted cubes is shown in Fig. 3.2. The
laser probe in front of the test-section was used to measure the flow field around
the cubes. Cubes of size H = 15 mm were mounted on the lower channel wall, from
now on referred to as channel floor. This gives a channel height-to-cube-height ratio
of D/H = 3.4. The local coordinate system is chosen in the same way as the global
coordinate system, but the local origin is now located at the front centreline of each
cube (see Fig. 3.3 for a sketch of the cube with its local coordinate system). The
test-section was oriented vertically to allow the measurement equipment to access
the measurement area around the cubes. This will be discussed in more detail later
in this section. Gravity forces acted in the vertical (spanwise, z) direction. For
most of the experiments performed, the Reynolds number was sufficiently large to
neglect buoyancy effects. For situations where the induced secondary flow was not
negligible, this will be explicitly mentioned in the text.

to the large width-to-height ratio. Tripping strips in the test-section entrance were

experimentally (from measurements) that the ‘zig-zag’ strip, commonly applied on
aerofoils to stimulate the transition to a turbulent boundary layer flow, was the most
suitable strip for our application. For most of the experiments performed, the strip
was located at the entrance of the parallel test-section at both the channel roof and
floor. This resulted in a fully developed channel flow in the measurement area at’z
= 0, located 2 meter downstream from the entrance. For some measurements, the

the cube. z, y and z refer to the streamwise, cube, consisting of the core of copper with the
normal and spanwise direction, respectively. epoxy layer around it.

The cubes were mounted on a base plate of formaldehyde. The thermal con-
ductivity of this insulator was measured with a steady state ‘sandwich’ technique,
described in Appendix A. The results showed a thermal conductivity of A = 0.33
W/mK. The experimental uncertainty, estimated from a single sample uncertainty
analysis and incorporating heat losses, was about 10%. The low thermal conduc-
tivity of the base plate prevented excessive conductive heat losses from the heated
cubes through this plate.

The experimental work discussed in this thesis was performed for four different
configurations of cubes which were already mentioned in the introduction: the single
cube, the in-line row of cubes with a streamwise distance of S/H = 2, the tandem-
configuration of two cubes for various values of S,/H and S,/H and the regular
matrix of cubes with S,/H = S,/H = 4.

Most of the results described in this thesis were based on the data acquired
with two experimental techniques. Infrared thermography was used to measure the

=== [PDF page 44] ===
42 Chapter 3. Experimental set-up and measurement methods

surface temperature distributions of the cubes which served as a basis for the de-
termination of the distributions of the local heat transfer coefficient. Laser Doppler
Anemometry was used to document the flow structure quantitatively. Both tech-
niques will be thoroughly discussed in the chapter on experimental techniques. Since
both techniques are based on optical principles, a suitable window was needed for
the measurement in question. The channel roof (the front wall) was, therefore, made
of glass in case of the LDA measurements. For the heat transfer experiments, the
channel roof was replaced by a special window (foil). Its transmittance character-
istics were compatible with those of the infrared camera used in order to transmit
the infrared radiation through the window.

The LDA probe was mounted on a traverse table which could be traversed in all
three coordinate directions (see Fig. 3.2). This allowed us to document the flow field
around the entire cube. The traverse system had an absolute location accuracy of
0.1 mm provided that a suitable reference point was selected. The reproducibility
was better than 0.1 mm. The infrared camera was also mounted on this traverse
table, but in a different set-up for the measurement of the surface temperature
distributions. The high traverse accuracy was necessary for accurate measurements
for both the Laser Doppler Anemometry and IR-thermography.

3.2 Time-averaged measurement method

A major goal of the present work was to investigate distributions of the time-
averaged local heat transfer coefficient. A local measure of h requires both a local
surface temperature and a local convective heat flux. The most common way is to
use passive heat flux sensors which are primarily based upon a known temperature
difference across a thermal insulator with a well known thermal resistance (thermal
conductivity and layer thickness). A drawback of such kind of sensors is the re-
stricted spatial resolution. Further, a disturbance of both the flow and the thermal
boundary condition may occur, particularly if the thermal properties of the sensor
differ much from those of the heated surface in question. A good review of the
applications, characteristics and drawbacks of passive and active sensors is given
by Diller [39]. A different approach is to impose a constant heat flux at the entire
surface of the cube. This is done, for example, by electrical powering of a thin sheet
of metal of relatively large Ohmic resistance, placed on top of the surface under
investigation. The electrical energy will be uniformly dissipated (provided that the
metal properties are isotropic and that the thickness is uniform) which results in
a constant heat flux through the surface. This approach has major constructive
and geometrical disadvantages and was, therefore, not considered to be a suitable
candidate.

To measure time-averaged distributions of the heat transfer coefficient with a
large spatial resolution, we followed a different approach. This was primarily based
on a known temperature difference and a known thermal resistance. A special
measurement assembly was designed and constructed. It had the capability of de-
termining the convective heat flux distribution at almost the entire surface of the
cube from which the local heat transfer coefficient was derived.

=== [PDF page 45] ===
3.2. Time-averaged measurement method 43

3.2.1 Measurement cube

The measurement assemblies consisted of an internal cubical core of copper covered
by a thin epoxy mantle of well defined thickness of 1.5 mm. The schematic of the
cube is given in Fig. 3.4, a detailed mechanical drawing is shown in Fig. 3.5. The
lower part of the cube was in the form of a bolt which was mounted to the base plate
by anut. The bolt consisted partly of a pass-edge to accurately position the assembly
in the base plate. It was important that the cubes were well positioned with respect
to the global coordinate system for accurate measurements (both anemometry and
thermography). The bolt was also made from the low thermal conductivity epoxy,
the nut was made from a different plastic, both for the sake of minimising heat
losses.

, EPOXY LAYER

FIGURE 3.5: Detailed cross-section of the composed measurement assembly mounted on the channel
floor. The sketch shows also a part of the mounting base and the nut.

It is very crucial that the thickness of the epoxy layer is uniform and well defined
to accurately determine heat fluxes through the layer, as will be seen later in this
thesis. Heat is generated in the centre of the copper core by a dissipating source.
The source consisted of a resistance wire which was twisted spirally around a glass
cylinder and electrically insulated by a glass tube. The lead pins to the heat source
were made of a low thermally and high electrically conducting alloy to minimise
heat losses through the pins and heat dissipation in the pins, respectively. The

=== [PDF page 46] ===
44 - Chapter 3. Experimental set-up and measurement methods

thermal conductivity of copper is about 1000 times larger than that of the epoxy
substrate (the thermal conductivity of copper is about 390 W/mK, that of the used
epoxy 0.24 W/mK). The ratio of the epoxy layer thickness to the copper thickness
was almost 1/5. Hence, the thermal resistance of the epoxy layer is much larger
compared to that of the copper core. The temperature change across the copper
core is, therefore, negligible compared to that across the epoxy-layer which resulted
in an almost uniform copper temperature within the tolerance of 0.05 °C. This
uniformity holds even for relatively high heat fluxes.

The epoxy layer represents a heat flux sensor: when the uniform copper tem-
perature (measured with a thermocouple) and the surface temperature distribution
(measured with thermography) are both known, the conduction problem in the
epoxy can be solved. The resulting temperature distribution in the epoxy served
to derive distributions of the local heat transfer coefficient. Details of the measure-
ment method are given in the next section. A constant copper temperature, stable in
time, was required for the application of the uniform Dirichlet boundary condition.
A special power supply was, therefore, designed to heat the cubes individually. This
controller used the temperature of the copper core, measured with a thermocouple,
and adjusted the power supply such that the set-temperature was obtained. The
adaptive feedback loop in combination with the frequency of updating (4-5 seconds
per update which was constrained by the scan frequency of the data-logger) ensured
a quickly obtained equilibrium state in which the temperature was stable within
0.1 °C. The response time of the measurement cubes, i.e. the time after which the
temperature of the copper core was constant within the tolerance, was about 5-8
minutes.

Because the epoxy layer was in fact the heat flux sensor, a sufficiently thick layer
was required in order to ensure accurate measurements. As we will see later, the
major contribution to the uncertainty in the heat transfer coefficient is from the
surface temperature measurement. A sufficiently large temperature decay across
the epoxy layer, facilitated by the layer thickness, was then required to satisfy the
accuracy demands. On the other hand, a too thick layer will cause a too low tem-
perature at the surface of the cube which is not beneficial for infrared thermography.
From estimations, an epoxy layer thickness of 1.5 mm appeared to be the optimum
selection.

All experiments presented in this thesis were performed at a copper core tem-
perature of 75.0 °C except for one series of single cube experiments for the sake of
investigating the effect of the thermal boundary condition on the convective heat
transfer.

3.2.2 Physical model

The uniform copper temperature of the measurement cube provided the Dirichlet
temperature boundary condition for the inner bound of the epoxy layer. The sur-
face temperature distribution of the outer bound of the substrate, acquired from
thermography experiments, provided the outer temperature boundary condition. A
simple one-dimensional analysis with the neglect of lateral conductive heat fluxes

=== [PDF page 47] ===
3.2. Time-averaged measurement method ; 45

leads to unacceptable error, especially close to the edges and corners.
Therefore, the temperature distribution in the epoxy substrate was calculated
numerically by solving the equation for the three-dimensional conduction problem

[78]: ;
6] OT (x,y,z) \ _

where T (a, y, z) is the temperature distribution in the epoxy substrate and Aij 1s the
anisotropic thermal conductivity. Measurements showed an isotropic thermal con-
ductivity of 0.24 W/mK which was spatially and temperature independent. This

core was imaginary removed from the computational domain. The temperature dis-
tribution of this imaginary plane was composed of the copper temperature for the
mid-plane (the square mid-section of size 12 x 12 mm) and a linear interpolation
between the surface temperature cf the outer wall and the copper temperature.
The remaining numerical domain consisted of the copper core inclusive the epoxy
layer covering the top and the four side faces. The dimensions of the computational
domain were then 15 x 15 x 13.5 mm. Since Dirichlet boundary conditions were
defined for the entire domain, the problem was well defined.

Besides the convective and radiative heat flow from the five faces of the cube
exposed to air, heat conduction occurred at the interface between the cube and the
lower base plate, the sixth face. This heat loss consisted of conduction through
the lead and thermocouple wires and of conduction through the epoxy interface.
However, these losses did not affect the measurement: the temperature distribution
in the entire cube is the result of the equilibrium of all heat fluxes involved. It is
noted that conduction through the base plate preheats the oncoming flow. This will
affect the local reference temperature and thus the heat transfer coefficient. This
effect is only of minor importance (second order effect) ahd is, therefore, not taken
into account.

The conductive heat losses through the sixth interface can be estimated from
an integral approach. The total heat supplied is the total power dissipated in the
electrical heat source and is simply the product of the voltage V and the electrical
current J, namely Pyup = V x J. The total heat Qcona through the five faces exposed
to air flow is the sum of the total radiation Q,ag and convection Qeony- This sum is
in fact the integrated conductive heat flux, as results from the temperature solution
of the Laplace equation:
