Reference temperature

The reference temperature used in the definition of the local heat transfer coefficient
needs special attention. Three different reference temperatures are commonly used:
Tin, Toxo and T,, which are the temperature of the flow at the channel inlet, the
temperature at infinity (often used for boundary layer flows) and the mixed mean
temperature. The latter is obtained from an energy balance over an infinitesimal
length of the channel, specifically:

Ptot

Tin = Tin += ’ (3.9)
MCp

where @yo¢ is the total heat added upstream to the flow, m is the mass flow rate
and ¢, is the specific heat of air. For one dataset of convective heat fluxes, the
use of different reference temperatures leads to different values of the heat transfer
coefficient. Consider a regularly arranged matrix of isothermal cubes (made from
aluminium or copper). If all cubes are heated, the mixed mean temperature of the
flow will increase in the downstream direction due to heat transfer by convection.
Compare this situation with the case that for the similar matrix configuration only
the cube in question is powered. The flow field around the cube in the matrix is,
when the temperature effects are neglected for simplicity, similar for both cases.
If we then define the heat transfer coefficient based on the inlet temperature, we
obtain a different value for the heat transfer coefficient. In the case that all cubes
were powered, the upstream heated cubes caused the air temperature to be higher
in the proximity of the cube in question. This consequently leads to a smaller
convective heat flux. If then the inlet temperature is used as reference temperature,
we obtain a significantly smaller heat transfer coefficient than in the case that only
the cube in question was heated (the temperature of the local environment is then
approximately the inlet temperature, leading to a much larger convective heat flux).

This example illustrates that the heat transfer coefficient is a derived property
depending on its definition, and that it will have different values dependent on the
reference temperatures used. Further, these definitions of h are strongly dependent
on the thermal boundary conditions. This is well known, for example, if the convec-
tive heat transfer in a pipe flow with a uniform wall heat flux is compared to a similar
pipe flow but with a constant wall temperature. The situation becomes complicated
if the upstream thermal boundary conditions are not uniform, for example, in the
case of discrete not-uniformly distributed heat sources. Heat transfer coefficients
based on the aforementioned reference temperatures will all give different values.
Further, the results are only applicable in situations with identical thermal bound-
ary conditions. To enable a proper comparison between laboratory experiments and
reality, or to allow the application of laboratory experiments to the evaluation of
practical heat transfer problems, this ambiguity needs to be eliminated. The goal is
then to find a universal descriptor, independent of the thermal boundary conditions
upstream.

Isothermal cubes The upstream cubes can be considered as discrete heat sources
causing non-uniform distributions of the wall temperature and the surface heat

=== [PDF page 50] ===
48 Chapter 3. Experimental set-up and measurement methods

flux. To address the problem of this non-uniform upstream heating, Moffat et
al. [9, 10, 114] introduced the concept of adiabatic heat transfer for this kind
of application. An adiabatic situation is, according to the physical meaning of
adiabatic, a situation with no heat exchange of any heat transfer mode (radiation,
conduction, convection) with the environment. To translate this concept to arrays
of cubes, the adiabatic situation can be seen as isothermal and perfectly reflecting
cubes (made of polished copper or aluminium) mounted on a perfectly insulating
board. Due to the zero emissivity of the surface, radiation can be disregarded. The
zero thermal conductivity of the mounting board prevents conductive heat exchange
with the environment. When the cube is not powered while the other cubes in the
array remain heated, the unpowered cube in question is convectively heated by the
air. In the equilibrium state it will reach the mean temperature of the air in the
proximity of the cube. Since no heat transfer can take place, this state is then
referred to as adiabatic. The constant temperature of the cube in that case is the
adiabatic reference temperature which is physically independent on the upstream
thermal boundary conditions.

The physical background was provided by Sellars et al. [150]. They considered
a steady-state fully developed laminar flow in a pipe. They assumed parabolic
velocity profiles in the pipe. Further, they assumed a superposition of individual
contributions of the arbitrary heat flux distribution upstream of the location in
question, which is allowed since the energy equation is linear. These contributions
to the local temperature are better known as Kernel functions. The superposition of
these Kernels gives the value for the adiabatic reference temperature. A good review
of this theoretical problem is also given in Eckert and Drake [42]. This approach
was extended by Moffat and co-workers for the mentioned equidistantly arranged
matrix. A superposition of measured thermal wake functions or Kernels resulted in
the adiabatic reference temperature for the matrix of cubes.

In laboratory applications, it is impossible to achieve a true adiabatic situation.
Board materials will always have a non-zero thermal conductivity and the surface
emissivity will always be larger then zero. Thus radiation and conduction can not
be ignored in laboratory experiments. This problem was addressed by Moffat and
co-workers by minimising the heat losses (conduction and radiation) by considering
polished aluminium cubes mounted on a board of balsa wood. The large thermal
conductivity of aluminium resulted in a uniform temperature of the entire cube. In
the case that the cube in question is unpowered while all others remained heated,
the heat losses are in equilibrium with the convective heat flow from the preheated
air to the unpowered cube. This heat loss, Qconv,unp, is directed towards the cube
(the air heats the cube) and it can be expressed as:

Qeonv.unp = haa A(Tunp _ Tad); (3.10)

where /iaq is the adiabatic heat transfer coefficient, Tunp is the unpowered isothermal
temperature of the cube and Tj4 is the adiabatic reference temperature. When the
cube is consequently powered while the other cubes remain heated, the temperature
of the cube will increase due to its own active contribution. The temperature rise
is then relative to the unpowered cube temperature. The convective heat flow,

=== [PDF page 51] ===
3.2. Time-averaged measurement method 49

Qconv,pow, then reads:
Qconv,pow = hadA(Thow - Tunp)s (3.11)

where T,.w is the isothermal temperature of the cube while it is powered. The
first order correction proposed by Moffat and Anderson [9, 10, 114] is only valid
for small heat losses. The adiabatic reference temperature was determined from
a superposition of thermal wake measurements. Another possibility is to measure
the unpowered reference temperature of the cube in question directly when the
cube is unpowered while the other cubes remained powered. The simple first order
correction then leads to the adiabatic reference temperature.

The advantage of copper or aluminium cubes is that the surface and internal
temperatures in both the powered and unpowered situation are uniform. Even in
the case of a relatively large heat flux (for large power dissipations), it can be shown
with a simple calculation that the temperature is uniform within 0.3 °C for the cube
size in question.

The protruding shape of the isothermal cubes will also cause complex flow and
vortex structures. Although the cube itself is isothermal (with the mentioned toler-
ances) this is not the case for the fluid in the vicinity of the cube. Since the local
convective heat flux is strongly dependent on the local fluid temperature, a marked
variation will be present.

Epoxy measurement cubes The measurement cubes used in this study consisted of
a copper core with a thin layer of epoxy. A low thermal conductivity of the epoxy
substrate was selected to make the method successful (the temperature gradients
across the layer give indirectly conductive heat fluxes). A consequence of this ap-
proach is that the surface temperature of the cube is not isothermal in the powered
situation, nor in the unpowered situation. This epoxy layer makes the experiment
different from that of Moffat et al.

Some issues need to be addressed to substantiate the application of the adiabatic
concept to the present situation. One of the major questions to be answered is
whether or not we should select a local reference temperature in the definition of
the local heat transfer coefficient, or whether an averaged temperature suffices. In
other words, are the temperature gradients in the adiabatic situation small enough to
allow the use of the cube- or surface-averaged reference temperature. The following
issues require special attention:

e In addition to strong temperature gradients across the epoxy layer, the low
thermal conductivity has other consequences. It will be shown in the following
chapters where the results are discussed that the flow field and vortex structure
around the cubes caused a strong variation in the surface heat transfer. As
a consequence, the cube has large spatial temperature gradients along the
surface (due to the low thermal conductivity of epoxy). This temperature
distribution can then be considered as a small-scale irregular thermal history,
like the heated upstream cubes in the matrix arrangement. In fact the problem
should be tackled at both scales: at cube level and system (configuration) level.

=== [PDF page 52] ===
00

Chapter 3. Experimental set-up and measurement methods

To measure the local effects at the cube surface, we should then be able to
locally unpower the cube, make it locally adiabatic and then measure the effect
of heating of the upstream surface. A difficulty arises for reverse flows since
heat is recirculated in the vortices. Questions which arise are: which direction
is upstream, what is the net effect on the local reference temperature and so on.
Similar difficulties are expected if multiple cube configurations are considered
since convected heat will recirculate back to the cube in question.

e In the case that the cube is unpowered while the other cubes in the configura-
tion remain powered, the vortex structures around the cube will cause similar
effects: temperature gradients at the cube surface are to be expected, however,
less strong. Due to the turbulent flow field around the cube, the heat which
is convected from the upstream cube will be quickly diffused. In other words,
the vortex and flow structure around the cube does not carry the temperature
information of the surface as it would when the cube in question was powered.
Therefore, the downstream cube will be exposed to well-mixed fluid at almost
uniform temperature.

Despite this well mixing, temperature differences are expected on a larger
scale, for example, between the front and the rear face. The copper then
acts as a heat sink which spreads the heat uniformly. In the case that a
strong temperature gradient exists across the cube, for example, when the
temperature of the front face of the cube is significantly higher than that of
the rear face, the copper core diminishes the temperature decay. This leads
to an underestimation of the temperature of the front face in the unpowered
situation. The surface temperature of the rear face is then overestimated.

It was attempted to measure the surface temperature of an unpowered cube,
placed in the in-line row of cube with the infrared camera (see chapter 6)
but due to the uncertainties encountered when temperatures just above room
temperature are measured, the results were not reliable at all.

Based on simple calculations it was concluded that in the unpowered situation,
the temperature decay across the epoxy layer is of the same order of magnitude
as the variation along the surface is. The decay was estimated to be less
than 0.5 °C. These local effects will give minor deviations in the reference
temperature and its contribution to the experimental uncertainty in the heat
transfer coefficient is negligible.

The cubes were painted with a thermally black paint. This paint was used to

enhance the thermal radiation from the surfaces in order to increase the accuracy of
the infrared surface temperature measurements. Almost all measurement discussed
in this thesis were performed in a turbulent channel flow for which the convective
heat transfer was significantly larger than the radiative heat transfer. Despite that,
the radiation was certainly not negligible.

The cubes were mounted on a board made from formaldehyde. This plastic had

a thermal conductivity of 0.33 W/mK which is about 5 times larger than that of
balsa wood. The selection of this material was based on the good machining quality

=== [PDF page 53] ===
3.2. Time-averaged measurement method 51

since precise positioning of the cubes was required for accurate thermography and
LDA measurements. The value of the thermal conductivity is still sufficiently small
to considerer the conductive heat losses to be much smaller compared to the rate of
convective heat transfer.

Reference temperatures used Based on the foregoing analysis, a cube-averaged adi-
abatic reference temperature was used in the definition of the local heat transfer
coefficient.

For most of the heat transfer results presented in this thesis, only the cube in
question was powered while the other cubes remained unpowered. In that case, the
local reference temperature of the cube is in fact the isothermal temperature of the
entire environment in the situation before starting the experiment: the adiabatic
situation. This temperature was then measured and used in the definition of h.
When the cube is then subsequently powered, conductive heat losses through the
base plate and radiation will both heat the direct environment of the cube and can
be convected back to the fluid. Although these second-order effects will increase the
reference temperature, they are estimated to be negligible.

The experiments for the in-line array of 9 cubes were performed while all cubes
remained powered. The adiabatic reference temperature was then measured in a
separate experiment. In that case, only the cube in question was cut from power
supply during the operation of all other elements. Due to the relatively small heat
fluxes through the walls of the unpowered cube, the temperature decay across the
epoxy layer is negligible. Therefore, the surface temperature was approximated with
the copper core temperature.

Variation of the epoxy layer thickness

The layer thickness of the epoxy substrate dictates in fact, in combination with the
local convective heat transfer, the temperature distribution of its outer boundary.
For a very thin epoxy layer, in the limit of zero thickness, the boundary condi-
tion approaches the uniform temperature condition. For rather thick layers, the
resulting boundary condition is something between a constant temperature and a
constant heat flux boundary condition. Although different surface temperatures
were encountered, consequently resulting in different local cooling rates, the heat
transfer coefficient should, since it is a definition, not be affected by the different
temperature distributions and thus different 6’s.

In order to study the effect of the layer thickness on the heat transfer character-
istics, the CFD-code FLOTHERM [49, 50] was used. Because of its restriction in
the availability of suitable turbulence models, a laminar flow was considered. The
simulations were performed for a single wall-mounted cube in a channel flow. The
channel height was 5 cm. The cube size was 15 mm for all cases, while the inner
copper core was varied in size to obtain different epoxy layer thicknesses. Five thick-
nesses of the epoxy layer were considered, namely: 0.6, 0.9, 1.5, 2.0 and 2.5 mm.
The Reynolds number of the flow was 1000, based on the channel width and the
inlet velocity of 0.32 m/s.

A sufficiently fine grid was designed to guarantee negligible truncation error. An
initial run was performed for the cube with an epoxy layer thickness of 1.5 mm
and with the inner core kept at 75 °C’. The coupled problem was solved for the
cube, i.e. heat conduction in the epoxy substrate, radiation exchange between the
heated cube and the environment and convective heat transfer to the surrounding
air. For simplicity, the effect of buoyancy was neglected. The total heat through all
six faces of the cube, in fact, the heat generation in the isothermal core, was equal
to 1.41 W. This total heat production is considered as the reference situation and
this was implemented as an evenly distributed heat source per unit volume in the
copper core for all other cases. Because of the non-equidistant grid as used in the
numerical model, the heat source per unit volume varies as the inverse of the cell
volume.

=== [PDF page 55] ===
3.2. Time-averaged measurement method 53

ABCDA and ABCD, respectively, for the five studied epoxy layer thicknesses 6.
Path ABCDA denotes a cross-section parallel to the base plate at y/H = 0.5, path
ABCD denotes a cross-section perpendicular to the base plate at z/H = 0. The
ambient temperature was chosen as reference temperature. From the figures it
is observed that the profiles coincide within discrepancies of less than 5%. It can,
therefore, be concluded that the heat transfer coefficient is independent of the epoxy
layer thickness. Although the temperature distributions varied significantly with the
thickness, the heat transfer coefficient apparently did not.

Although a rather small Reynolds number was chosen to study the behaviour of
the epoxy layer thickness on the heat transfer coefficient, it is assumed that these
simulations represent reality. In the case of larger Reynolds numbers, representative
for the turbulent regimes considered in this study, it is believed that differences
become even smaller than for laminar flows since the convective heat transfer is
then less dependent on the thermal boundary conditions.

3.2.3. Radiative heat transfer

For most situations considered in this thesis, the radiative heat flux comprises just
a small part of the total heat transfer. However, in some situations with low charac-
teristic velocities, i.e. natural and mixed convection and areas with strong recircula-
tions, the radiative heat fluxes were of the same order of magnitude as the convective
heat fluxes. The net radiative heat flux from an irradiating surface consists of the
partly reflected irradiation and the emitted radiation [108]:

between surfaces can be expressed in an integral equation [108]:

where S (m) is the distance between subsurfaces A, and A; and @ is the angle be-

It is not desirable to take an infinite number of radiative surfaces with corre-
sponding view factors into account. Besides the fact that this requires temperature
and emissivity information, it is much too complex for an experimental evaluation.
A drastic simplification will already give satisfactory and accurate predictions of the
radiative heat exchange.

According to the analysis of Ortega and Moffat [130], above algebraic analysis
can be adapted to derive the four faces approach. This approach maps the total
surface under consideration onto four representative surfaces. For each of the sur-
faces, the mutual view factors were then calculated. For most of the heat transfer
experiments performed, the cube under investigation was only powered in order to
achieve adiabatic conditions. In that case, the net radiative heat exchange from the
cube surface reduces to the simple Stefan-Boltzmann equation:

PraalM £) = Ee (Tour; é) ~ Tino) > (3.16)

where ¢,,4(7,€) is the local net radiative heat flux, a is the Stefan-Boltzmann con-
stant (9 = 5.67 10-8), ¢ is the surface emissivity, assumed to be constant for both
the cube and the environment, and T,,,, is the ambient temperature. Most of the
experiments were performed in the forced convection regime, where the convective
heat fluxes were much larger than radiative heat fluxes (approximately one order of
magnitude). Some low-Reynolds number experiments were performed for the in-line
array of 9 cubes for which all cubes were powered at the same time (the adiabatic
reference temperature was then measured in a separate experiment). For this case,
the four faces model was used to get a good approximation of the local radiative
heat flux.

Minor contributions to the experimental uncertainty in hag are the errors in the
ambient and reference temperatures. These were measured with copper-constantan
thermocouples (type T) which had, after appropriate calibration, an experimental
uncertainty of 0.1°C. Due to radiative exchanges etc., the accuracy of these temper-
ature measurements was estimated to be within 0.5 °C. The copper temperature,
also measured with an embedded and calibrated thermocouple, yielded an accuracy
of 0.1 °C’.

The uncertainty in the surface temperature distribution, measured with infrared
thermography, had a large impact on the overall accuracy of the heat transfer coeffi-
cient. Application of both the in situ calibration and the image restoration technique
resulted in an absolute accuracy of 0.4 °C in the surface temperature for the mid-
plane of the faces, and of about 0.6 °C’ close to the edges because of the effects of
image mapping. In the next chapter, a thorough discussion will be given of both
the image restoration and the in situ calibration. Other important contributions to
the experimental uncertainty in hag are the errors in the thermal conductivity of the
epoxy substrate, the layer thickness 6 and possible truncation errors in the numeri-
cal solution of the internal temperature distribution. In the next sections, a detailed
discussion is given of these contributions to the total accumulated uncertainty in
the heat transfer coefficient.

Thickness of the epoxy layer

The accuracy of the heat transfer coefficient is very sensitive to the epoxy layer
thickness. Therefore, this layer was machined very accurately to a thickness of 1.5
mm with an uncertainty of 0.01 mm. The thickness of the layer was also effected
by thermal expansion of the epoxy substrate during the heat transfer experiments.
A one-dimensional (1D) analysis with additional measurements was performed to
examine the thermal expansion of the epoxy substrate.

The thermal expansion of a one-dimensional layer of a material of thickness H,

=== [PDF page 58] ===
56 Chapter 3. Experimental set-up and measurement methods

(m) in the reference situation (ambient temperature) is expressed as:
H = H,(1+ AT), (3.20)

where H is the thickness of the layer at the temperature T, a (°C ~') is the thermal
expansion coefficient and AT (°C) is the temperature rise (I - Tams). Such an
expression can be assumed for both the copper core and the epoxy layer in the 1D
approach. The total thickness of the composed material then becomes L = 2d +
D, where d (m) is the layer thickness and D (m) denotes the rib of the copper.
Expressed in thermal expansion, this becomes:

L=f,+ (2dQepoay AT + Docopper AT2), (3.21)

where Qepory aNd Qcopper are the thermal expansion coefficients of epoxy and copper.
To simplify the analysis, the entire material is assumed to be at uniform temperature
which implies that the temperature rises AT; and AT) are equal.

Thermal expansion experiments were performed by monitoring the instantaneous
thickness L of the entire cube as a function of the instantaneous temperature during
a heating up cycle from 20 °C’ to 80 °C. The thickness increase was measured with
an accurate dilatometer which measures the expansion compared to a reference
point. The thickness increase (ZL — L,)/ Lp is shown in Fig. 3.8. The contribution of
the copper core to the total increase in length is estimated from the known thermal
expansion coefficient of copper, namely Qcopper = 1.7 10-5 °C-!, The remaining
expansion was attributed to the two epoxy layers. For one layer, the relative thermal
expansion becomes (d — d,)/d, = 5.8 x 107%, at a temperature of 70 °C’. This is
an increase of less than 0.6%. The thermal expansion coefficient of epoxy was
determined from the slope of the curves in Fig. 3.8. A value of 2.71 x 107° was
found with a least square fit, resulting in a thermal expansion coefficient of Qepory =
6.8 x 1075 °C for epoxy. This is in good agreement with the range of expansion
coefficients reported in handbooks Gepory = 5-4-9.0 x 107° °C.

3.2, Tume-averaged measurement method oT

this layer was uniform and smaller than 0.06 mm at the entire surface. Further,
the thermal conductivity of the black paint is much larger than that of the epoxy
substrate (about one order of magnitude) which implies that the temperature de-
cay across the paint-layer is negligible. Therefore, the used black paint does not
introduce additional error to the epoxy layer thickness and, thus, to the calculated
conductive heat fluxes.

Truncation errors

Since the finite volume method (central differencing) was used to solve the Laplace
equation numerically, the truncation error in the temperature solution is of second
order. Grid refinement was applied to both an artificial test case and real experi-
ments to investigate the truncation errors involved. The test case was examined to
study fine grids. The spatial resolution in the measured surface temperature distri-
butions (obtained from the infrared thermography) allowed a selection of maximum
30 x 30 grid cells on one face of the cube. This resolution was artificially increased
by interpolation between pixel points. This, of course, does not add information
but allows us to examine the magnitude of the truncation error.

Test case. For the test case, constant outer wall and copper temperatures of 60
and 80 °C’ were selected as boundary condition. The Laplace equation was solved
for different grid sizes. From the temperature solution, the local conductive heat
fluxes through the outer surface were calculated. An integration of the local heat
flux resulted in the conductive heat flow. Since three-dimensional effects (the lateral
fluxes) were expected to be most significantly present in the edge region, a distinction
was made between the mid-plane and the edges/corners. The mid-plane refers to
the area above the copper core, with dimension 12 x 12 mm. The edges are the
remaining part of the face. Due to the constant temperature boundary condition
and the symmetry of the numerical domain, the heat transfer was similar for all
four side faces. The conductive heat through the top face will be different from that
through the side faces because the lower epoxy layer was imaginary removed from the
computational domain. Six equidistant grid sizes were investigated, corresponding
to 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 and 7 grid cells in the layer thickness. The results for the test case
are given in Figs. 3.9 and 3.10. The different symbols refer to the top and side faces.
With an increasing number of cells, the total conduction converges gradually to an
asymptoting limit, found for the infinite number of volume cells or the analytical
solution. Differences in the calculated conductive heat flow between the course
grids and the asymptotic limit are largest for the edges. This is expected since
three-dimensional effects (the lateral heat fluxes) are most dominantly present in
this part of the epoxy layer. The differences between the calculated conductive heat
flow through the edges for the course grid (2 cells along the thickness) and the fine
grid (7 cells along the thickness) were about 5% for both the top and side faces.
The differences between the grid with 3 cells along the thickness of the epoxy layer,
the resolution which is used for most of the results given in this thesis, are of the
order of magnitude of 2-3%. Because of the relatively thin epoxy layer compared to

=== [PDF page 60] ===
58 Chapter 3. Experimental set-up and measurement methods

the overall dimensions of the cube, the heat transfer is more or less one-dimensional
in the mid-area of the faces. This results in small truncation errors, even for the
courser grids (see Figs. 3.9 and 3.10). The differences between the course (2 cells)
and fine grid (7 cells) were of the crder of magnitude of 0.5% for both faces. This
value drops to 0.3% for the grid with 3 cells along the thickness of the epoxy layer.

Real experiment. The resolution of the surface temperature fields was artificially
doubled by linear interpolations between the grid nodes. A resolution of 60 x 60
surface cells per face was achieved in this way (six cells along the thickness). For
a representative case, the single cube in a turbulent channel flow, the results for
the two different grid sizes are compared in Fig. 3.11. As expected, differences
in the local heat transfer coefficient for the two different sizes are very small in
the mid-plane region, while these increase somewhat close to the edges. Values
of the truncation error for these regions are in the order of magnitude of 2% and
5%, respectively. It was already argued that the accuracy of the local heat transfer
coefficients close to the edges suffered from larger experimental uncertainty than the
heat transfer coefficients at the mid-plane do. The larger truncation errors in this
area add up to the experimental uncertainty and, therefore, support this conclusion.
The total conduction through the five faces exposed to air was calculated for both
the regular size of 30 x 30 grid cells and the finer grid with 60 x 60 grid cells per
face. The results showed that the total conduction was approximately 1-2% smaller
for the finer grid. Differences in the averaged heat transfer coefficient were also 1-
2%. The artificial increase of the spatial resolution by linear interpolation modifies
the surface temperature somewhat and, therefore, introduces an interpolation error.
It is not clear if both contributions add up or nullify each other but since the errors
are only 1-2%, it is assumed that the 30 x 30 grid is sufficiently accurate.

From these observations it can be concluded that for the grid size with 30 x
30 surface cells (3 cells in the layer thickness) the truncation error is within 3%.

Conservation of heat fluzes per volume cell. The equilibrium of conductive heat

the rounding error (about 1 x 10~*) which verified that the code was implemented
correctly.

Overall accuracy of the heat transfer coefficient

From the above analysis of the different contributions to the experimental uncer-
tainty it is concluded that the accumulation of errors results in approximately 5%
accuracy in the local heat transfer coefficient for the mid-region of the five faces
of the cube. The heat transfer coefficients at the edges have a somewhat higher
uncertainty of about 10 % due to, among others, mapping inaccuracies.

3.3. Time-dependent heat flux method

It will be shown in the following chapters in which results are discussed that the
flow around the configurations of cubes is locally unstable which leads to periodic
vortex shedding in the wake of the cube. These large coherent structures have a
significant impact on the convective heat transfer. It was assumed that the vortex
shedding was directly coupled to the time-dependent convective heat transfer. This
has been demonstrated for the flow past obstacles of other geometry, like cylinders
in cross-flow [146, 147, 148, 155, 99, 142]. These studies clearly showed that the
velocity field and convective heat transfer from the heated cylinder were strongly
coupled (the coherence between both time signals showed a strong peak at the vortex
shedding frequency in question). To obtain more insight in the physical interaction
between the flow and the surface heat transfer in flows over wall-mounted cubes, the
time-dependent convective heat transfer was measured with a fast-responding heat
flux sensor. Diller [39] has reviewed most of the useful passive and active techniques

=== [PDF page 62] ===
60 Chapter 3. Experimental set-up and measurement methods

and calibration methods. The review summarises the difficulties and problems a
researcher has to face to obtain accurate results. The response time of the sensor
is one of the most significant requirements for transient heat transfer research. The
sensor needs to be flush mounted since layer differences will cause distortion of
the flow and, therefore, distortion of the measurement. Further, different thermal
properties of the sensor will cause disruption of the thermal boundary layer. This
effect is not significantly present when an active sensor is used. In that case, the
only thermal boundary which will develop is created by the sensor itself. One of
the most recent developments is the heat flux microsensor which basically consists
of a deposited insulation layer with a thickness of less than 1 micron with on both
sides deposited thermocouple junctions. These junctions are connected serially to
form a thermopile aimed at increasing the signal-to-noise ratio. The output signal
is then a summation of all small thermocouple voltages. Details of this sensor and
the technique involved are discussed in Hager et al. [59, 60], Diller [39], Mancuso
and Diller [99] and Simmons et al. {155}.

In this work, an active time-dependent heat flux sensor was developed. This
sensor consisted of a heated thin metal film deposited on an insulating substrate of
glass. The frequency response of the sensor was determined from a calibration with
chopped laser beams which illuminated the sensor. Further, the sensor response
was calibrated to on fore hand known periodic oscillations in the flow. This was
achieved by placing the sensor in the wake of a cylinder. The shedding frequency
was then simultaneously measured with a hot-wire anemometer. Our main objective
of this heat flux sensor was the measurement of possible coherence between the
vortex shedding and the time-dependent convective heat flux. This implied that we
were mainly interested in detecting possible characteristic frequencies carried by the
time-dependent heat flux signal rather than determining the absolute value of the
time-dependent convective heat flux.

3.3.1 Fast-responding heat flux sensor

The heat flux sensor consisted of a rectangular glass substrate of size 10 x 4 mm.
Two vacon pins (a metal alloy) were melted in the glass at a distance of 4 mm
apart in a parallel orientation. Lead wires were soldered to these pins for electrical
power supply. Vacon was selected since it was well suited to be embedded in glass.
Further, vacon has a relatively large electrical conductivity which prevented power
dissipation in the lead wires itself. The top layer of the glass substrate with the
two vacon pins embedded in parallel was polished to obtain a smooth surface with
a roughness of less than 1 nanometer. A layer of platinum with thickness of 0.2
micron was vapour deposited on the entire polished surface with a vacuum deposition
technique. Platinum adhered well to the used glass. The two vacon pins were then
electrically connected through the platinum film on top of the surface. The active
area of the sensor was 4 x 4mm. A sketch of this sensor is given in Fig. 3.12. A
more or less similar approach was followed by Campbell et al. [26] and Simmons et
al. [155].

A voltage drop was maintained across the metal foil. Due to the large electrical

conductivity of vacon, the two parallel pins acted as a kind of electrical current
spreader. This resulted in a parallel electrical field through the platinum film which
caused a spatially uniform dissipation of the electrical energy and, consequently, a
uniformly generated heat flux in the metal foil. This uniformity in the heat flux
distribution is only guaranteed if the electrical field is parallel, the foil is of uniform
thickness and the specific resistance of the film material is not a function of the
spatial coordinate.

The thin film had a relatively large (positive) temperature-dependent Ohmic re-
sistance R(T) (see Fig. 3.14). Several sensors were made for which the resistances
were about 3.9 2 at room temperature. The metal sheet was thin and small enough
to assume the temperature to be constant across the entire metal foil (no spatial
temperature gradients). The operation of the sensor was based on this constant-
temperature assumption. A controller was used to adjust the power supply to main-
tain the metal sheet at a constant temperature (a conventional hot-wire anemometer
controller was used [90]). The convective heat transfer from the heated foil is then
directly proportional to the power supply required to maintain the temperature of
the foil at the constant set value. The output voltage of the controller was sampled
and stored with a signal memory recorder. The sample frequency was between 300
and 500 Hz which was sufficiently large compared to the vortex shedding frequencies
in question. The number of data points for one measurement was about 32,000.

The hot-film sensor was built in a cube made from formaldehyde. The thermal
conductivity of formaldehyde was experimentally determined to be 0.33 W/mK (see
appendix A). Care was taken to mount the sensor flush with the surface of the cube
to prevent distortion of the flow. The hot-film sensor was embedded in the cube at

=== [PDF page 64] ===
62 Chapter 3: Experimental set-up and measurement methods

actually two different places at the side face, as indicated in Fig. 3.13, to measure
the effect of vortex shedding. In the sketch, the sensor is embedded in the back half
of the side face. Note that if the cube is 180° rotated, the sensor is located in the
front half of the cube.

3.3.2 Time-dependent heat transfer coefficient

The measured resistance across the platinum foil is surface-averaged and corresponds
to one film (sensor) temperature, Tsen- The heat transfer coefficient h(t) is, therefore,
an average over the entire film surface, namely:

Qeonv (t)

n(t) A(Toen(t) ~ Tret) , (8.22)
where Qconv(t) is the time-dependent convective heat flow, A refers to the sensor
area exposed to air (A = 4 x 4 mm?) and T,.¢ is the reference temperature. Since
only a small part of the cube is heated (only the sensor area) the local oncoming flow
can be assumed at isothermal conditions (T,.5 = Tin, the inlet temperature of the
flow). The sensor temperature Tsen(t) is controlled to a constant value by means of
the Wheatstone bridge and is, compared to the variation of the convective heat flux,
approximately constant. The only time-dependent quantity in the expression for the
instantaneous heat transfer coefficient is then the convective heat flow. The total
electrical energy dissipated in the platinum foil is the product of the time-dependent
voltage drop V(t) and the electrical current I(t):

P(t) = V(t) x I(t). (3.23)
A global energy balance over the platinum foil reads:

where plpV & is the transient contribution (p is the density of platinum, c, is its
specific heat and V is the volume of the platinum foil), Qioss(t) is the time-dependent
heat loss which consists of radiation (from the upper face) and conduction (through
the glass substrate and vacon pins). The platinum foil had a low emissivity of €
~ 0.1 and radiative heat losses are, therefore, small. The thermal conductivity of
glass is in the order of magnitude of 1 W/mK. Due to the slow time response of
the underlying system of the glass substrate, ie. the cube of formaldehyde and
the vacon connecting pins, the temperature variations are damped. It is difficult to
accurately estimate the time-dependent heat losses. A way of minimising the heat
losses is by maintaining the entire cube at the sensor temperature by an additional
heat source in the cube itself. The zero temperature difference between the sensor
and the cube then minimises the time-dependent heat losses.

In first instance, this sensor was developed to measure absolute values of the
time-dependent convective heat flux and heat transfer coefficient. But due to its
disappointing accuracy, we decided to concentrate only on the frequency informa-
tion carried by the time-dependent heat flux signals. Therefore, only a dynamic
calibration of the sensor was performed.

3.3.3 Calibration procedures

The electrical resistance dependency R(Tsen) of the platinum film on the sensor
temperature T,., was determined with a temperature-controlled oven. To account
for all contributions to the total resistance imposed by the intermediate equipment,
the sensor was calibrated while it was connected to the Wheatstone bridge. The
temperature dependency of the film resistance, including the connected metal wires,
is shown in Fig. 3.14. The linear relation is obvious from the figure. The sensitivity
is sufficiently large to estimate the actual sensor temperature while the sensor is
operated.

FIGURE 3.14: Temperature dependency of the electrical resistance of the platinum film, including
the connecting wires, of the heat flux sensor.

The frequency response of the sensor was calibrated with a chopped laser beam
from an Argon-Helium laser. This is a well known technique and has been used by
several researchers to obtain the dynamic characteristics of heat flux sensors (see, for
example, Holmberg and Diller [65], Hager et al. (59, 60] and Diller [39]). The laser
beam illuminated the platinum film with the frequency imposed by the chopper. The
resulting saw-tooth like response signal allowed us to estimate the response time of
the sensor. From experiments with different chopper frequencies is was concluded
that the deposited platinum layer was sufficiently thin to follow frequencies of at
least 200 Hz. A second dynamic calibration of the heat flux sensor was performed
in the wake flow of a suspended circular cylinder. The sensor was flush mounted in
the top face of the cube while the cylinder was oriented horizontally upstream of the
cube (in parallel with the base plate). The vortex shedding from the cylinder caused
large-scale flow periodicities in its wake. When the sensor is placed in this oscillating
flow, the heated area will be periodically cooled by convection. The time-dependent
convective heat flux contains then the vortex shedding frequency.

A typical power density spectrum of the time-dependent heat flux is shown in
Fig. 3.15 for a bulk velocity of 2.1 m/s. The spectrum clearly shows a peak at about
f = 85 Hz which is attributed to the vortex shedding. This frequency was estimated
to be accurate within 5%. This value included experimental uncertainties as well as

=== [PDF page 67] ===
3.3. Time-dependent heat flux method 65

The calibration measurements were performed for a range of shedding frequen-
cies, ie. 10 < f < 200. The results confirmed a good dynamic response of the
used heat flux sensor for frequencies up to at least 200 Hz. This value is an order
of magnitude higher than the vortex shedding frequencies present in the wake flow
of wall-mounted cubes in the turbulent channel flow for Reynolds numbers between
1000 < Rey < 5000 as considered in this study. It is, therefore, concluded that
the dynamic response of the sensor is sufficient to accurately detect dominant fre-
quencies in the time-dependent convective heat flux. It is explicitly mentioned that
the sensor was not statically calibrated to determine accurate convective heat fluxes
with it. Much more effort is then needed to analyse and to estimate the different
time-dependent heat losses involved.

66

Chapter 3. Experimental set-up and measurement methods

=== [PDF page 69] ===
CHAPTER 4

Diagnostic measurement techniques

The measurement techniques discussed in this chapter are used to provide the nec-
essary information for the evaluation of the local convective heat transfer from the
cubes and of the flow structure around the cubes.

Distributions of the time-averaged surface temperature were determined with in-
frared thermography (IR). This is a commonly used diagnostic measurement tech-
nique applied in a broad range of thermo-fluid problems. But the application of
infrared thermography in this study required significant image processing and a
sophisticated calibration in order to achieve a high accuracy in the temperature
results. The discussion in this chapter will therefore mainly be concentrated on the
development of these improvements rather than describing infrared thermography in
general. For such a description, reference is made to standard literature. To obtain
an objective validation of the time-averaged infrared temperature measurements,
the liquid crystal thermography technique was explored. For similar experimental
situations, the infrared surface temperature measurements were validated with liq-
uid crystal measurements. The calibration and the application of the liquid crystal
method used will be discussed in this chapter.

Flow visualisation was performed with smoke and oil-film visualisation tech-
niques. Since these techniques are commonly used, only a brief description of the
used application method will be given. Detailed documentation of the time-averaged
vortex structures and flow dynamics around the cubes was performed with the Laser
Doppler Anemometry technique (LDA). LDA is a well known and thoroughly dis-
cussed non-intrusive measurement technique to measure fluid velocities in a seeded
flow. But the LDA method involves several sources of uncertainty which depend,
for example, on the system, the geometry, the optics and the situation. Detailed
analysis and diagnostics of the results are needed and all errors involved need to be
addressed before the experimental results are acceptable and satisfactory.

Further, topological principles were applied to the velocity data obtained with
the LDA and visualisation techniques to derive additional flow details.

Finally, local and face-averaged static pressure coefficients were measured with
a pressure-cube.

4.1 Liquid Crystal Thermography

The cholesteric liquid crystals (LC) measurements performed in this study served
for the validation of the infrared surface temperature measurements. A cletailed
discussion of the different properties and possible applications of liquid crystals
is given in the accessible literature (see, for example, Moffat [114] and Baughn
{18]). The used encapsulated cholesteric liquid crystals are substances with both
the properties of a liquid and a crystalline state. The molecular ordering of these

67

=== [PDF page 70] ===
68 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

liquid crystals changes under the influence of temperature. This reversible change in
molecular structure causes wavelength-selective reflection of the incident light. The
unambiguous relation between temperature and reflected light enables the observer
to identify surface temperatures. The liquid crystals are encapsulated to improve
the stability since they are protected from atmospheric contamination. A liquid
crystal is characterised by its event temperature and the corresponding band-width
being the temperature range in which the crystal changes from colourless to red,
green, blue, and back to colourless.

4.1.1 Calibration of the Liquid Crystals

A mixture of five crystals was used which had narrow band-widths of approximately
2 °C and event temperatures at respectively 47.5, 55.0, 60.0, 65.0 and 73.0 °C’ [132].
Moffat et al. [45, 46] developed a sophisticated calibration system based on the
brightness, hue and intensity of the emitted light which unambiguously related each
colour to a surface temperature for a mixture of liquid crystals. Such an approach
was also adopted by Stasiek {157}, Sun et al. [159], Hollingsworth et al. [64] and
Akino et al. [4]. Since we were only interested in an objective validation of the
infrared measurements, we adapted a different approach. Only the sharp transition
lines between the green and the red appearance, which are isotherms on the surface,
were considered. The reason was twofold. First, the transitions were very sharp and
easy to recognise. Second, the transitions provide an objective colour interpretation
and the real colour, the human sensation, was not relevant.

The LC mixture was calibrated with an a priori known temperature distribution
of a heated copper plate, thermally insulated with foam from below and from the
sides. This copper plate (calibration rig) is schematically shown in Fig. 4.1. The
upper plot in the figure shows the front view. The marked holes represent the lo-
cations of the thermocouples. The lower plot in Fig. 4.1 refers to the cross-section
_ at the centre-line of the calibration rig. A temperature gradient across the 10 mm
thick copper plate was maintained by means of two heat exchangers, located at each
side of the plate. The resulting one-dimensional almost linear temperature decay
was monitored with 22 thermocouples distributed along the centre-line, some of
them were located off-axis. The thermocouples were mounted just underneath the
upper surface. The high thermal conductivity of the copper plate and its relatively
small thickness ensured that no temperature gradients existed along the thickness.
The off-axis thermocouples were used to ensure the one-dimensionality of the tem-
perature gradient. The thermocouples along the centre-line were used to measure
the temperature gradient. A typical temperature profile is given in Fig. 4.2. The
calibrated thermocouples were accurate within 0.1 °C’.

The copper plate was painted with the liquid crystal mixture. The colour pat-
terns were photographed and from these pictures, the temperatures corresponding
to the green-red transitions were determined from a linear interpolation between
the subsequent temperature readings. The calibration temperatures found with this
method were confirmed with a second calibration performed with a pure copper
cube. This cube was controlled to a constant temperature within the tolerance of

=== [PDF page 72] ===
70 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

purpose of the liquid crystal measurements was only to validate the temperature
results from the infrared technique.

4.1.2 Data acquisition and processing

The surface of the cubes in question was painted with the calibrated mixture of
five liquid crystals. Care was taken to guarantee that the experimental conditions
during the calibration were similar to those during the real experiments, such as
the source and the angle of illumination, the age of the mixture, the intensity of
the light source, etc. An infrared filter was used to prevent heating of the surface
by the incident thermal radiation. Excessive thermal radiation effects the surface
temperature of the faces of the epoxy cube due to the low thermal conductivity of
the epoxy substrate. Since this filter effects the incident radiation (light intensity,
brightness, etc.), it was applied for both the calibration measurements and real
measurements to maintain similar experimental conditions. The colour patterns
were then photographed with a fine grain size colour film in order to elucidate the
colour pattern at the cube faces. A zoom-ocular was used for the magnification of
the object. A typical example of a liquid crystal measurement is given in Fig. 4.3 for
a Reynolds number of Rey = 5066. The image shows the temperature distribution
at two faces of the cube, namely the side and top face. The cube was located in
the in-line row of 9 cubes, as discussed in chapter 6. The method used to calibrate
the liquid crystal mixture provided five absolute surface isotherms. For the result
shown here, only four isotherms appear at the surface which correspond to the four
lowest temperatures (ie. 47.5, 55.8, 60.5 and 65.2 °C’). These photographs were
then mapped onto an isotherm plot.

4.2 Infrared thermography

Infrared thermography is commonly used to analyse convective heat transfer phe-
nomena, as encountered in thermo-fluid problems. Good reviews of the different

=== [PDF page 73] ===
4.2. Infrared thermography 71

applications and the technical drawbacks of infrared thermography are hard to find
but work of Carlomagno et al. [27, 28] are recommended. Further, background
information is given in the Agema training manuals [3].

Some advantages of infrared thermography are that this technique is rather easy
to operate and that a two-dimensional temperature field is obtained after proper
calibration. Care should be taken when this technique is employed for the measure-
ment of surface temperature fields of small heated three-dimensional protrusions.
In particular when temperature distributions with a high spatial resolution and a
large depth-of-field are required, the measurements can suffer from large spatial
image degradation which may lead to an unacceptable error in the measured sur-
face temperature. This obviously prevents the reliable evaluation of heat transfer
phenomena, in particular in laboratory applications. The accuracy of infrared tem-
perature measurements for these kind of applications can be increased by employing
an image restoration technique. Furthermore, environmental contributions to the
total radiosity received by the infrared sensor are usually difficult to quantify and
complicate an accurate analysis. This problem can be faced by employing an in situ
calibration which accounts for the various environmental reflections and emissions.

This section gives a detailed analysis of the infrared thermography used in this
thesis to measure the steady-state surface temperature distributions of the cubes.
The characteristics of the infrared camera used are firstly discussed. Further, details
of the mapping procedures and data processing are given. Much attention is given
to the in situ calibration and the image restoration techniques, both developed to
increase the absolute accuracy of the temperature measurements.

4.2.1 Infrared camera characteristics

The infrared camera used [169] had a two-dimensional scanning mechanism to create
200 image-lines, each consisting of 300 pixels. The low frame-rate of 2 Hz restricted
its application to steady-state situations. This camera was suitable for our applica-
tion since we were only interested in steady-state surface temperature distributions.
The wavelength bandwidth of the camera was between 3 and 5 micron. An 8-bits
AD converter digitised the received signal in 256 grey values. This dynamic range
ensured a thermal resolution of at least 0.2 °C for the situations investigated in this
research. The images used were accumulations of five images, consecutive in time,
stored in a temporary buffer in the camera. The time scales of the heat transfer
processes studied were far beyond the time to create these accumulated images (4-5
seconds) and therefore, this low scan frequency did not hamper the measurements.

The calibration curve as supplied by the manufacturer, given in Fig. 4.4, shows
the linearity of the system for a relatively large temperature range, namely between
-35 °C and 200°C. The resolution in the for the present study relevant temperature
range (45-65 °C’) was low and, therefore, a recalibration was performed with a perfect
black body for the reduced temperature range of 30-70 °C’. A simple calculation ver-
ified that the emissivity of the used black body was almost 1 (0.999, see [108]). The
black-body temperature was measured with a PT100 element. The camera response
versus the black-body temperature, plotted in Fig. 4.5, shows again the linearity of

part of the calibration is less accurate. An in situ calibration was developed, to be
discussed in the next section, to obtain accurate temperature measurements.

A copper object of square cross-section was perpendicularly scanned to measure
the sampling density as function of the object-distance. A sampling density of
approximately 7 pixels/mm was encountered for an object distance of O, = 0.19 m.
No difference in sampling density was measured between the horizontal and vertical
scan-direction, thus providing a square sampling grid.

4.2.2 Data acquisition and processing

To measure the surface temperature distribution of the cubes in the closed wind
tunnel, the infrared radiation from the cube surfaces needs to be transmitted by
the window. This requires a window with transmittance characteristics compatible
to those of the infrared camera used. A foil was selected which had a uniform
transmittance of 0.84 in the range of 1-10 micron.

The side faces of the cubes, oriented perpendicularly to the mounting base, were
scanned at a 45° scan angle because of geometrical restrictions of the closed wind
tunnel. This scan facility is drawn in Fig. 4.6. The non-perpendicular scan-direction
has two major complications. First, the spatial resolution decreases with the scan-
angle. And second, a scan at 45° requires a depth-of-field of at least + 5 mm for
a direct and accurate temperature measurement across the entire face. A typical
example of an infrared image scanned with this set-up is given in Fig. 4.7.

The cube surfaces were painted with a thermally black paint to enhance the
radiation needed for infrared thermography. For scan angles up to approximately

95-60°, the emissivity appeared to be independent of the scan angle and equals ¢ =
0.95, see Fig. 4.8. This value was verified with measurements performed with two
other methods. One method used a vacuum chamber in which a copper cube was
hung. The cube was powered internally with an electrical heat source. The vacuum
conditions were used to minimise the convective heat transfer from the cube to
the environment. Conduction losses were minimised by using thin lead wires. An
energy balance over the cube gave an accurate expression for the total radiative
exchange in which the surface emissivity of the used paint was the only unknown.
The other method was based on a black body experiment. The results involved
an experimental uncertainty of 2%. An accurate value of the surface emissivity
was not necessary for accurate surface temperature measurements since the in situ
calibration, to be discussed later, counter-balanced the emissivity (the calibration

=== [PDF page 76] ===
74 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

was performed with an identical object which was painted with the same thermally
black paint). An accurate value for the emissivity is only required for the calculation
of the net radiative heat flux needed to determine the convective heat flux from the
equilibrium of heat fluxes at the surface of the cube.

Four different projections were employed to cover the total surface of the three-
dimensional cube. A schematic is given in Fig. 4.9. The five different cube faces are
denoted as rear (R, the face in the wake of the cube), side (S, the north and south
side faces), front (F, the windward face), and top (T). The coordinate system is also
given in Fig. 4.9.

FIGURE 4.9: Sketch of the four different projections employed to cover the total surface of the
three-dimensional cube.

Since the cube faces cover only a small part of the entire recorded image, loca-
tion identification was a necessity. Especially the optical blur prevented a correct
identification of the exact position of the object in the infrared image. This problem
was solved by a grid cube which had a high precision thermal grid at its surface.
This cube was made of copper with precisely located grooves which were filled with
black paint. The high thermal conductivity of copper resulted in a uniform copper
surface temperature. The pronounced emissivity differences between black paint (€
= 0.95) and copper (€ & 0.3) supplied a good contrast. A schematic drawing is
shown in figure 4.10. The infrared image in Fig. 4.11 of two faces of the grid-cube
clearly shows the good contrast. The cube location in the image was determined
from these images of the grid-cube and was applied to all other measurements. The
used traverse mechanism had a displacement accuracy of 0.1 mm which ensured a
reproducible measurement to allow this one-to-one mapping. It was possible to de-
termine perspective functions from this grid-cube but perspective effects appeared
to be minor and, therefore, this is not further discussed here.

With the relative pixel coordinates of the cube faces in the infrared image ob-
tained from a separate experiment with the grid cube, an equidistantly constructed
orthogonal grid of NxN surface cells was then superimposed on the two faces con-
tained by the infrared image to obtain the spatial distribution of the averaged pixel
value. In fact the distribution of the pixel intensities is mapped upon a equidis-

tant grid of size NxN. The infrared system had a focal matrix of 300 x 200 pixels.
The optimum grid resolution appeared to be 30 x 30 grid cells per cube face (see
chapter 3). For this resolution, the focal length was 0.19 m and the averaged in-
tensity per surface cell was determined from about 5-8 individual pixels. This focal
length corresponded to the minimum possible distance to operate the camera due
to geometrical restrictions. An increase of the focal length leads consequently to
a decrease in image magnification and, thus, to a loss of spatial resolution. For
larger grids, for example 40x40 surface cells, the averaged pixel value per surface
cell is based on less individual pixels which leads to an increased noise level in the
measurements.

The procedure to define the corners is estimated to be accurate within 1 pixel-
position. This corresponded to a temperature uncertainty of less than 0.1 °C for
the mid-plane and less than 0.2 °C’ for the edges.

4.2.3 Intensity-temperature conversion
Global conversion

Infrared thermography is based on the unambiguous relation between the surface
emission of electro-magnetic waves of a heated object and its surface temperature.
The blackbody spectral emittance is described by Planck’s law [108, 154]. From an
integration of the spectral intensity distribution over the wavelength interval 0 < A
< 00, the total radiant emittance W, of a black body (e = 1) is obtained, which is
known as the Stefan-Boltzmann equation [108]:

W, = oT", (4.1)

where o is the Stefan-Boltzmann constant. In a practical situation, a surface will
almost never comply with the laws of perfect black-body emission over an extended
wavelength region but it may be approached in a certain spectral interval.

The total radiosity received by the thermal detector consists of several contri-
butions from the thermal environment which can be divided in four global main
contributions [108, 3]:

=== [PDF page 78] ===
76 - Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

- The emission from the target.
The total power emitted by the target is:

oon; = €o0j 7 Toby (4.2)

where €,,; is the object-emissivity, 7 denotes the Stefan-Boltzmann constant
and T),; is the object temperature.

- Reflections from the environment.
The total reflected power on the object from the environment:

Benv = Pobj env Deny = (1 _ Eobj)EenvO Lemy (4.3)

where .»; is the reflection coefficient of the object, €eny is the emissivity of
the environment and T.,, denotes the temperature of the environment.

- The emission from air.
The total emitted power from the air is:

- air = Cain T = (1 _ Tair OTS

air air?

(4.4)

where €qi, is the emissivity of air, Tair is the transmittance of air and Tj; is
the air temperature. , ;

- The internal camera contributions.
Internal camera reflections, emissions, etc., contribute to the total received
power:
Ecamera = —€chpPehp + €optPopt; (4.5)

where P.», is radiative power from the chopper, Po: is the power from the
optics and €gp and €cnp are the emissivity of the optics and the chopper, re-
spectively.

Radiative power emitted and reflected by the object is attenuated by air (absorp-
tion). This is accounted for by a multiplication with the transmittance of air Tair,
specifically:

Tair — eo, (4.6)

where d is the distance between the target and the sensor and a is the absorptivity of
air. For small distances, 7,;- is almost unity. The camera optics (lens etc.) will also
attenuate the transmitted radiation and, therefore, the individual terms have to be
multiplied by the transmittance of the optics Top. If the object is observed trough
a window, the transmitted thermal radiation will be attenuated by absorption and
reflection. Therefore, the transmittance of the window 7,, should also be taken into
account.

The previous emissivities, transmittances and reflection coefficients can be wave-
length dependent. The considered expressions denote integrated values over a lim-
ited wavelength region (3-5 micron) which is determined by the optical system (lens
etc.) and the thermal sensor.

The summation of all global contributions finally gives an expression for the
object temperature. It is obvious that all contributions are integrated over the entire
environment. The camera does not provide a possibility to distinguish between
contributions from discrete heat sources or, in other words, to incorporate view
factors.

Temperature evaluation from the in situ calibration

Contributions from local hot spots, which are particularly present when the target is
surrounded by a discrete distribution of heat sources, for example in multiple-cube
configurations, are disregarded by the global approach. This is highlighted when
the total radiosity received by the thermal sensor at pixel position (2, 7) is expressed
in a functional formulation:

Leam (i, j) = F (Tsur(E, n); Q, i, Treigs Tomb; Fj, Coby: Eneigs Cenvs Tw) ’ (4.7)

where € and 7 are the surface coordinates at the cube faces, Tyu,(€,7) is the local
surface temperature of the target, a is the scan angle, f is the focal length, Tyeig
and Toms are the surface temperatures of the neighbouring sources and the ambient,
F;,; are view factors between the object and other thermal sources, €5,, Eneig and
€eny are surface emissivities and 7, is the transmittance of the used foil. This
expression shows that most of the radiative contributions are location dependent
and an evaluation of the individual contributions is difficult to perform. A possibility
is to define view factors for the local thermal sources but this even complicates the
situation. The relation between the local surface temperature Tyur(€,7) and the
camera reading Izam was determined directly by means of an in situ calibration to
incorporate the location dependence of the contributions involved.

The in situ calibration was performed with a cube of only copper with the same
dimensions as the epoxy cubes (15x15x15 mm). This calibration cube was also
equipped with an electrical heater and painted with the same black paint as used
for the epoxy cubes. The high thermal conductivity of copper led to a uniform tem-
perature, internally as well as at the surface (the tolerances were within 0.05 °C,

=== [PDF page 80] ===
78 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

even for relatively large heat fluxes). The copper temperature was measured with a
thermocouple, embedded in the core region of the cube, and controlled by means of a
feedback loop to the set-temperature, similarly as for the epoxy cubes. Variation of
the copper temperature resulted in different distributions of the radiative emission
from the surface of the five faces of the cube exposed to air flow. These intensity
distributions were determined according to the mapping and the processing pro-
cedures described in the previous section for 9 different surface temperature levels
of the copper cube, equidistantly distributed over the temperature range 35-75 °C.
This resulted in a matrix of 7200 calibration curves, describing the relation between
the camera reading Izam(i,7) and the actual local surface temperature Tyu(&,7) of
the copper cube (the five relevant faces of the cube were mapped in 4 projections,
each consisting of two faces, each face consisting of 30x30 surface cells (or surface
locations), giving a total of 7200 calibration curves). The top face was scanned four
times and the obtained temperature fields were subsequently averaged. Fig. 4.12
shows a series of typical calibration curves for four surface cells situated at the cen-
treline of the top face of the cube. It is noted that the camera reading decreases with
increasing surface temperature. This was due to the inverted intensity conversion
from the received radiosity to the camera reading. The differences in the camera
reading between different locations correspond to temperatures up to approximately
5 °C. These differences would not be accounted for when the infrared system was
calibrated with only one or a few thermocouples embedded in the cube. For the
present case, in which inaccuracies in surface temperatures of maximum 0.5 °C are
tolerated, in situ calibration is a necessity.

For the in situ calibration, the epoxy cubes were substituted by the copper
calibration cubes. It was guaranteed that the experimental conditions during the
in situ calibration and the real experiment were similar. The matrix of calibration
curves, measured in a separate experiment, was then directly applicable to determine
the local surface temperature of the epoxy cubes.

4.2.4 Image degradation in general

Several sources contribute to temperature inaccuracies in infrared images. Temper-
ature noise, which manifests as deviations in the thermal value of individual pixels,
can be minimised by averaging over a sufficiently large number of images. Devia-
tions due to the restricted digitisation resolution of the camera (8-bits converter) is
also mentioned as a possible contribution (it was already noted that the temperature
resolution was at least 0.2 °C’). Further, spatial image degradation is a consequence
of optical diffraction and lens aberrations (for instance the defocussing of the ob-
ject). Spatial averaging over the instantaneous projected area is also classified as
possible cause [141].

Noise

It is beyond the context of this thesis to discuss all the different sources that con-
tribute to the noise present in an infrared system. For this, reference is made to
the detailed analysis given by Hudson and Wordsworth Hudson [67]. To find the

=== [PDF page 81] ===
4.2. Infrared thermography 79

noise characteristics of the present camera, a uniform temperature field kept at dif
ferent temperature levels between 30 °C’ and 80 °C’ was scanned. This temperature
field was created with a black copper plate with a uniform surface emissivity. The
variance of the spatial intensities J’? is plotted versus the mean field intensity Tin
Fig. 4.13. Above the noise floor, the stable plateau at the lower intensities, the rela-
tion between the mean intensity and the variance develops to a more or less linear
profile which illustrates the Poisson-limited behaviour of the infrared camera. The
noise shown in this figure corresponds to temperatures between roughly 0.1-0.2 °C’.

A different way of determining the noise level in the accumulated image is to
consider the standard deviation in the separate pixels and subsequently average it
over all pixels:

(isd) = ard ad T(i,3)) (4.8)

where I(i,7) and /(i,j) are respectively the grey-values of the instantaneous images
and the accumulation of five images at position (i,j). 7, which is the average of all
pixel positions M, is a measure for the noise level of the accumulated image. The
noise-level was calculated for several images for different conditions (i.e. surfaces of
uniform temperature, strong gradients, and so on) and in ail situations, the noise
level corresponded to temperatures of about 0.10-0.12 °C.

Hysteresis

Since a camera with a scanning mechanism with only one thermal sensor was used,
spatial blur could possibly be caused by hysteresis effects: the sensor release time
exceeds the period of two consecutive pixel recordings. The circular aperture of
a large black body was scanned to study possible hysteresis effects. The emitted
radiation of the source is uniform across the entire aperture according to the defi-
nition of a black body [108]. Cross-sectional slices of the camera response are given
in Figs. 4.14 and 4.15 for respectively the horizontal and the vertical direction.
The focal length was M = 0.20 m. The temperature profile is almost uniform in
the centre, while it decays gradually to the ambient temperature at the edges (note
that an increase in camera intensity corresponds to a decrease in temperature). The
rotation-symmetric profiles illustrate that the scan frequency was sufficiently low to
deny hysteresis effects.

Slit response function

When small objects are considered, spatial image degradation becomes critical and a
modified recording (consisting of object and environment contributions) is obtained.
A common method to measure this effect is based on the placement of a variable slit
in front of a large black body. The un-modulated signal is then determined by the
radiation from the black body without the slit in front of it. Radiation which passes
the variable slit will be modulated. The ratio of the distorted to the undistorted
signal is called the modulation (Mod). This modulation is better known as the
slit response function (SRF) when it is plotted versus the slit width c.q. object
