angle in mRad (the object angle is defined as the angle between half the slit width
and the object distance). A typical SRF of the infrared system used [169] is given
in Fig 4.16. A modulation of Mod = 98% is indicated as the dotted line in the
figure. For infinite slit widths, the modulation becomes 1 (100%). A zero slit width
corresponds to a zero modulation.

Usually, the spatial resolution is specified for the slit width that gives 50% modu-
lation [61]. The spatial resolution is the number of resolving pixels (i.e. the amount
of pixel elements in the slit width). The largest acceptable error in the temperature
reading can be expressed in terms of a minimum required modulation level. The
minimum object size can be calculated when the object distance is known (for small
angles, tan(a) = a, and therefore, object angle = object size/object distance).

The SRF, depicted in Fig. 4.16, exhibits a steep gradient for very small object
angles. After a modulation of approximately 70%, the modulation improves slowly
with increasing object angle. Even for rather large object angles, (up to 80 mRad)

=== [PDF page 83] ===
4.2. Infrared thermography 81

the modulation is still less than 99% which implies an unacceptable error in the
temperature reading.

4.2.5 Spatial image degradation

It was argued that the scan angle of 45° required a depth-of-field of at least + 5
mm. Further, a sufficiently large spatial resolution was needed to calculate the tem-
perature distribution in the epoxy layer accurately. The compromise between these
requirements was a focal distance of M = 0.19 m. Because of this focal length,
spatial image degradation was significantly present and needed to be corrected for.
A reconstruction technique based on the Optical Transfer Function (OTF) was pro-
posed to correct for the spatial image degradation. First, an outline of previous
work on the characterisation of spatial image degradation is given. The discussion
proceeds with the formulation of spatial image degradation and the developed model
for the OTF. The measurement of the OTF is discussed in detail. Special attention
is given to the accuracy of the OTF, particularly the depth-offield and the tem-
perature dependence. The measured OTF was used to construct a restoration filter
which was applied to restore the infrared images.

Literature survey

Several studies reported in literature were already devoted to different measurement
techniques to characterise imaging systems with the Optical Transfer Function or
its magnitude, the Modulation Transfer Function (MTF). Mullikin et al. [120]
discussed the characterisation of different CCD cameras using, among others, the
measured spatial frequency responses to a step-edged object in the focal plane of the
camera. Marchywka and Socker [100] investigated a modulation transfer function
technique which is suitable to characterise large-format, small-pixel detectors for the
visible wavelength region. The target image from which they derived the MTF was
a sinusoidal holographic test pattern. Daniels et al. [36] measured shift invariant
MTFs for scanning imaging systems for application in the visible, 3-5m and 8-
12m wavelength bands. Their target images were randomly created pixel patterns
printed on a transparency and placed in front of a uniform radiation source (black-
body). Tzannes and Mooney [168] reported on the characterisation of PtSi infrared
cameras. A knife-edge technique was used to obtain edge spread functions, from
which the two-dimensional MTF was derived. de Luca and Cardone [92] presented
the formulation of a cascade model to predict the MTF of a sampled infrared system.

Bougeard et al. [22] used infrared thermography to evaluate the convective heat
transfer from fin-tube heat exchangers. The technique included an image restora-
tion based on Wiener filtering and the Optical Transfer Function. However, the
performance of the image restoration was only qualitatively shown and no objec-
tive accuracy analysis was provided. Recently, Carlomagno [28] provided a detailed
analysis of the various aspects which contribute to the accuracy degradation of con-
vective heat flux measurements. Among others are the inaccuracies involved when
using infrared thermography to measure surface temperatures of the test objects.
He showed that, in particular for situations with large spatial temperature gradients,

Be

=== [PDF page 84] ===
82 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

both infrared image and heat flux degradation prevent the accurate determination of
heat transfer coefficients. Aliaga et al. [5, 6] used infrared thermography to measure
the surface temperature distributions of multiple wall-mounted ribs in channel flow.
Lorenz et al. [91] recently reported on results for a similar configuration. Although
temperature fields of complex geometries were subject of study, they did not report
on the use of image restoration and in situ calibration (the system was calibrated
with thermocouples, embedded in the surface).

Formulation of spatial image degradation

The unique relation between the original thermal object i(z,y) and the recorded
blurred image m({zx,y), expressed in the spatial domain, is given by the 2D Point
Spread Function (PSF) h(z,y). It indicates the power distribution in the image
plane due to a point source in the object plane [22]:

m(z,y) = A(z, y) * i(z,y) + n(z,y), (4.9)

where + is the convolution operator, x and y are the spatial coordinates and n(x, y)
denotes an additional noise term. The convolution product reduces to an ordinary
product after Fourier transformation:

M (fe, Fy) = H (fe, fy) I( fey fy) + N(fes fy); (4.10)

where H(f,, fy) is the Optical Transfer Function (OTF) in spatial frequencies f,
and f, and M, I and N denote the Fourier transforms of m, i and n, respectively.
The modulus of the complex OTF is the Modulation Transfer Function (MTF).

The OTF describes the degradation process, which is determined by the focal-
length, optics, lenses, temperature range, etc. Due to the small wavelength band-
width of the infrared camera (the spectral response is between 3 and 5 microns)
and the limited temperature range of the studied objects (about 25 °C), the as-
sumption of a temperature-independent optical transfer function is permitted. This
statement was supported by experiments and will be discussed later in this section.
Furthermore, the OTF is believed to be in good approximation spatially invariant
which is required for applying the measured OTF to the entire image field. Close
to the image borders, the imaging system may exhibit invariant behaviour, how-
ever, experiments showed randomly distributed noise in the individual horizontal
and vertical edge-responses across the entire image and no trend in absolute level
was observed. The blurred image can be restored with a restoration filter W(f,, fy)
yielding the restored image I(fo, fy) {80}:

I (fe: fy) = W (fer fy) MU fos fy): (4.11)

Restoration with the inverse filter, being the reciprocal of the OTF i.e. W;(f., fy)
= 1/H(fz, fy), results in unlimited amplification of the response when H(fz, fa)
approaches zero. This undesired amplification of the response can be avoided by
using the Wiener filter W..(fe, fy) [80]:

"(fas fy)
|H (fz, fy)? + (Gnoise/Pimage)

Ww (fe: fy) = (4.12)

=== [PDF page 85] ===
4.2. Infrared thermography 83

where H*(f,, fy) is the complex conjugate of the OTF and ¢noise ANd Pimage are the
power spectral densities of the noise and the image, respectively. The additional
ratio dimage/Pnoise is the signal-to-noise-ratio of the image, which attenuates high
noise components. The Wiener filter becomes the exact inverse filter for noise-free
images, le. noise = 0.

Model for the OTF

The infrared imaging system is characterised by its PSF or OTF. It is often easier
to acquire Line Spread Functions (LSF), from which the OTF can be derived. The
camera response to a perfect thermal step-edge, the Edge Spread Function (ESF)
k,(2x), is the convolution product of the original one-dimensional step-edge u,(x) and
the Line Spread Function (LSF) 1,(z) (noise is omitted to simplify the derivation):

k,(z) = uz (x) * l, (2). (4.13)
A similar expression holds for the response in the vertical scan-direction:

Ky(y) = uy(y) * ly(y), (4.14)

where k,(y) is the ESF, u,(y) is the step-edge and 1,(y) is the LSF. The relations
between the LSF and the PSF are the next integrals:

I2(@) = [ A(z, y)dy, (4.15)
ly(y) = [ Ala, y)de. (4.16)

Differentiation with respect to the dependent coordinate and using convolution prop-
erties result in:

I,(2) = Tn (4.17)
iy) = Se. (4.18)

Fourier transformation yields the Fourier transformed Line Spread Function Lz (fz)
and L,(f,), which are the cross-sections through the centre of the 2D OTF for the
horizontal and vertical scan-direction, respectively. A weighted sum of both Fourier
transformed LSFs gives an interpolation function for H( fs, fy):

A (fe, fy) = & Le(fe) +8 Ly(fy), (4.19)

where @ and @ are interpolation coefficients. For a linear dependency on the rotation
angle of the edge in the first quadrant @, a and / can be selected as a = (90 ~ 4)/90
and 3 = 6/90. This linear approach is verified with measurements of the edge
response for certain rotation angles of the thermal step-edge which will be discussed
in the next section.

Measurement of the OTF

Experimental thermal step. Optical systems are commonly characterised by slits
or knife-edges, where usually a plate at uniform temperature is covered by a thin
strip at a different temperature. The gap between the two materials, since the hot
and the cold plane can not be located in the same plane of view, may result in
non-ideal behaviour of the thermal step-edge (for instance defocussing). Therefore,
a different approach was adapted. An almost perfect thermal step-edge was realized
by an abrupt step in the surface emissivity of a heated copper plate. One half of the
plate was covered with a very thin coating (< 0.01 mm) with emissivity ¢, and the
other half with a thin coating with e2. The temperature of the entire surface of the
test-plate was uniform (within 0.05 °C) because of the high thermal conductivity of
copper and the very small layer-thickness of the coating. The difference in surface
emissivity of the test-plate caused an abrupt change in the emitted radiation, which
resulted in a thermal step-edge, with a hot and a cold part located in the same plane
of view.

The setup was equipped with a controlled heater for adjusting the copper plate
temperature at the desired temperature levels. Rotation of the set-up permitted
measurements of the LSF at different rotation angles (0°, 90°, 180° and 270°) in order
to investigate possible anisotropic behaviour. The setup could also be positioned
at inclination angles of 30°, 45° and 60° to verify the weighted sum approach for
obtaining the 2D OTF. The object-distance could be varied between zero and infinity
to examine out-of-focus and object-distance dependencies of the OTF.

Implementation details. A cross-section of the in-focus step-edge response of the
infrared camera is shown in Fig. 4.17 for a focal length of M@ = 0.19 m, illustrating
the relatively large image degradation. The step function u,(z, y) was located at the
inflection point of the camera response (s-curve). The significant deviation between
the original and the measured image would obviously lead to unacceptable errors in

The step-edge was aligned with the sampling grid within 0.1°. For each individual
scan-line, the derivative of the ESF was approximated with a sample difference filter:

a
dx

where the indices i and j denote the discrete pixel location. The derivative of the
ESF was averaged over all scan lines (200 and 300 for the horizontal and vertical
step edge, respectively), to arrive at the LSI. Misalignment of the edge caused by
a slight translation would obviously lead to broadening of this averaged LSF and,
therefore, the line response was aligned to the centre of the step-edge at sub-pixel
level. The shift was estimated from a least squares fit of a linear function through
the phase data of the Fourier transform. The Fourier transform of a shifted function
l(% —2p) will have a phase component proportional to the frequency, i.e. eI" L(w).
Multiplication of the Fourier transform with the inverse of this phase term resulted
in perfect alignment. The noise level of the source image was low since an average
of 125 images was used.

The Modulation Transfer Function, characterising the horizontal scan-direction
(rotation angle 0 °) is shown in Fig. 4.18 for a focus-length of M = 0.19 m. The
amplitude at zero frequency is scaled at 100. The image size was converted to
256x200 in order to apply the Fast Fourier transformation (FFT). The pixel size
was 0.1724 mm and corresponds to a maximum spatial frequency of 5800.5 1/m.
The smooth appearance of the MTF is attributed to the noise-limited source image
(an average of 200x125 scan-lines). The MTF is peaked in the low frequency region
and decays to zero for the larger spatial frequencies. The notable plateau appeared
to be an artifact of the camera. The narrow-banded appearance was because of

ke (7,7) a ky (4, J) —k,(t—1,3), (4.20)

=== [PDF page 88] ===
86 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

the oversampling of the camera. Less than half of the entire spatial frequency
domain was used and, therefore, the possibility of reducing the bandwidth was
examined. With 2x2 binning, the sampling density was reduced with a factor 2
in both scan-directions, yielding a pixel size of 0.3448 mm which corresponds to a
maximum spatial frequency of 2900.2 1/m. The incorporated averaging resulted in
an improved signal-to-noise-ratio, appearing from the smoother MTF (see Fig. 4.19,
M = 0.19 m). Despite the reduction in sampling density, the MTF still did not
cover the entire spatial frequency range. It is noted that both MTFs for M = 0.19
m (shown in Fig. 4.18 and Fig. 4.19) contain basically the same information, except
for the noise level. A stronger reduction in resolution will obviously lead to aliasing
and, therefore, the 2x2 binning appeared to be the optimum choice for present
application.

In the following discussion, all MTFs were determined for the reduced image
scale except Fig. 4.25 in which the object distance dependency is shown.

Out-of-focus of object. Out-of-focus of the infrared camera was investigated by con-
sidering step-edge responses which were by purpose rendered out-of-focus. There-
fore, the focal-length was varied between 0.05 and 1.00 m while the object-distance
was fixed at O, = 0.19 m. The corresponding MTFs are shown in Fig. 4.19. When
the focal-length of the camera markedly exceeds the object-distance, with infinity
as the limit, the step-response approaches a linear profile with a constant derivative.
Such a smooth profile corresponds to a narrow, spiked MTF. The same holds for the
cases that the focus distance M was smaller than the object distance O,. However,
small deviations are observed for the higher spatial frequencies. All out-of-focus
MTFs are enclosed by the in-focus MTF, at least in the low-frequency range. This
provides a suitable tool to examine whether an image is in-focus or not which is
described in detail in Boddeke et al. [20]. Examination of the behaviour of the
MTF shape for small out-of-focus situations provided knowledge of the depth-of
field of the infrared camera. Fig. 4.20 shows the Modulation Transfer Functions for
a fixed focus-length of Mf = 0.19 m for differences in object-distance of + 5 mm.
The deviations between the different MTFs were small. To examine the involved
temperature effect, a test-image was restored with rotation-symmetric Wiener fil-
ters, constructed from these one-dimensional OTF's. The test-image is a two-faces
projection of the measurement cube, as given in Fig. 4.21. Horizontal cross sections
of the restored test-images are given in Fig. 4.22. The profiles denote intensities but
these are directly related to surface temperatures by means of a calibration. It is
almost impossible to observe differences between the three profiles which indicates
that the discrepancies between the restored images were very small (in the order of
magnitude of 0.1 °C}. This illustrates that the depth-of-field of the infrared camera
is sufficiently large to measure the entire surface temperature distribution of the
cube.

der operational conditions ranged from 40 °C’ to 65 °C’. According to Wien’s dis-
placement law, the maximum power is emitted at corresponding wavelengths be-

through the centre of the restored images are given in Fig. 4.24. These profiles are
also directly related to surface temperatures. The small deviations in the restored
results correspond to temperature differences of about 0.1-0.2 °C’. It is important to
note that the final OTF, used to restore the real infrared images, was determined at
a step-edge temperature of approximately 55 °C. The small temperature differences
in the restored results verify the temperature independence of the OTF.

the angled edge responses with a rotation- and bspline interpolation function to

MTF (Fig. 4.28), were acquired at corresponding rotation angles and presented as

corresponding measurements justify the application of the weighted sum approach.

Restoration filter

With the measured optical transfer function, thoroughly discussed in the previous
sections, the Wiener restoration filter can be constructed. The only unknown which
remains in this filter is the ratio of the power spectral densities of the noise and
the image, noise and dimage, respectively. Although these densities are spatial fre-
quency dependent, it was shown that the variation with spatial frequency was very
small. Therefore, it is sufficiently accurate to assume both spectral densities to be
constant, at least in the range in which the infrared camera was operated. The
Tatio Qnoise/Pimage, better known as the inverse signal-to-noise-ratio of the image
(1/SNR), is also constant for this approach, giving the pseudo-Wiener filter:

= H* (fas fy)
Wu fes fy) _ |H (fe, fy)? + SNR

(4.21)

This filter was used to restore all infrared images (both from the in situ calibration
and real measurements). Based on experimental evidence, a value of 1/SNR = 0.05
was used for all infrared images.

When the SNR is overestimated, the high-frequency noise contributions are
underestimated and, therefore, are amplified by the Wiener filter. This leads to
undesirable amplifications of the restored image. But because relatively high fre-
quencies are incorporated, it is possible to recover some of the sharpness of the
original image which, as discussed, has to be paid for with additional noise. In
contrast, if the SNR is chosen too small, the noise level of the infrared image is
overestimated leading to a severe damping of the noise. The higher frequencies are
then filtered out which leads to smooth restored images. Due to the absence of the
higher spatial frequencies, originally sharp edges are less sharp in the restored image.
These effects are illustrated for a copper cube of size 15 mm which was mounted on
an insulated base-plate. The copper cube was heated similarly as the copper cubes
used for the in situ calibration and controlled to a temperature which was constant
within 0.1 °C. Because of the large thermal conductivity of copper, the (surface)
temperature was uniform within 0.05 °C and therefore, this situation resembles a
perfect thermal block. The object was scanned perpendicularly at M7 = 0.19 m for
which the OTF was determined. A cross-section at the centreline of the unrestored
camera response to the thermal block is shown in Fig. 4.30. The profile is smooth,
there is hardly no noise but the spatial image degradation is obvious. The other
curves refer to image restorations with pseudo-Wiener filters based on different val-
ues of the SNR. Especially when the noise contribution is underestimated, for the
lower 1/SNR values, the restored image is more irregular as appears from the large

=== [PDF page 93] ===
4.2. Infrared thermography 91

fluctuations in the restored profile. However, these restorations are accompanied
by a steeper restored step-edge (image). The restored results with the larger values
of 1/SNR are less irregular but also less sharp at the edges. The somewhat lower
level of the unrestored camera response compared to the restored profiles is only a
matter of scaling. This test-case is a good example of the compromise between sharp
reconstructed gradients and noise-amplification, involved in the Wiener restcration
technique.

Validation of the accuracy

The improvement of the image restoration technique was confirmed with liquid crys-
tal surface temperature measurements. It was guaranteed that the experimental
situation during both thermography measurements were identical to allow a quan-
titative comparison between both results (an identical experimental situation refers
to the power supply, air temperature, air flow, and so on).

Figure 4.31 gives profiles of in situ calibrated infrared surface temperatures along
path ABCD, which is a cross-section perpendicular to the mounting plate at the
symmetry plane (z/H=0). The (Q) denotes a temperature profile for which both
the in situ calibration images and the measurement images were not processed with
the restoration filter. The (©) indicates the infrared temperatures obtained from
the restored calibration and measurement images. It is important to note that
the originally recorded infrared images are the same for both temperature profiles;
the only difference is the application of the image restoration filter. Despite the
use of the in situ calibration, the discrepancies between both temperature profiles
(with and without Wiener filtering) are up 3 °C for certain areas at the surface
of the cube. The previously discussed mixture of five liquid crystals was used to

lution is rather poor, but this is of less importance since validation was the only

In conclusion, the coinciding surface temperatures of the two independent. tech-
niques illustrate, first, the necessity of image restoration for the considered appli-
cation. And second, it provides an overall temperature accuracy of 0.4 °C for the
restored and in situ calibrated infrared measurements. The small discontinuities at
the corners are attributed to the somewhat larger inaccuracy in the surface temper-
ature as caused by the mapping of the different faces.

4.3 Laser Doppler Anemometry

Laser Doppler anemometry (LDA), a well established non-intrusive measurement
technique, is particularly suited for the investigation of the low Reynolds number
flow around small three-dimensional protrusions. However, care must be taken
to eliminate numerous causes of error if the LDA measurements are performed in
small flow passages and close to solid walls. The LDA technique uses laser beams
which intersects in a measuring volume of finite size. The interference of these laser
beams causes a fringe pattern. Particles in the flow (the seeding) which pass this
measuring volume will shift the frequency of the incident laser light (the Doppler
effect). The frequency shift is directly proportional to the velocity of the particles,
and with the assumption of no slip between the flow and the particles, the velocity
of the flow. Detection of the reflected and frequency-shifted laser light (detection
involves also the validation of the signal, meaning that it should satisfy certain
quality criteria) allows the measurement of the instantaneous velocity of the particle
passing the measuring volume. Consecutive velocity realisations give a time series
of the velocity from which, with statistical processing, the first (mean velocity)
and higher order moments (Reynolds stresses etc) can be derived. By traversing
the measuring volume with an accurate traverse mechanism, profiles of the mean
velocity and turbulence fields can be measured in the entire domain.

A detailed discussion of the application and the limitations of Laser Doppler
Anemometry in general are given in, for example, Absil [1], Durst et al. [40] and
Drain [41]. The discussion in this thesis will be focussed on the specifications and
the accuracy of the LDA-system used to acquire velocity data in the flow around
the cubical protrusions. Further, the measurement and the involved accuracy of the
third velocity component is discussed.

=== [PDF page 96] ===
94 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

4.3.1 Specifications of the LDA-system

A two-component back-scatter Laser Doppler Anemometer (TSI 9230/9201) was
used. The geometrical situation allowed us to select an optical focal length of
120.5 mm which involved a good signal-to-noise ratio and a relatively large beam
intersection angle of 0.380 rad. The resulting measuring volume had dimensions
of 0.20 mm in length and 0.04 mm in diameter. Care was taken to properly align
the optics. It was verified by projection on a wall with a micro-objective that both
volumes of the green and the blue pairs of laser beams coincided at least within 95
%.

The measurement of flow reversal was enabled by the selection of a preshift
frequency of 5 MHz which was based on the velocity range of interest. A mixture
of atomised glycerol/water seeded the flow (the particle sizes were between 0.1 and
1 pm). A TSI IFA-750 was used for data acquisition. Typical data rates ranged
between 300-1000 Hz.

Coincidence windows between 50-100 ys were used to resolve the Reynolds shear
stresses uv’, uw’ and vw’. This range of coincidence windows was determined
from the averaged residence time of a particle in the measuring volume and the
micro time-scales in the flow. The residence time was in the order of magnitude of
50 ys, while the micro time scale was estimated from the channel dimension and
the averaged bulk velocity. Further, Reynolds shear stresses were measured with
various coincidence windows for several locations in the flow indicating that the
measurements were invariant for this range of coincidence windows.

4.3.2 Measurement of the third component

The regular orientation of the probe was perpendicular to the channel wall, al-
lowing velocity measurements in the longitudinal (u-component) and spanwise (w-
component) direction of the flow. The flow around a cubical obstruction is highly
three-dimensional and, therefore, an accurate analysis of the flow structures is hardly
possible without knowledge of the velocity components in all three directions. The
easiest way is the make the test facility optically accessible from the sides of the
windtunnel but this was not a realistic option in the present situation. First, the
focal length of the optics needed to be increased with a factor 3-4 for measurements
from the sides. This reduces the signal-to-noise ratio of the LDA signals drastically
but further, it gives taller measuring volumes. The latter was not desirable due to
the large velocity gradients which were present in the flows in question. Second, in
the case of multiple-cube configurations with several rows of cubes (for example, the
matrix flow) the cubes located at the sides of the cube in question will obstruct the
laser beams and thus preventing a LDA measurement. The laser beams, therefore,
needed to enter the set-up from the front, the normal direction y. Two different tech-
niques were developed to measure the third velocity component. The first method
was based on a direct measurement of the normal velocity with a novel prism cube.
In the second method, the normal velocity was reconstructed from different velocity
measurements acquired at different inclination angles of the LDA probe.

=== [PDF page 97] ===
4.8. Laser Doppler Anemometry 95

measuring
volume

FIGURE 4.33: Schematic of the prism-cube to measure the normal velocity component (v).

Prism-cube

An in-house developed measurement cube was used to measure the normal velocity
component directly. The cube consisted of two prisms, one was made of glass and
the other was made of bronze with a mirror at the diagonal plane (the hypothenusa).
A schematic of this prism cube is given in Fig. 4.33 (the refraction of the laser beams
is omitted to simplify the drawing). A photograph of the prism-cube with the laser
beams during operation is given in Fig. 4.34. The mirror at the hypothenusa-plane
reflects the laser beams at an angle of 90° and creates a measuring volume in the
inter-obstacle spacing (in front of the prism cube). For the shown orientation in
Fig. 4.33, a traverse of the laser head (LDA probe) in the normal direction results
in a traverse of the measuring volume in the streamwise (x) direction. Vertical and
horizontal movement of the laser probe equally cause a translation of the measuring
volume in the vertical and normal direction, respectively. The reflected measuring
volume enables the measurement of the spanwise (w) and the normal velocity (v)
components in coincidence mode. A 90° rotation of the whole prism-cube allowed
the measurement of the streamwise (u) and the normal velocity components in the
region adjacent to the sides of the cube. Measurement of v and w in the wake region
was possible with a 180° rotation. The measurement was geometrically constrained
by the size of the cube and the intersection angle. Measurements were only possible
in a region bound by the side faces of the cube.

Rotation of the laser probe

The second method explored was based on a reconstruction of different velocity
measurements for which the optical probe was rotated in the zy-plane. The mea-
sured velocity component, oriented perpendicularly to the optical axis of the probe
consists then partly of a normal and a streamwise component in the global reference
system. A sketch of the measurement situation is given in Fig. 4.35. The inclination
angles of a, 8 and y refer to the optical axis of the laser probe. Rotation of the probe
(or the optical axis) with inclination angle a results in a local reference system in
which the velocity component in the local x-direction is measured directly: uz. The

instantaneous velocity u, can be decomposed according to Reynolds decomposition
into a long-term averaged part U, and a fluctuating part Ur!

Ug = Ug + Uy: (4.22)

This velocity u, can then be expressed in terms of the global velocities u and v,
which are also Reynolds decomposed:

Ug = Ua + ul, =u cos(a) +v sin(a) = (U+u') cos(a) + (G+ v') sin(a). (4.23)

Rotation at a second inclination angle @ gives the velocity ug which is decoraposed
similarly:

The long-term averaged velocities % and 0, defined in the global coordinate system,

It is noted that the system was operated in coincidence mode during the inclined
measurements. Therefore, correlations between the inclined components ug, etc.
and the spanwise velocity w were measured directly in the form of Reynolds shear
stresses uw’, ugw! and ww’.

It is not obvious to select the inclination angles (a, @ and y) to reconstruct
the normal velocity component since the selection is constrained by several aspects.
The reconstruction of the velocity is based on measurements which always involve
experimental uncertainty. Since only a small part of the normal velocity component
is measured for small angles, it is preferable to select a large angle which minimises
the uncertainty involved. Another complication is the refraction of the laser beams
by the window (a window was needed since the configuration of cubes was placed
in a closed wind tunnel, while the Laser probe was located outside the channel).
When the probe is oriented perpendicularly to the window, both laser beams are
refracted in the same way. This gives only a displacement of the measuring volume
which can be seen from the sketch of Absil [1], see Fig. 4.36. The displacement is
then expressed as [40]:

cos(@/2)

Az = 1- ——==——..
(ae)? — sin?(0/2)

(4.35)

The intersection angle, which determines the fringe spacing, is not changed. In the
case that the laser probe is rotated at an angle, the two laser beams in the plane of

=== [PDF page 101] ===
4.3. Laser Doppler Anemometry 99

FIGURE 4.36: Schematic of the refraction of two laser beams which pass a window of finite thickness
d (from Absil, 1996).

rotation then intersect differently with the glass window which consequently results
in differently refracted laser beams. The waists of both laser beams are then shifted
differently which implies that the measuring volume is not located at the waist of
both laser beams anymore. This causes a fringe gradient in the measuring volume
[1, 40]. In other words, a relatively thick window will cause significant misalignment
of the optics which has to be paid with the aforementioned fringe gradient and,
therefore, artificial turbulence. This argument pleads for a small rotation angle
which involves a small difference between the incident angles. A thin sheet has less
stiffness than a thick window. Perturbations induced by the turbulent channel flow
or by flow periodicities (like the vortex shedding in the wake of the cubes) can cause
the window to vibrate. If the amplitude of the induced sheet oscillation is too large,
it may also cause misalignment of the optics and, consequently, artificial turbulence.

The optimum solution consisted of a significantly thin window (100 micron} made
of a plastic sheet, and rotation angles of -35°, 0°, and 35°. It was estimated that
possible sheet oscillations had negligible effects on the accuracy of the measurement
for this situation. For the regions in the vicinity of the front-face-channel-floor
corner and the rear-face-channel-floor corner, the laser beams were blocked by the
cube and, therefore, different sets of inclination angles were selected to overcome
these geometrical restrictions. For the front face region, inclination angles cf -35°,
-20° and 0° were used, for the rear face region, the used angles were 35°, 20° and
0°. A sketch of these angled projections is given in Fig. 4.37.

4.3.3 Experimental accuracy

Absil [1] gave a very detailed and complete analysis of all possible contributions to
the uncertainty and errors involved in LDA measurements. In this thesis, we will
discuss only the most significant errors involved in the experimental results.

The experimental uncertainty will be determined for the velocity measurements
with the LDA probe in the standard orientation, which includes u, w, and also ug,
ug, etc. In the next section, this analysis is extended to estimate the uncertainty
for the normal velocity component (v).

=== [PDF page 102] ===
100 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

Statistical errors

samples which gave a statistical uncertainty of less than 0.02 m/s. This value was
determined from an existing dataset of 1,000,000 realisations from which first and
higher order moments were calculated with different sample sizes, varying between
100 and 1,000,000. It was concluded that the number of 10,000 samples was suffi-
ciently large to obtain statistically independent first and higher order moments.

Errors related to the system

Several different errors contribute to the total experimental uncertainty during the
route from scattered light to the Doppler frequency. An extensive analysis of the
inaccuracies involved is reported in, for instance, Absil [1], Durst et al. [40] and
Drain [41]. The velocity can be written in terms of the Doppler frequency:

=== [PDF page 103] ===
4.8. Laser Doppler Anemometry 101

Several methods exist to determine the intersection angle. Absil [1] examined four
techniques. A distinction between direct and indirect methods is usually made. A
direct method is based on the relation between the Doppler frequency and an «a priori
known velocity field, for example, a rotating disk or a well calibrated velocity field
[1]. The indirect method is based on the measurement of the wavelength and the
intersection angle. The latter is usually measured with a theodolite or the graphic
method. Absil [1] and other researchers concluded that the indirect method with use
of a theodolite provided accurate and reliable calibration intersection angles. This
method was used here. The measurements with a theodolite gave an intersection
angle of 0.383 rad which was accurate within 0.001 rad. This accuracy contributes
to about 0.3% error in the velocity.

The green and the blue laser beams of the used Argon laser had wavelengths of
514.5 and 488 nm, respectively. The uncertainty in the wavelength was about 0.1
nm which did not contribute significantly to the error in the measured velocity.

The accuracy of the measured Doppler frequency is more complicated. As al-
ready pointed out, the error in it consisted of the errors caused by the selected
preshift and the detector frequency. The different contributions to the uncertainty
in the detector frequency are summarised as follows:

- Finite time resolution: The detector frequency fae: is determined from an auto-
correlation function in case of the TSI IFA 750 processor. In the TSI-manual, an
accuracy of 0.05% is claimed which corresponds to 0.003 MHz in the case of a 6
MHz detector signal.

- The bragg-cell oscillator used in the TSI-system operates at 40 MHz with a devi-
ation of about 0.1%. This uncertainty is carried through the circuit of downshift
filters. When a 5 MHz downshift frequency is selected, the additional uncertainty
is + 0.005 Mhz. For a measured signal of 6 MHz, the corresponding Doppler fre-
quency of 1 MHz contains an uncertainty of 0.005 Mhz, which corresponds to an
error in the velocity of approximately 0.5%.

- Although an auto-correlation function is used, high frequency noise can still affect
the determination of the detector frequency. The good quality of the Doppler burst
as encountered in the present study eliminates this error.

Errors caused by misalignment of the optics

- Fringe gradients: out of waist intersections of the laser beams result in a gradually
varying fringe spacing, which is known as a fringe gradient [1, 40]. Particles,
moving with the same velocity (for example in a laminar flow) which pass the
measuring volume at different locations (the measuring volume has a finite size)
will experience a different fringe pattern. This leads to different Doppler frequencies
and thus to different velocities. Time averaging of these velocity time series will
introduce artificial turbulence which is in fact noise. In the case of a turbulent
flow, the physical turbulence level will be significantly corrupted by this effect
which obviously will lead to misinterpretation of the physics. Proper alignment

=== [PDF page 104] ===
102 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

minimises this effect. LDA measurements in a laminar flow of uniform velocity
showed that the turbulence intensities were smaller than 1%, which is sufficiently
low to verify the negligible influence of fringe gradients.

- Fringe chirps: a fringe chirp is also caused by misalignment of the laser beams,
when both waists of the laser beams are located in opposite side of the intersection
point. The resulting fringes are basically parallel, however with varying spacing.
The velocity measurements in the laminar channel flow, see above, verified also the
absence of this misalignment.

Errors caused by bias-effects

- Velocity or sampling bias is the most important among the bias effects. It is the
effect that for a uniformly seeded turbulent flow, periods of high velocity are bene-
fited to periods of lower velocity due to wrong statistical averaging of the velocity
samples. This wrong averaging has the consequence that the calculated velocities
are significantly larger than the real time-averaged velocities. For equidistantly
sampled processes, the arithmetic-averaged results are not hampered by velocity
bias. This is also the case for some other irregular sampling methods as long as
the sampling process is independent on the physical process. Arithmetic averages
of the quasi Poisson-distributed LDA-realisations are indeed hampered by velocity
bias since the detection of a particle is related to the Poisson-distributed particles
in the flow which makes the sampling process dependent on the physical process.

Several detection methods are proposed in the literature to indicate the presence of
velocity bias in the LDA measurements. The interarrival time between consecutive
realisations is a good measure for possible velocity bias. In the case of significant
velocity bias effects, fast particles will pass more frequently the measuring volume
than slower particles will do. Thus, relatively small interarrival times are more
often encountered in the velocity time series. The averaged interarrival time then
decreases with increasing velocity. In the case of an unbiased signal, the relation
between the averaged interarrival time and the velocity is more or less constant.
This detection method was firstly introduced by Adams [2]. An other detection
method uses the Poisson distributions of the particles in the flow. The probability
density function of the interarrival time between consecutive realisations (pdf (At))
can be described as:

pdf (At) = ve’**, (4.39)

where v is the mean sampling rate, and Ad is the interarrival time. Taking the
exponent of relation 4.39 gives a linear relation: In(pdf(At)) = In(v) ~ vAt. De-
viations from the linear profile indicate that bias effects were present.

Numerous velocity bias correction methods have been reported in the literature
which can be divided into two main categories. The first category covers the sam-
pling techniques, which provide a regular sampling of the times series. Examples
include the sample-and-hold method, the saturated processor and the controlled
processor which are all discussed in Edwards [43] and Absil [1]. The methods

=== [PDF page 105] ===
4.8. Laser Doppler Anemometry 103

of other category use weighting factors to obtain a better estimate of the time-
averaged quantities. The weighted mean velocity u and higher order moments u/”
are expressed as the next finite summations, illustrated for the streamwise compo-
nent u:

If a constant weighting factor is selected (w; = 1 for all 7) the arithmetic average

explained in more detail:

- Interarrival time.

large interarrival times occur in the dataset. The above approximation is,
therefore, only valid if the interarrival time is much smaller than the micro
time scales of the studied flow [43]. This requires relatively large averaged data
rates, ie. Tu x v > 10, where Tu is the integral time scale of the flow and v
is the averaged sampling rate [1]. The integral time scale was estimated from
the ratio of a characteristic velocity V and a characteristic length L. Typical
values for the flow around the cubes are: V = 1 m/s and L = 15 mm, the cube
size. This gives an integral time scale of about 0.015 sec. The integration of
the autocorrelation function (see expression 2.28), described in detail in Absil
[1], yielded also integral time scales in the order of magnitude of 0.015 sec.
To satisfy the constraint Tu x v > 10, the averaged sampling rate should
be of the order of magnitude of 500. Since data rates were roughly between
400-1000, some assessment of the interarrival time weighting is needed. This
is discussed in the next section.

=== [PDF page 106] ===
104 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

- Inverse velocity.

The averaged number of particles N passing the measuring volume in a time
period AT can be approximated with N = |V|A,p,AT, in which |V| is the
magnitude of the instantaneous mean velocity vector, assumed constant in the
measuring volume, A, is the cross-sectional area normal to the velocity vector
and pp is the particle density (number of particles per unit volume in the uni-
formly seeded flow). For a constant particle density, N/AT is proportional to
|V|A, and, therefore, 1/|V|A, is a proper weighting factor to correct for ve-
locity bias. For an ellipsoidal measuring volume, the cross-séctional area A, is
not constant but varies with the direction of the instantaneous velocity vector.
The inverse velocity weighting factor then becomes 1/ jw +w? + (dn/d))?v?
in which d,, and d; are the diameter and the length of the measuring volume,
respectively. In the case of a two-dimensional LDA system, the third instan-
taneous velocity component is unknown. When d,,/d; is much smaller than
1, which is usually the case for long measuring volumes (due to a smail inter-
section angle), the term (d,,/d))?v? is negligible compared the other two. In
that case, the weighting factor consists of only the velocity components u; and
w; and is, therefore, called 2D. The measuring volume in our measurements
was rather short and thick, the aspect ratio was d»/d; ~ 0.2. Therefore, the
third velocity component was estimated according to Nakayama [121] who
suggested to take the average of the variances in the z- and z-direction o? =
z(o2 + 02) to approximate the third unknown squared velocity component:

wy = 1/ yu +w? + (dm/d;)?o2. This estimator is referred to as 2D+.

Amplitude bias is related to the fact that the signal-to-noise ratio in the LDA signal
is dependent on the Doppler frequency: high-frequency Doppler bursts have gen-
erally smaller amplitudes than low-frequency Doppler bursts. When the Doppler
signals are of the order of magnitude of the noise level of the system, the bursts
are not recognised and validated by the processor. This causes that low-frequency
bursts are more often validated than high-frequency bursts which consequently
leads to biased quantities. For the present study, this type of bias is of minor
importance due to the good signal-to-noise ratio of the LDA signals as a result of
the small focal length. The effect of amplitude bias is estimated to be less than
0.5% in the velocity measurements.

Fringe bias can be encountered when a minimum number of fringe crossings is
required to be recognised as a valid realisation. Particles which move through the
centre might be benefited to particles crossing the edges of the measuring volume.
When the preshift frequency is at least twice the dominant Doppler frequency, this
effect is negligible [1].

Velocity gradient bias is caused by the finite dimensions of the measuring volume.
When a steep velocity gradient is encountered by the measuring volume, particles
with different velocities will add artificial turbulence to the measurements. Durst
et al. [40] proposed an estimate of the additional mean velocity and variance,

=== [PDF page 107] ===
4.3. Laser Doppler Anemometry 105

assuming a Gaussian detection probability function across the measuring volume
and approximating the velocity distribution by a Taylor expansion: —

_ _  _ Gr, { Puly)

U— Ucenter = 32 ( ay? > (4.42)
oe @, (duly)\’

12 __ 4 f2 = me
U U~ center 16 ( ay 5 (4.43)

where Ucenter is the averaged velocity and U? center iS the Reynolds normal stress
at the centre of the measuring volume. The small measuring volume used in the
present study minimised the effects of velocity gradient bias. The largest occurring
velocity gradients were in the order of magnitude of 2000 1/s. The deviations in
the mean and variance of the velocity components were estimated with the use of
expressions 4.42 and 4.43 and were about 2%.

- The size of the seeding particles is important for accurate LDA measurements.
Particles which are significantly larger or smaller than the fringe spacing in the
measuring volume will not generate detectable Doppler bursts but generally will
contribute only to the background light intensity. Besides this, when the particles
are too large to correctly follow the flow (particle slip) the velocity measurements
are not accurate. This phenomenon is known as particle lag bias. The size of the
particles was Gaussian distributed in the range between 0.1 and 1 micron. This
distribution was determined by Opstelten [128].

4.3.4 Evaluation of the experimental uncertainty

The above overview provides already insight in the significance of the most common
causes of uncertainty involved in LDA measurements. This paragraph supports
that discussion with a quantification of the overall accuracy. The used LDA-system
generated signals with a good signal-to-noise-ratio (SNR) although the system was
operated in the back-scatter mode. This good signal quality was, among others,
caused by the relatively small focal length of the optical system (i.e. 12.05 cm),
the spherical seeding particles which reflected the incident laser light pretty well,
and the good optical quality and the proper alignment of the system. A good
SNR eliminates several sources of inaccuracy related to data acquisition and the
processing of the LDA signals.

A quantitative impression of the accuracy of the used LDA system, including
signal processing, was obtained from measurements in a laminar uniform flow. A
laminar flow was established using smooth channel walls and a sufficiently small
Reynolds number. The resulting velocity measurements had small-banded and sym-
metric probability density functions. The standard deviation was less than 1%. As
already mentioned, such a small turbulence intensity denies the existence of numer-
ous misalignment errors.

For turbulent flows, the error analysis is more complicated. The flow around
the protruding cubes is characterised by different types of flow: separation, reat-
tachment, recirculations, shear flow. Thorough diagnostics of the LDA-signals is,

=== [PDF page 108] ===
106 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

therefore, required which had to be performed in all mentioned types of flow. Ve-
locity bias effects are one of the most important contributions to the experimental
inaccuracy of LDA measurements in a turbulent flow where large velocity fluctua-
tions are typically present. Therefore, this issue deserves special attention.

Variation of the particle density

The influence of the particle density on the distribution of the interarrival time was
examined for the matrix flow at the location 2/H = 1.3, y/H = 0.5 and z/H = 0.5.
It is assumed that the particles were uniformly distributed in the flow. The seeding
density was varied for different experiments. This resulted in variable averaged
interarrival times (6d) which are plotted in Fig. 4.38. The particle density was
largest at run 1, thus giving a small averaged interarrival time (or a high average
sampling rate). The opposite applied for run 13, in which the particle density was
decreased drastically (low average sampling rate). The corresponding probability
density functions of the interarrival time are shown in Fig. 4.39 in log coordinates
(the pdf for run 1 was almost identical to the pdf for run 2 and is, therefore, not
shown). According to the Poisson distribution of the particles in the flow, the shape
of the pdf is linear on log scale. For most of the experiments (runs) shown in
Fig. 4.39, this Poisson distribution is obvious. Deviations from this behaviour are
observed for the experiment with the largest averaged interarrival time, namely run
13. The tail in the pdf for run 1-4 is probably due to an increased noise level.

The pdf for experiment 13 exhibits more or less a concave profile; this deviation
from the linear shape is an indication of velocity bias (see the above discussion).
Particles with a relatively high velocity are more often validated than the slower
particles. This observation leads to the suggestion that velocity bias is related to
the particle density in the flow. Apparently, for a significantly low data rate (as in
run 13) the interarrival time of the particles is not longer Poisson distributed. This
is in contradiction with the findings from Absil [1]. It was ensured that ali LDA
experiments shown in this thesis were performed with data rates between 400-1000
which corresponded to the experiments in the intermediate regime.

It is interesting to note that the slope of the linear part of the histogram
in log coordinates becomes steeper with increasing particle density (or sampling
rate). This is in agreement with expression 4.39 from which the linear relation
In(pdf(At)) = In(v) — vAt was deduced: an increase in the sampling rate causes
indeed a steeper slope.

Multiple burst validation is the effect that long Doppler bursts are more than
once validated. For example, if a burst consists of 100 cycles, and if the validation
criterion is based. on 30 cycles, then theoretically it is possible that one burst is
validated three times. The result is a corrupted velocity time series, which will
obviously give wrong statistics, i.e. first and higher order moments. Multiple-burst
validation can be detected from the probability density function of the interarrival
time. If multiple validation of one burst occurs, the interarrival time between two
consecutive velocity measurements is almost zero. If this happens frequently, this
will give a lob close to zero interarrival times. For the results presented in this

thesis, it was ensured that proper settings of the data acquisition system avoided

around zero interarrival times.

Velocity bias

The probability density functions of the streamwise velocity component are discussed
along a traverse in the normal direction at ¢/H = -0.3 and z/H = 0 for the cube
matrix. This profile starts at the channel floor, just in front of the cube, passing
the turbulent inter-obstacle cavity, the core flow region and ends at the opposing
wall. Pdfs of the streamwise velocity are given in Fig. 4.40 for 25 stations along the
traverse. The stations y/H are indicated in the figure and correspond to the profile
of the averaged velocity given in Fig. 4.41 (the figure shows the comparison between
different weighting methods which is discussed later). The pdfs are based on 10000
data points; the velocity in each of the 25 velocity bins is based on approximately
400 data points. The (*)-markers refer to the mean interarrival time, normalised
with the maximum averaged value found, as a function of the velocity of the particles
passing the measuring volume.

Fig. 4.40 shows that the pdfs in the core region of the flow (Fig. 4.40e-h) are
Gaussian shaped. For these stations, the interarrival time of the particles is more
or less constant or, in other words, independent of the velocity of the particles. A
negative slope in the interarrival time is found for the pdfs at the stations close to
the opposing channel wall (Fig. 4.40a-d). This decrease towards the higher velocities
indicates that faster particles pass more often the measuring volume than the slower
particles do. A similar observation is made for the pdfs in the inter-obstacle cavity
(Fig. 4.40m-t) and close to the channel floor (Fig. 4.40u-y).

We notice that the turbulence intensity, in terms of the Reynolds normal stresses
u”, is rather small in the core region for the corresponding station at «/H = -0.3
and z/H = 0 (see Fig. 4.42, this figure also shows the comparison between different
weighting methods but the profiles are qualitatively similar), while it increases to-
wards both channel walls with a pronounced maximum at y/H = 1/3. The rather
low turbulence level in the core region explains the constant relation between the
mean interarrival time and the velocity in the flow (see Fig. 4.40e-h). The velocity
fluctuations are moderate and, thus, bias effects caused by fast alternating velocity
differences are less pronounced. The opposite is found for the wall regions. The
relatively large velocity fluctuations at these locations in the flow, causing the ob-
served high levels of turbulence, give rise to differences between periods of low and
high velocities and thus to the occurrence of velocity bias.

Bias effects become even more pronounced for areas were the averaged velocity
approaches zero. The velocity distributions become more or less bimodal since
particles hardly pass the measuring volume during periods when the instantaneous
velocity approaches zero. This is obvious from the last two pdfs in Fig. 4.40, namely
x and y, for which the averaged velocity is close to zero. The mean interarrival
time decays towards the higher (absolute) velocities, causing the mountain-type of

relation. It is important to note that this bimodal behaviour was not caused by
possible coherent structures in the flow, for example the quasi-periodic oscillations
in the flow as caused by the vortex shedding from the lateral faces of the upstream
cube. Power density spectra, calculated from the velocity time series, did not exhibit
an intensification at the predominant frequency of vortex shedding. It is, therefore,
concluded that the bimodal behaviour was caused by velocity bias effects.

The mean interarrival time of the measurements at the discussed stations, at 2/H
= -0.3 and z/H = 0 given in Fig. 4.43, shows an increase towards the channel floor
(y/H =0). To explain this shape, we need to consider the velocity fluctuations in the
spanwise direction as well. These are represented as the Reynolds normal stresses
v® in Fig. 4.44. While the mean spanwise velocity 3 is zero in the symmetry plane
z/H =0, the fluctuations increase significantly in the vicinity of the cube wake. The
corresponding high turbulence level causes biased velocity histograms (they become
bimodal in the worst case), and a strong reduction in the sampling rate.

Both the inverse velocity and the interarrival time weighting were assessed to
obtain a suitable quantification of the error made when no correction is applied.
Although this analysis was performed for several locations, the measurements along

=== [PDF page 113] ===
4.8. Laser Doppler Anemometry 111

the traverse in the normal direction at «/H = -0.3 and z/H = 0 (matrix flow)
are used. The different correction methods are compared for the mean velocity U
and the Reynolds normal stresses u® and v”, given in Figs. 4.41, 4.42 and 4.44,
respectively. For this profile, the average sampling rate ranged between 500 in the
core region and 150-200 in the vicinity of the cubes. The arithmetic average (in
which the weighting factor is unity for all realisations!) is indicated as (+). The
results obtained with the three considered interarrival time corrections, At; = tii1
- t;, At; = 0.5 (tig: - ti) and At; = t; - ti-1, are indicated with (itl, *), (it2, 0)
and (it3, x), respectively. The results from the three interarrival time weighting
schemes differed less than 0.5% from each other. In the case of sufficiently small
interarrival times, or large data rates, no differences in discretisation are expected.
Therefore, we limit the discussion only to the sample-and-hold estimator (At; =
tiz1 - t;). The inverse velocity corrections, 2D and 2D-+, are also indicated in the
Figs. 4.41, 4.42 and 4.44.

An examination of the figures learns that the differences between the corrected
results (for all the correction methods studied) and those obtained from arithmetic
averaging are negligible in the core region. This is expected since in this region no
significant bias effects were detected from the probability density functions and the
plots of the averaged interarrival time (Fig. 4.40). Differences between the corrected
and the uncorrected results become distinguishable in the region close to the oppos-
ing channel wall and in the vicinity of the cube, the inter-obstacle wake (y/H < 2),
where the turbulence level significantly increased. This is also according to the obser-
vations made from the velocity histograms. The pronounced differences between the
interarrival time correction (either one of the three discretisations shown) and the
inverse velocity weighting are most likely due to the somewhat low data rate in these
regions to allow the application of the interarrival time correction (Tu x v > 10).
Because of the relatively small aspect ratio of the measuring volume, the effect of
the third velocity component on the particle distribution in the measuring volume
is not negligible, and its contribution to the velocity vector should not be under-
estimated. The 2D+ weighting method differed, therefore, markedly from the 2D
approach. This effect becomes more pronounced in the turbulent region, where also
large instantaneous normal velocities were found.

From the aforementioned analysis is can be concluded that severe velocity bias
effects are most likely to occur at locations were the turbulence intensities are signifi-
cantly large (Tu > 5%). To support this conclusion, the velocity field was calculated
from the LDA measurements with the inverse velocity bias weighting method 2D+
and without weighting (from an arithmetic average). This analysis was performed
for the flow over two cubes in the off-axis tandem configuration (the streamwise
distance was S,/H = 4, the spanwise distance was S,/H = 1) in a fully developed
channel flow at a Reynolds number of Rey = 3900 (see chapter 7). The plane con-
sidered is at half the cube height (y/H = 0.5) and contains the streamwise (u) and
spanwise (w) velocity components. The difference between both averaging methods
(2D+ and arithmetic) is expressed in the velocity difference and scaled with the

=== [PDF page 114] ===
112 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

magnitude of the velocity vector in that plane:

Au = (uep4 — un) / fur, + wr, (4.44)
Aw = (wap — wy) / uh + wn, (4.45)

where the subscripts 2D+ and N refer to the inverse velocity and arithmetic av-
eraging, respectively. The arithmetic averaged velocity vector is selected for the
normalisation (note that this is an arbitrary selection). These velocity deficits are
interpreted as a vector from which the magnitude M is calculated:

M = VAw + Aw. (4.46)

An iso-value plot of M is given in the upper plot of Fig. 4.45. The corresponding
plot of the turbulent kinetic energy k, based on the two measured Reynolds normal
stresses (k = 0.75 (uw? + w”) ) is given in the lower plot of Fig. 4.45. From the
comparison between both plots we learn that the largest differences between the
weighted and the unweighted velocities (large values of M) occurred at locations
where the turbulent fluctuations (turbulence intensities) were large. Note that the
scaling factor explodes for zero velocity vectors which is the case in the reattachment
region.

Experimental accuracy of the normal velocity component

The two methods to measure the normal velocity component introduce additional
experimental error. This is highlighted in the next paragraphs.

Prism-cube. The prism-cube was constructed from two prisms which were glued
together at the hypothenusa. This interface, oriented at an angle of 45°, reflected
the incident laser beams at an angle of 90°. When the glass prism, through which
both laser beams passed, is a perfect perpendicular triangle (i.e. the two side faces
of the prism are exactly perpendicular to each other) the paths of both laser beams
through the glass are equal. However, a small deviation in this 90° angle will
cause that the laser beams have slightly different path lengths while passing through
the glass. Since the laser beams are then refracted differently, the waist of the
individual laser beams will be shifted differently. The resulting misalignment of the
measuring volume may cause fringe gradients which attribute to artificial turbulence
[1]. The glass-prism with its mirror at the hypothenusa, a deposited metal sheet, was
precisely manufactured. The angle and parallel tolerances were better than 0.01°
and 0.01 mm, respectively. Expression 4.35 was used to estimate the displacement
difference between both shifted waists. If the path length difference of both laser
beams through the glass is estimated to be at maximum 0.01 mm, a shift of less than
3.6 ym exists if the angle of incidence was 10°. This error is, therefore, estimated
to be less than 0.1%.

Further, the glass prism was carefully attached to the bronze prism to create
the prism-cube. Differences in the glue layer thickness may cause that the interface

measurements. The good agreement between the results (even at locations of large
velocity gradients) illustrates further the good reproducibility of the measurements.

Reconstruction from the inclined measurements. It was already mentioned that the
inclination angles were selected according to certain criteria like the refraction of the
laser beams, the optical accessibility of the measurement domain, etc. The method
is based upon the principle that a part of the normal velocity is measured when the
optical laser probe is used at an inclination angle. For the angles involved (sets of
35°, 0°, -35° and 35°, 20° and 0° and -35°, -20° and 0°) only a small part of this
normal velocity can be measured. The reconstruction of the u and v component is
based on the subtraction of two velocity components, each scaled with a coefficient
which depends on the inclination angles used (see equation 4.26). When the sub-
tracted values are of the same order of magnitude, the difference will be small and,
therefore, the reconstruction is very sensitive to experimental error.

The experimental uncertainty in the values of U,., Ua, ua, ug and u2, was
obtained from the foregoing analysis. Because a very thin sheet was used through
which the inclined laser beams entered the wind tunnel section, the measuring vol-
ume was still well aligned. Misalignment of the measuring volume, therefore, does
not contribute to additional error in the inclined measurements. An attempt was
made to evaluate the experimental uncertainty for both the long-term mean veloc-
ities and the Reynolds stresses from a single sample uncertainty analysis. But the
sinus functions in the coefficients in equations 4.26 and 4.34 gave complex derivatives
making this analysis hard to perform. To estimate the sensitivity of the coefficients,
a different approach was adapted. The equations were implemented in a spread
sheet program and by systematic variation of the coefficients (or, to precise, the
angles a, 6 and +y) the sensitivity of the reconstruction was evaluated.

For the selected angle combinations, an experimental uncertainty of about 10%

An important contribution to experimental uncertainty is the repeatability of
the positioning of the measuring volume. To obtain one data set of two velocity
components and three Reynolds stresses, three measurements at the same location
were required. In particular, when the measurements are performed in regions with
steep velocity gradients, a bad reproducibility of the positioning of the measuring
volume will lead to large experimental error and consequently, will lead to the fail-
ure of the velocity reconstruction technique. For different inclination angles, the
measuring volume was aligned with a reference position using a binocular. This
procedure ensured a reproducibility of 0.1 mm which was sufficiently small with
respect to the velocity gradients in the flow.

The reconstructed streamwise velocity 7 and the Reynolds normal stress u?
were compared with direct measurements with the laser probe in the regular (per-
pendicular) orientation to obtain a quantifiable validation of the accuracy. Since
a relatively large part of the streamwise component u is measured with the used
inclination angles, the agreement between both results should be good. This can be
seen in Fig. 4.47 in which profiles in the y-direction of @ and wu? are compared at
x/H = 1.7 in the symmetry plane for the spatially periodic matrix flow (see chapter
8). The reconstructed streamwise velocity and Reynolds normal stresses (indicated
with (A) in Fig. 4.47) are in good agreement with the measurements performed
with the laser probe in the regular orientation (denoted as (0) in Fig. 4.47).

The reconstructed normal velocities 7 and Reynolds normal stresses v? were
compared with velocity measurements acquired with the prism-cube. This com-

z/H = 0, again for the spatially periodic matrix flow. The reconstructed results
are denoted as (A), the measurements obtained with the prism-cube are indicated
with (0). The velocities of both techniques compare very well which support the
conclusion that the reconstructed normal velocities are accurate within 10%. While
the Reynolds normal stresses measured with the prism-cube collapse to a smooth
profile, the values of v® obtained from the reconstruction are scattered around the
