4.3.5 Accuracy values for the experimental results

All the LDA measurements presented in this thesis were processed with velocity
bias weighting methods to eliminate, or at least to minimise, the error introduced
by velocity bias. For the regions in the flow without severe velocity bias effects, for
example the core flow in the channel corridor above the cubes, the correction did not
effect the results, as was demonstrated in the foregoing paragraphs. In the cases of
large turbulence intensities (or velocity bias effects), marked differences between the
weighted and unweighted long-term averaged velocities and higher order moments
were observed. Most of the velocity data were processed with the inverse velocity
bias method 2D+. In some cases, the interarrival time weighting was used. For
these cases, it was demonstrated that no marked differences existed between the in-
verse velocity and the interarrival time weighting due to the fact that the sampling
frequencies were significantly large to satisfy the criterion Tu x v > 10 (Tu is the
integral time scale and v is the averaged sampling rate). Based on the evaluation
of all possible experimental uncertainties involved, the long-term averaged veloci-
ties presented in this thesis are accurate within 5%. The Reynolds stresses were
estimated to be accurate within about 7% while a value of 10% pertained to the
higher order moments (skewness and kurtosis factors). These values apply also for
the normal velocities measured with the prism-cube. The measurements of 0 and
v® obtained with the reconstruction technique were accurate within 10% and 20%,
respectively.

=== [PDF page 119] ===
4.4. Flow visualisations 117

4.4 Flow visualisations

4.4.1 Oil-film visualisations

The oil-film visualisation technique is a widely used measurement technique to qual-
itatively visualise the time-averaged flow pattern just above the surface of interest
(the near-wall velocity vector). The technique is primarily based on the accumu-
lation and the removal of tracer particles, present at the surface. The transport
of these tracer particles and the resulting streak lines on the surface are directly
proportional to the surface stress vector, which must be parallel to the near-wall
velocity vector. This condition is needed for the successful application of this tech-
nique [68]. Particularly for flows over wall-mounted obstacles, this technique is very
useful because of the different flow regimes encountered in these kind of flows. Flow
reattachment and separation cause significant differences in the surface shear stress
and consequently cause clear pigment patterns. This is illustrated from the numer-
ous papers in which oil-film visualisations of the flow over wall-mounted prismatic
obstacles are discussed [68, 101, 102, 86]. But also for other configurations, the
oil-film technique appeared to provide suitable visualisations of the surface pattern.
The flow features around a square cylinder in the vicinity of a channel wall in a
turbulent flow was experimentally studied by Wu and Martinuzzi [177, 105]. In
addition to LDA measurements, they performed oil-film visualisations to document
different types of flow as a function of the distance between the suspended cylinder
and the channel wall. Ruderich and Fernholz [143] studied the flow characteristics
of a bluff plate with a splitter plate located in the symmetry plane of the bluff
plate (the configuration was in fact a T-junction with a long tail). The bluff plate
caused the flow to separate where after it reattached on the splitter plate. Oil-film
visualisations on both the splitter plate and the side wall were used to derive flow
details.

The working principle is as follows. The surface was brushed with a tracer mix-
ture which was composed of jet fuel, low transmission oil and graphite (copier toner).
The low transmission oil was added to make the mixture viscous for embedding the
graphite tracers. The jet fuel made the mixture more liquid. The mixture compo-
sition was chosen in a way, by means of trial and error, that it was viscous enough
to prevent surface pattern diffusion due to turbulent fluctuations in the near wall
velocity field. Further, it was guaranteed that the mixture was still liquid enough to
allow the particles to follow the near-wall velocity field. The typical duration of an
oil-film visualisation experiment was about 15-20 minutes. In that period, the jet
fuel was sufficiently evaporated in a sense that the graphite patterns were attached
to the surface.

Although the oil-film visualisation technique provides information only of the
near wall flow features, careful topology analysis allows to extend these results to
obtain a qualitative description of the macroscopic flow features away from the wall.
Due to the fine detail possible with the technique, it is possible to identify small
structures which could be difficult to detect with other methods. In addition to
providing quantitative information of the location of separation and reattachment
lines, this method could also serve to guide anemometric studies.

=== [PDF page 120] ===
118 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

4.4.2 Smoke visualisations

Smoke visualisations, obtained from smoke injection and illumination, give instanta-
neous records of the flow field. The smoke particles were illuminated in a thin sheet
of light, created with a coherent laser beam and a rotating mirror (polygon). The
smoke was injected in the flow with either a smoke tube or with a hot wire method.
For more background information on smoke visualisation methods, we refer to the
literature [11, 119]. It was verified for both methods that the smoke injection did
not influence the flow field. Images of the smoke traces were recorded on video tape
with a CCD-camera. Separate frames were selected from the movie and digitised
with a frame grabber. Good contrast in the images was obtained for low Reynolds
numbers (1000-2000). Due to turbulent mixing, the smoke traces vanished in the
turbulent field for the larger Reynolds numbers and, therefore, no distinguishable
flow pattern could be observed from the separate frames. But in that case, the
recorded movie revealed sufficient details and information.

Further, a stroboscope was used to freeze the flow structure in time. Predominant
frequencies in the flow, for example the vortex shedding from the lateral faces of the
cube, could be visualised with this method. In fact, it is a conditional sampling of
the velocity field,

4.5 Structure identification with topology principles

The pattern of pigment accumulation or removal in the oil-film visualisation can
serve to identify critical points, where the surface stress and the velocity vectors
identically vanish, or critical lines along which the surface shear stress component
normal to the line is zero. There are two classes of critical points [68, 134]. Nodes, or
nodal points, are isolated singularities through which an infinite number of surface-
stress trajectories pass either radially (simple nodes) or as high vorticity confocal
spirals (foci). Reattachment (stagnation) and separation points correspond to di-
vergent and convergent flow patterns, respectively. Typical examples are shown in
Figs. 4.49a and b. Saddle points are singularities through which only two critical
lines cross. The flow converges along one line and diverges along the other, see
Fig. 4.49c, (the diverging critical line is often rich in pigment). Establishing the
physical significance of saddle points depends on a proper interpretation of the local
flow.

For a general flow field, the difference between the total sum of nodes, > N, and

saddles, 5° S, satisfies:
YON -S°S =2- 2g, (4.47)

where g is the genus or connectivity of the surface. The flow at the channel walls
and at the surface of the wall-mounted cubes is topologically equivalent to a sphere
(g=0) with two additional nodal points (channel inlet and outlet) so that 2—2g=0.
Hunt et al. [68] extended the topological principle to the analysis of the velocity
field in planes normal to walls. It was shown that, for the symmetry plane z/H=0:

(ON+5DN)- (lst 5Us)=0 (4.48)

=== [PDF page 121] ===
4.5. Structure identification with topology principles 119

ta

(a) (b) (c)

FIGURE 4.49: Types of topological critical points: Nodal points (N): (a) attachment, (b) separation
and (c) Saddle point (S).

where N’ and S’ are half nodes and saddles, respectively, occurring at the solid
boundaries.

The three-dimensional mean flow structure was inferred from these critical points
and lines at the surface of both the obstacle and the channel wall, together with Laser
Doppler Anemometry measurements and smoke visualisations of the flow structure.

Some other structure identification methods are shortly mentioned. Usually
velocity vectors and streamlines are examined to study coherent structures in the
flow but the substantial computation time is a major drawback of this approach,
especially for complex flows. It is therefore beneficial to introduce scalar quantities
which represent vortical motion. Robinson [138] introduced the concept of using
low-pressure regions in the flow as possible regions with vortex cores. Sandham
and Kleiser [144] confirmed that this method represented coherent structures in a
channel flow. The modulus of the vorticity is also often used to identify coherent
structures in the flow. Joeng and Hussain [81] applied this method successfully to
free shear flows. Chong et al. [32, 135] employed a method based on critical points
in the flow to derive the flow pattern in the entire flow domain. The flow pattern was
defined by the eigenvectors and eigenvalues of the velocity gradient tensor which were
evaluated at the critical points. de Souza et al. [156] applied three coherent structure
identification techniques to measurements obtained in the near wake of a circular
cylinder suspended in a turbulent boundary layer flow. One of the three techniques
was based on the principle that the large-scale structures are associated with sudden
variations in the velocity. The other two methods were filtering techniques, based on
the spectral and on the time-frequency (wavelet) decomposition of the instantaneous
velocities. The method of Joeng and Hussain [81] was extended by Michard et
al. [107] to detect coherent structures from PIV measurements and DNS data of
turbulent rotating flows.

=== [PDF page 122] ===
120 Chapter 4. Diagnostic measurement techniques

FIGURE 4.50: Sketch of the pressure-cube to measure the local static surface pressure at the five
faces of the cube.

4.6 Static pressure measurements

Static pressure measurements were performed at the channel floor and at the five
faces of the cube exposed to air flow. In a separate experiment, the channel floor was
equipped with small pressure taps. Turbulent fluctuations damped out completely
and the technique is, therefore, only suited to measure long-term averages of the
static pressure. A special cube was designed to measure the static pressure at the
cube faces. It consisted of five separate chambers, one for each face exposed to the
flow, with 9 pressure taps at each face. These pressure taps were located at the face
in an equidistant arrangement. A schematic of this cube with pressure taps is given
in Fig. 4.50. The cube allowed the measurement of the face-averaged static pressure
when all the taps are open. By taping 8 of the 9 holes, disconnecting 8 pressure taps
from the underlying chamber, a local static pressure measurement was performed.
The static pressure was represented in the pressure coefficient, defined as:

Ps — Pr
C, = 4.4
° 5pU2, (4.49)

where p, and p, are the surface static pressure and the reference pressure, U,, is the
reference velocity and p is the fluid density.

CHAPTER 5

Single cube

The single cube is the most basic model considered. The cube was mounted on the
channel floor in the closed wind tunnel. The transition from a laminar to a turbulent
boundary layer flow was forced by a tripping strip which was placed on the channel
floor about 75 cm upstream of the cube. It was experimentally determined that
the flow was effectively tripped for bulk velocities ug > 2.5 m/s (which corresponds
to Rey = 2500). Therefore, the flow structure around the single cube was studied
in the Reynolds number range of 2500 < Rey < 5000. The LDA-analysis was
conducted at a bulk velocity of 4.47 m/s which corresponds to a Reynolds number
of Rey = 4440. Complementary flow information was obtained from visualisation

b=
VPLLLESSALLSL ALESIS SLDLSSUPPLLSEL LPL LELLSSEDSPL LY

FIGURE 5.1: A side view and three-dimensional view of the wall-mounted cube in the channel flow.

experiments (smoke and oil-film visualisations). Differences in the flow field for the
different Reynolds numbers appeared to be insignificant. Therefore, the presented
flow field analysis applied to the Reynolds number range of about 2500 < Rey <
5000.

5.1 Oncoming flow conditions

The tripping of the flow resulted in a developing turbulent boundary layer on the
channel floor and a developing laminar boundary layer at the opposing wall. A
typical velocity profile at the centreline of the channel at z/H = 6.7 is given in
Fig. 5.2. Due to the growing boundary layer in the downstream direction, the
velocity deficit in the boundary layer resulted in an acceleration of the displaced
flow in the core region (due to the incompressibility of the flow). The resulting
velocity gradient in the core region was determined to be dU,./dz = 0.67 1 /s, see
Fig. 5.3.

In order to compare the velocity profiles with literature data from Erm et al.
[44] the momentum thickness @ was evaluated. This thickness was used to define

5.2. Vortex shedding past the cube 125

A consistency check was conducted with conservation of mass at the five subse-
quent stations. The axial mass flow rate was determined from:

D
My = paz | ady, (5.1)

where D is the channel height and ¢ is the fluid density. The mass flow was obtained
from a numerical integration of the individual profiles which yielded an averaged
mass flow rate of 0.262 kg/s per unit area (dz). The averaged bulk velocity, derived
from the ratio of the averaged mass flow rate and the integration area, was 4.47
m/s. The discrepancies between the calculated bulk velocities for the five profiles
were within 1%. This result confirms conservation of mass and illustrates the good
quality of the LDA measurements.

5.2 Vortex shedding past the cube

The vortex shedding from the side faces of the cubes caused the wake flow to fluc-
tuate periodically. The coherent structures were detectable from all three velocity
components. But because of the strong spanwise coupling of the shed vortices, the
velocity amplitude of the periodic motion of the spanwise velocity w was the largest.
Maximum amplification of the periodic motion was observed for locations around
t/H = 2, y/H = 0.5 and z/H = 0.5, the oscillations were attenuated for larger
streamwise locations. The power density spectrum was then calculated from the
measured velocity time series. Long-term averaged spectra were obtained from time
durations of 500,000 data points which corresponded to about 20,000-40,000 vortex
shedding cycles. This number of realizations was required to ensure a sufficiently
large signal-to-noise ratio. A slotting, sample-and-hold and re-sampling method
were compared to confirm the accuracy of the calculated power density spectra and
of the obtained wake frequencies. Details of these methods can be found in Ab-
sil [1] and Tummers and Passchier [167]. The wake frequencies obtained from the
calculated spectra were within 0.5 Hz. This value pertains also to the uncertainty
of estimating the frequencies from the spectra and to the experimental uncertainty
involved. A typical example of a spectral power density function is given in
Fig. 5.5 for a station at «/H = 2.7, y/H = 0.5 and z/H = 0.5. The peak in the
spectrum around f = 33 Hz is associated with the passage of shed vortices. The
coherent structures in the flow carry a substantial part of the turbulent kinetic en-
ergy which appears from the observed peak. The dashed line, also indicated in
Fig. 5.5, is related to the incoherent structures (stochastic motion) in the flow. It is
observed that the spectral energy level of the periodic component is approximately
three times larger than that of the incoherent structures. This separation between
organised and disorganised structures in the flow can be elucidated by writing the
periodically fluctuating velocities, analogously to Reynolds decomposition, as a su-
perposition of a long-term average @, a turbulent or random fluctuating part ui, and
the periodic mean component uy, (triple decomposition):

u=Utu,t+u,. (5.2)

FIGuRE 5.6: Autocorrelation function of the w-velocity measured at the location «/H = 2.7, y/H

The coherent component can then be considered as the sum of the long-term average
and the periodic mean component. It can also be considered as a phase average.
This decomposition is commonly used to separate the coherent contribution from
the incoherent part, for example, by Balachandar et al. (14).

Since a highly turbulent flow was considered, vortex shedding was not activated
at one distinguishable frequency but it occurred at a broader range of frequencies.
The dominant characteristic frequency was derived from the frequency in the spec-
trum which corresponded to the maximum energy level. The peak in the spectrum
given in Fig. 5.5 is related to a wake frequency of 33.2 Hz. The corresponding
Strouhal number St = f H/ug, based on the bulk velocity and the cube height, was
St = 0.093. The periodical behaviour of the spanwise velocity can also be observed
from the corresponding autocorrelation function, given in Fig. 5.6, which is based
on the same dataset of 500,000 points. The autocorrelation function consists of an

=== [PDF page 129] ===
5.2. Vortex shedding past the cube 127

incoherent or random part and a coherent part. The random part with the value
1 for time delays of 0 sec, the origin, and with a gradual decay to 0 for large time
delays, is indicative for turbulent flows. The coherent part caused the sinusoidal
shape of the autocorrelation function around the a-axis. Integral time scales Tu
were derived from the time integration of the autocorrelation function (expression
2.28). Typical absolute values were of the order of magnitude of 12-13 msec; values
from a true integration, with nullifying positive and negative contributions, were
about 7 msec. The ratio of the characteristic length (the cube height H = 15 mm)
and the characteristic velocity (about 1 m/s) gave also a value of about 15 msec for
the integral time scale.

From Taylor expansion, the discretized second time derivative at t=0 was ob-
tained to estimate the Taylor micro scale Ar:

At?
2(p(0) — p(1))’

where p(0) and p(1) are the values of the autocorrelation at respectively ¢ = 0 and
t = At sec. For the isolated wall-mounted cube, this value was in the order of
magnitude of 2.5 msec.

Wake frequencies were measured for a range of mass flow rates. The results are
plotted versus the bulk velocity in Fig. 5.7. All shown frequencies were determined
from 500,000 data points. The relationship is clearly linear which indicate that the
results are independent of the Reynolds number. This was expected since the con-
sidered range of Reynolds numbers covered less than one decade. The somewhat
larger discrepancies from the linear regression for the lower bulk velocities are prob-
ably due to the somewhat larger experimental uncertainty. The Strouhal number of
St = 0.095 was determined from the slope of the linear regression and was accurate
within 5%.

50 mm which was mounted on a flat plate in a free turbulent boundary layer flow.
Only for these cases, the wake frequencies were determined from hot-wire velocity
measurements. Further, a single cube in a fully developed and a developing channel
flow were considered. The Strouhal numbers for these cases are plotted in Fig. 5.8
as a function of the Reynolds number Res = 5U,.;/v, based on the boundary layer
thickness (6) and a reference velocity (U,.7). It is seen that the values are scattered
around a mean value of about St = 0.10. The deviations are approximately 15 %
and the Strouhal numbers do not fully collapse on a single curve. Further, these
values differ significantly from St = 0.145 as measured by Martinuzzi [101] in the
wake flow of a single cube in a fully developed channel flow at Rey = 40,000 and a
channel-height-to-cube-height ratio of 2. It is believed that the observed differences
between the present results as well as compared to the value of St = 0.145 were
caused by different levels of the free stream turbulence which was significantly higher
in the fully developed high Reynolds number case of Martinuzzi. Another reason
may be the difference in aspect ratio which impose different blockage effects (the
channel-height-to-cube-height ratios are different).

5.3 ‘Time-averaged flow field

The sketch given in Fig. 2.1 in chapter 2 is representative for the flow over the iso-
lated wall-mounted cube in the developing turbulent channel flow. In the following
discussion of the relevant flow details, reference is also made to that sketch.

5.3.1 Front of the obstacle

The flow in the front region of the cube is primarily dominated by the horseshoe
vortex. The trace of this vortex in the upstream stagnation region along the centre
plane (z/H = 0) is readily identified in the smoke visualisation patterns, shown in

=== [PDF page 131] ===
5.3. Time-averaged flow field 129

Fig. 5.9. The figure gives an instantaneous image for Rey = 1000, flow is from
right to left. The smoke, injected upstream, is convected smoothly in the vortex
core of the primary vortex. Smoke visualisation experiments, performed at higher
Reynolds numbers, revealed similar observations but the smoke traces were more
distorted. Although the smoke traces were not distinguishable anymore from single
shots, the recorded movie clearly showed the paths of the smoke particles, Since
no significant differences were observed between the experiments for low and high
Reynolds numbers, it is concluded that the flow visualisations given in this thesis
are representative for the Reynolds number range of 2500 < Rey < 5000.

FIGURE 5.9: Instantaneous smoke visualisation of the fow pattern in the symmetry plane z/H =
0. Flow is from right to left.

The surface imprint of the horseshoe vortex is observed from the oil-film visu-
alisation given in Fig. 5.10. This flow visualisation was obtained for Rey = 8000.
For the oil-film visualisations also, it is noted that experiments at lower and higher
Reynolds numbers revealed similar oil pattern. It is, therefore, concluded that the
shown oil-film results are representative for the Reynolds number range of interest
(2500 < Rey < 5000). The primary horseshoe vortex is observed from the pig-
ment pattern marked A in Fig. 5.10. It caused a flow separation which resulted in
the separation saddle, denoted as S, in Fig. 5.10. A system of induced vortices is
located upstream of the primary horseshoe vortex. It extends up to the primary
separation saddle point Sz, seen as the accumulation of pigment in Fig. 5.10 which
is located approximately 1.4H upstream of the leading face. The flow separation
line originates at this saddle point and is delimited by the pigment line which is
extended laterally and then further downstream from this point.

The structure of this vortex system appears clearly from the smoke visualisation
given in Fig. 5.11. This image shows a cross-section of the three-dimensional flow
structure illuminated by a laser sheet at the symmetry plane z/H = 0. The smoke
visualisation observations showed the cyclical growth and collapse of the horseshoe
vortex structure. Similar observations were made by Martinuzzi et al. [86, 101] for a
single cube in a fully developed channel flow. They postulated a hypothesis that two
different flow modes counterbalance each other. The alternation between these two
states caused bimodal velocity distributions in the horseshoe vortex region. Such
bimodal behaviour was also detected from LDA measurements in the horseshoe

FIGURE 5.11: Smoke visualisation of the front face of the leading cube in the array of cubes (flow
is from right to left). S, and S, correspond to the notation in the oil-film results.

vortex region for the single cube case considered in this thesis. A typical probability
density function of the streamwise velocity u measured at the station #/H = -
0.5, y/H = 0.1 and z/H = 0 is given in Fig 5.12. Three distinguishable peaks
are observed in this histogram. Two peaks are concentrated around zero velocity
which is expected due to velocity bias. Particles with low velocities passed the
measuring volume less frequently then particles with higher velocities did. This
led to the marked minimum ‘around zero velocity (see the discussion of velocity
bias in chapter 4). The peak in the interarrival time around zero velocity, which is

given as a function of the corresponding velocity (see (+) in Fig 5.12), supports this
conclusion. If the velocity probability density function was weighted, for example
with the reciprocal of the instantaneous velocity, this minimum.around zero would
diminish. A third peak is observed around u + -1.5 m/s. With the knowledge that
velocity bias corrupted the histogram around zero velocity, we clearly observed a
bimodal behaviour which supports the hypothesis of the two preferred flow states.
Devenport and Simpson [38] measured also bimodal pdfs in the horseshoe vortex
region were always one peak was found at zero velocity. Power density spectra of the
velocity datasets were calculated in the area were the probability density functions
displayed bimodal (not due to velocity bias) behaviour to examine whether or not
the dynamic behaviour involved a characteristic frequency. The spectrum shown in
Fig. 5.13, which corresponds to the dataset shown in Fig 5.12, only decays with a
—5/3 slope when plotted in log-log coordinates. No increased activity was observed
around a specific characteristic frequency which suggests that the dynamics occurred
randomly. Therefore, it is not clear if this dynamic process is coupled with the
vortex shedding from the side faces of the cube. With the employed experimental
techniques, it was not possible to measure velocities in the wake and in front of the
cube simultaneously and, therefore, it was not possible to detect a coupling between
the dynamics in the horseshoe vortex and the vortex shedding from the sides of the
cube. Martinuzzi [101] calculated also power spectral densities from the velocity
time series in the horseshoe vortex region and he also did not observe predominant
frequencies.

The stagnation flow in front of the cube, with the vortex roll-up, was also ob-
served from pressure measurements at the channel floor. The local static pressure
coefficient C,, was measured at the centre-line upstream of the cube from z/H = 0
to approximately 2/H = - 2.7. The atmospheric pressure and the bulk velocity were
used to define the pressure coefficient. The results are shown in Fig. 5.14 and cor-
respond to four different Reynolds numbers. Although some minor differences are
observed, the profiles are qualitatively similar for the different Reynolds numbers.

=== [PDF page 134] ===
132 Chapter 5. Single cube

Far upstream, C, asymptotes to a constant value of about C, ~ 0.2. In the di-
rection towards the cube, the pressure increases almost monotonically and becomes
maximum close the windward face of the cube. The results differ somewhat from
the experiments performed by Martinuzzi et al. [101, 102]. They detected a local
maximum in the static pressure at the time averaged stagnation point, at about
x/H = - 0.5. During the period when the four-vortex structure exists, the stag-
nation point is the location where the surface pressure becomes maximum. They
argued that due to the larger relaxation time of the pressure field compared to that
of the velocity field, the pressure imprint of the flow onto the surface is long enough
preserved to be measured as a time-averaged increase. This maximum was not very

FIGURE 5.14: Profiles of the pressure coefficient C, for four different Reynolds numbers: (+) Rey

clear from the present measurements but a somewhat increased pressure coefficient
was observed around z/H =- 0.7. Another difference with the results of Martinuzzi
et al. [101, 102] is that the values of the pressure coefficient are in general somewhat
lower. This is explained by the different channel-height-to-cube-height ratio D/H.
While the results from Martinuzzi were obtained for D/H = 2, in our study this
ratio was D/H = 3.4. In the smaller aspect ratio case, the flow is more constrained
by the channel roof and, therefore, causing a larger build up of the pressure. When
the aspect ratio is larger, which implies that the oncoming flow can be trapped
easier over the top face, the stagnation pressure is lower.

LDA data of the dimensionless mean streamwise velocity U/ug and Reynolds
normal stresses u?/uz, and w?/u3, are plotted in Fig. 5.15 for five different heights
at the front centreline of the cube. The location of the horseshoe vortex can be
identified as the region of negative streamwise velocities and the vortex extends up
to approximately half of the cube height. The peaks in the Reynolds normal stresses
u”™ are found in the proximity of the horseshoe vortex region and coincide with
areas of maximum turbulence production due to large velocity gradients. Further,
the instable behaviour of the horseshoe vortex contributes also to the increased
turbulence production.

The skewness S, (S, = u/o3) is given in Fig. 5.17 for some profiles in the
symmetry plane z/H = 0. The profiles which cross the primary horseshoe vortex at
y/H =0.07-0.1, around z/H = -0.5, have a large positive peak. This deviation from
a Gaussian velocity distribution (the skewness is 0 for a Gaussian pdf) illustrates
that the time series of the streamwise velocity contained dominant positive spikes.
These large velocities indicate that high momentum fluid is integrated in the primary
horseshoe vortex. The pronounced minimum around z/H ~ -0.8 indicates that
strong negative velocity spikes dominate the velocity statistics. The appearance of
the strong positive and negative spikes were caused by the flow dynamics observed
in the horseshoe vortex region. Despite significant variation in S,, the skewness for

the spanwise velocity S,, was more or less 0 (with some experimental uncertainty)
which is expected for the symmetry plane in which the time-averaged spanwise
velocity @ is zero. The kurtosis K, (K,, = u4/o%), also shown in Fig. 5.17, deviates
significantly from 3 (K,, = 3 is denoted as the solid line). Close to the front face
of the cube, K,, exceeds this nominal value. In the horseshoe vortex region (y/H
= 0.07-0.1, 2/H = -0.5) K,, becomes significantly small (about 2) which indicates
that the distribution becomes broader. This is also the location were the turbulence
intensities (seen as the peaks in u® and w”, Fig. 5.15) becomes maximum. The
kurtosis of the pdf of the streamwise velocity, K.,, was more or less 3 according to
that of a Gaussian distribution.

FIGURE 5.19: Sketch of the critical points and stream lines for the symmetry plane z/H = 0

upstream of the cube.

The location of the critical points on the channel surface y/H = 0 in front of
the cube are transposed to the plane of symmetry z/H = 0 and are schematically
summarised in Fig. 5.19. For example, while a reattachment point appears as a
simple node (N) on y/H = 0, it appears as a half saddle (S’) in the plane z/H =
0. The location of free saddles (S) and nodal points in the plane of symmetry is
then deduced to satisfy the topological constraints, given in the chapter on exper-
imental techniques. The sketch was derived from topology analysis together with
the flow visualisation observations and the LDA measurements. The derived sketch
shows much similarities with the sketches given by Martinuzzi [101], Baker [13] and
Devenport and Simpson [38]. It is important to note that the fine flow details are
hard to detect with conventional anemometry techniques, like LDA, Particle Image
Velocimetry (PIV) or hot-wire, because of the finite resolution of the measuring
volume or probe, influences from the wall, etc.

5.3.2 Wake of the obstacle

The mean flow pattern in the wake recirculation region was dominated by an arc-
shaped vortex. This vortex was identified in the symmetry plane z/H = 0 from
smoke visualisation experiments (see Fig. 5.9). The unstable character of the wake
flow was apparent from the recorded smoke visualisations which was already dis-
cussed, The arc vortex is also apparent from the two vorticity concentration nodes
marked with B in the oil-film result given in Fig. 5.10, which are the two counter-
rotating footprints of the wake vortex. The shear layer separated at the top leading
edge and reattached at approximately 1.5H downstream of the trailing face, at 2/H
= 2.5 along the reattachment line, marked with C in Fig. 5.10. Along the reattach-
ment line, a node is observed at z/H = 0 and two saddle points in the proximity
which suggest that the recirculation region cannot be closed. A vector plot of the
time-averaged velocity field at y/H = 0.5 is given in Fig. 5.20. The figure shows
only one half of the flow domain since flow symmetry was demonstrated from mea-
surements across the entire flow domain. The trajectories given in the same figure
are not real streamlines since a three-dimensional flow is considered and the nor-
mal velocity component is not zero in this plane. But these refer to the slope of
the velocity vectors and, therefore, indicate the direction in which a fluid volume is

=== [PDF page 139] ===
5.8. Time-averaged flow field 137

forced. The wake vortex is clearly present and extends, for this location y/H = 0.5,
to approximately one cube size behind the cube. The plot clearly shows a saddle at
approximately one cube distance (at x/H = 2) in the wake of the cube.

FiIGuRE 5.20: Long-term averaged vector plot of the flow around the cube at y/H = 0.5 for one
half of the symmetric flow domain.

Profiles of u/uy at five y/ H-locations in the symmetry plane z/H = 0 are given
in Fig. 5.21. The dashed line at z/H = 1 indicates the location of the rear face of
the cube. The arch-shaped vortex is apparent from the negative velocities. The line
where the velocity changes sign is referred to as the separation line. The reattach-
ment point at the channel floor was derived from an extrapolation of the separation
line to the channel floor. The reattachment point found in this way occurred at z/H
= 2.4-2.5, which is in good agreement with the location of the reattachment point
observed from the oil-film observations, see Fig. 5.10. As would be expected, the
magnitude of the Reynolds normal stresses w? and w?, given in Fig. 5.21 for five
stations in the symmetry plane z/H = 0, are relatively high in the shear layer (the
profiles at y/H = 0.7 and 0.9). The strong velocity gradients caused high shearing
and thus a significant production of turbulence. Further, the shear layer which sep-
arated from the leading top edge reattached at the top face more downstream. Due
to Kelvin-Helmholtz instabilities, the free shear layer in the wake will also carry a
quasi-periodic component. These fluctuations contribute also to the high turbulence
level. It is noted that this periodic motion is significantly weaker in strength than
the vortex shedding from the side faces since it is suppressed by the channel roof and
wall. The skewness, given in Fig. 5.22 along profiles at locations which are similar
to the stations shown in Fig. 5.21, does not show large variations. It seems that
the skewness becomes maximum at a location of maximum turbulence intensity (see
Fig. 5.21). The same holds for the kurtosis, also given in Fig. 5.22, where the values
correspond to more or less Gaussian-shaped velocity distributions. Again it is noted
that the skewness and the kurtosis of the spanwise velocity were also more or less
equivalent to those for a Gaussian-shaped pdf (5S, = 0 and Ky, = 3, respectively)
because of the flow symmetry.

Profiles of u/uz,, w?/uz and ww’ /u?, are given in Fig. 5.23 for three stations
in the wake of the cube at y/H = 0.5. The profiles show large Reynolds normal
stresses, in particular close to the side edge at z/H = 0.4. The large values are again
explained by the high shearing at locations of large averaged velocity gradients.

power density spectrum in Fig. 5.5), it was difficult to resample the datasets (with

periodic fluctuations. The increased turbulence in the shear layer was shown from
the turbulent kinetic energy k. Since the third component, v”, was not measured

for all configurations and stations, this component was estimated from: v” = 0.5
(u? + w). Substitution then leads to the next expression for k:

k = 0.75(u? + w?), (5.4)

Although the Reynolds normal stresses are anisotropic (this can readily be seen
from the profiles of w?/u?, and w/w, given in Fig. 5.23), this approach leads to
reasonably accurate values for k (within 10%). This was verified for the matrix flow,
to be discussed in chapter 8, with additional measurements of the normal velocity v
with the prism-cube. An iso-value plot of the turbulent kinetic energy in the plane
y/H = 0.5, given in Fig. 5.24, shows the increased activity in the free shear layer
behind the side face of the cube. Downstream of the reattachment point (c/H =
2.5), the shear layer has broadened significantly due to diffusion. The turbulent
kinetic energy is rather low in the centre of the wake vortex which indicates that
the perturbations induced by vortex shedding are damped out towards the centre.
The recovery of the flow further downstream is also observed from Fig. 5.24.

5.3.3 Obstacle top face

The top face is characterised by a bound vortex in the vicinity of the leading edge.
The oil-surface image of Fig. 5.10 shows two vorticity concentration nodes marked
D, which are attributed to the footprints of this vortex. Fig. 5.25 gives vector plots
of the velocity field in two parallel planes at a distance of 1 mm (z = 16 mm, left
plot) and 1.5 mm (z = 16.5 mm, right plot), just above the top face. The velocity
field in the lower plane (z = 16.0 mm) is in agreement with the features derived from
the pigment pattern at the top face. The smaller pitch between the counter-rotating
centre nodes for z = 16.5 mm is consistent with the bent shape of the top vortex. An
extrapolation of the separation line with @ = 0 suggests that the reattachment of the
shear layer at the top face occurred almost at the trailing edge. This is consistent
with the oil-film plot in Fig. 5.10 which shows a smeared pigment pattern in the
proximity of the trailing edge. Traverses of @/ug, u?/u% and w?/u% at the top
centreline of the cube are plotted in Fig. 5.26 for different values of r/H. A strong
recirculation zone progresses in the downstream direction where the largest negative
velocities are observed for z/H = 0.5. The velocity gradients are particularly large
in this recirculation area and are of the order of magnitude of du/dz ~ 5000 1/s. It
was already discussed in chapter 4 that these strong velocity gradients may possibly
lead to velocity gradient bias due to the finite size of the measuring volume. But
because of the relatively small measuring volume encountered in the LDA optics
used, this error is much smaller than the experimental uncertainty. The profiles
of u®/u, and w?/u2, show maxima at. approximately 0.13H above the top wall of
the cube which coincides with areas of large local production of turbulence. The
from the leading top edge separated shear layer reattached intermittently at the top
face close to the trailing edge which also contributed to the increased turbulence

were directed in opposite direction in the vortex core. The kurtosis Ky, given
in Fig. 5.27, is significantly smaller than 3 at the locations close to the cube face

5.3.4 Obstacle side face

The flow at the two sides of the cube was dominated by a side vortex close to the
leading edge, confined by the main stream and the cube. These side vortices had
origins at the nodes close to the leading edge corner on the channel floor which
are marked with E in Fig. 5.10. The imprints of the vortex on the side faces can
be seen in the oil-film close-up given in the right image in Fig. 5.18. The removal
of visualisation pigment close to leading edge, the white band, is the result of the
rotation of the vortex. The pigment structure close to the trailing edge is caused
by the flow reattachment. This side vortex was also demonstrated from LDA-

of u'w! in the vicinity of the side vortices and the free shear layers just behind the
cube.

The skewness factor S,,, see Fig. 5.31, displays a marked peak close to the side
wall at approximately 1-2 mm away from the wall, similarly as for the top face.

5.3.5 Comparison with literature

The single cube case discussed in this thesis differed in several aspects from the
case of Martinuzzi et al. [101]. The isolated cube as discussed here was placed in a
developing turbulent channel flow at Rey = 4440, while Martinuzzi et al. considered
a fully developed channel flow for a Reynolds number which was almost one order
of magnitude higher (i.e. Rey = 40,000). Further, the channel-height-to-cube-size
ratio was different (D/H = 3.4 in the present case versus D/H = 2 in Martinuzzi’s
case).

Although, the flow fields were in general more or less qualitatively similar, we
found shear layer reattachment at the top and side faces of the cube while this was
absent in the fully developed case. Further, the flow reattachment at the channel
floor downstream of the cube occurred at a larger distance away from the leeward
face than in the fully developed channel flow. The flow dynamics was also different
as illustrated by the measurements of the vortex shedding frequency. Although the
present flow is superficially comparable to the fully developed channel flow, these
noticeable differences in the flow will have a significant effect on the local convective
heat transfer from the cube.

Profiles of the scaled Reynolds normal stresses w/w? mas and w [0 max and
mean velocity U/Uma, are compared in Fig. 5.34 to results from Martinuzzi et al.

[101]. To allow the comparison, the values were scaled with the maximum value

aforementioned flow differences. The deviations observed around y/H = 2 are due
to the presence of the channel roof for the literature data (the aspect ratio was D/H
= 2 in that case).

5.4 Convective heat transfer

Heat transfer measurements were performed for bulk velocities of 2.8, 3.2, 4.0, 4.5
and 5.0 m/s which correspond to Reynolds numbers of respectively Rey = 2750,
3200, 4000, 4440 and 4970. For these Reynolds numbers, the channel flow was
turbulent. While the flow field was documented with LDA for Rey = 4440, flow
visualisation experiments and measurements of vortex shedding frequencies in the
wake of the cube illustrated that the derived flow field applied to the entire range
of Reynolds numbers in which the heat transfer measurements were performed (i.e.
2500 < Rey < 5000).

Additional measurements were performed for laminar developing channel flows.
Such a situation was achieved by running the wind tunnel at a sufficiently low speed

=== [PDF page 149] ===
5.4. Convective heat transfer 147

such that the tripping wire was not effectively tripping the flow to turbulence. The
Reynolds numbers for these situations were Rey = 533, 888, 1332, 1776 and 2220.
Since no LDA data were collected for these cases, only some flow visualisations, the
interpretation of the results is limited to a qualitative one.

The temperature of the internal copper core was adjusted to 75.0 °C’, which
was regulated to the supplied power through a feedback loop. For one Reynolds
number, Rey — 4440, the copper core temperature was varied between 60 °C’ and
80 °C to investigate the influence of the surface temperature boundary condition on
the heat transfer coefficient. Further, these experiments illustrated the accuracy of
the surface temperature measurements. The inlet temperature of the air was kept at
room temperature (20-21 °C’). The real ambient temperatures during the different
experiments were measured and used to calculate the radiative heat fluxes and the
heat transfer coefficients.

5.4.1 Distributions of the surface temperature

Verification of the accuracy of the infrared thermography was enabled by comparing
the infrared surface temperature distributions with liquid crystal temperature mea-
surements. The improvement of infrared image restoration was already confirmed
in the discussion of the infrared image restoration technique (see chapter 4). The
results shown here refer to different Reynolds numbers.

Liquid crystal results are shown in Fig. 5.35 along two paths at the cube surface
(cross-sections ABCD and ABC'DA) for three Reynolds numbers, Rey = 2220, 3200
and 4440. The lowest Reynolds number corresponds to the laminar developing
channel flow. The results for this Reynolds number were added to demonstrate
the agreement between infrared and liquid crystal measurements in the entire range
of Reynolds numbers considered. The upper figure corresponds to cross-section
ABCDA which denotes a plane parallel to the mounting base at y/ H = 0.5 and cross-
section ABCD denotes a plane perpendicular to the mounting base at the symmetry
plane z/H = 0. The LC measurements, only available at discrete locations at the
cross-sections of isotherms, are indicated with A, 0 and © for Rey = 2220, 3200
and 4440, respectively. Notice that liquid crystal data are not available for all the
cube faces for Rey = 2200. The infrared measurements, obtained under similar
experimental conditions, are also plotted in the figures (lines with +, o and x for
Rey = 2220, 3200 and 4440, respectively). The surface temperatures from the two
different thermography techniques agree very well. This confirms the accuracy of
0.4 °C’ which is claimed for the IR temperature measurements.

Isotherm maps of the five faces exposed to air flow for the five turbulent Reynolds
number cases, shown in Fig. 5.36, are very similar. The five faces are folded out and
mapped into one plane. The flow is from left to right. Together with the oil-film
results it is reasonable to assume that the surface heat transfer and flow field are
qualitatively similar throughout the turbulent Rey-range studied. The topology of
the surface temperature plots show a marked similarity with the pigment structure
at the top face of the cube (see Fig. 5.10). An accumulation of pigment, the imprint
of the bound top vortex, is observed at the location of the hot spot. Since the cubes

=== [PDF page 150] ===
148 Chapter 5. Single cube

75

FIGURE 5.35: Surface temperatures along path ABCDA (upper plot) and along path ABCD (lower
plot) for Rey = 2220, Rey = 3200 and Rey = 4440 respectively: comparison between LC (A, O
and ©) and infrared thermography (lines with +, 0 and x).

were mounted vertically, it is important to note that the isotherm distributions are
symmetric. This symmetry indicates that buoyancy effects are negligible, which was
to be expected since the ratio of the Grashof number to Reynolds number squared
(Gr/Re?) is significantly smaller than one (Gr/Re? = HgAT/TU? x 0.001, where
g is gravity, H = 0.015 m is the cube size, AT = 40 °C is the temperature difference
between the surface and the ambient, T = 333 °C’ is the absolute temperature and
U = 4 m/s is the characteristic velocity).

Variation of the copper temperature leads to different levels of the surface tem-
perature distributions. For five copper temperatures, i.e. T,. = 60, 65, 70, 75 and
80 °C, the surface temperature distribution was measured with the infrared sys-
tem. The surface temperatures along cross-sections ABCDA and ABCD are given
in Fig. 5.37. It is noted that the temperature profiles are more or less congruent,
this illustrates again the accuracy of the measurements. Further, the heat transfer
coefficients calculated from these surface temperature boundary conditions differed
about 5%. These differences are within the experimental uncertainty of the method
and measurement technique. Taking this experimental uncertainty into account, it
can be concluded that the convective heat transfer did not depend on the boundary
condition imposed by the different surface temperatures.

5.4.2 Distributions of the local heat transfer coefficient

Profiles of the surface temperature and heat transfer coefficient are given in Figs. 5.38
and 5.39 along paths ABCDA and ABCD, respectively. The local heat transfer
coefficients were made dimensionless with the cube-averaged heat transfer coefficient
h. The coinciding profiles of the heat transfer coefficients again illustrate that
similarity was verified throughout the range of Reynolds numbers considered for

=== [PDF page 152] ===
150 Chapter 5. Single cube

A a: — | Cc | | D A
location on path

FicurE 5.38: Distributions of the surface temperature and heat transfer coefficient along path
ABCDA parametric in the Reynolds number: (+) Rey = 2750, (0) Rey = 3200, (x) Rey = 4000,
(A) Rey = 4440 and (O) Rey = 4970.

the turbulent heat transfer measurements (2750 < Rey < 4970) and that the flow
field determined for Rex = 4440 applied for this range. The temperature profiles
are added to support the discussion of the marked variations in the local convective
heat transfer. Further, these are the measurements from which the convective heat
fluxes were derived.

Front face

From the flow study it was found that the oncoming flow impinged at the front face
of the cube leading to a flow stagnation point at about y/H ~ 2/3 at the symmetry
line z/H = 0 (see Fig. 2.1). Flow impingement generally causes a large and uniform
convective heat transfer in the proximity of the stagnation point. This is clearly
seen in the profiles along path ABCDA and ABCD in Figs. 5.38 and 5.39, partition
BC and CD, respectively. The values of h in the proximity of the stagnation point
compared well with data found in the literature.

The small minimum in the heat transfer coefficient, found at the foot of the front
face close to the mounting base (see Fig. 5.39, partition C'D), corresponds to the cen-
tre of the horseshoe vortex while h slightly increases towards the channel floor, point
D, which is in opposite direction to the local fluid velocity in the horseshoe vortex.

=== [PDF page 153] ===
5.4. Convective heat transfer 151

FIGURE 5.39: Distributions of the surface temperature and heat transfer coefficient along path
ABCD parametric in the Reynolds number: (+) Rey = 2750, (o) Rex = 3200, (x) Rey = 4000,
(A) Rey = 4440 and (GO) Rey = 4970.

The study of the flow field revealed that the Reynolds normal stresses (or turbulent
kinetic energy) showed large peaks in the proximity of the horseshoe vortex. The
peaks in the Reynolds normal stresses coincided with maximum turbulence produc-
tion areas due to large velocity gradients. Furthermore, the unstable behaviour of
the horseshoe vortex contributed to the increased turbulence production. Since this
region was identified as an area of minimum convective heat transfer, it is suggested
that a dissimilarity existed between the heat and momentum transfer: although
the level of turbulence generally intensifies the heat transport leading to higher h
values, this does not apply for the horseshoe vortex region. An explanation for this
departure was sought in the local fluid temperature. The rotational behaviour of
the horseshoe vortex caused the residence time of the fluid in the vortex to be high,
allowing the local fiuid temperature to increase and, in turn, reduced the local con-
vective heat transfer. Further, heat leakage via conduction from the cube through
the mounting base might cause a slight increase in temperature of the base plate
upstream of the cube. The rotating horseshoe vortex was, therefore, also heated
by this secondary effect, although the effect was estimated to be smaller then 5%
(this was estimated from a global energy balance). An opposite observation was
made around the stagnation point. Although the heat transfer coefficients are high
and rather uniform, the turbulent kinetic energy was moderate compared to those

=== [PDF page 154] ===
152 Chapter 5. Single cube

measured in the horseshoe vortex region. But for this area, the high-velocity and
low-enthalpy impinging flow caused a beneficial convective cooling.

A similar mechanism was observed by Suzuki et al. [82, 160, 161, 162] for the
surface heat transfer in a boundary layer flow disturbed by an inserted cylinder
normal to the flow direction. They found a significant enhancement in the local
surface heat transfer at the location where the friction factor became minimum.
The observed dissimilarity was assumed to be directly related to the vortex shedding
from the cylinder. The so-called washing action caused entrainment of cold air at
the location where the vortex was directed downward to the surface. An ejection of
hot fluid existed at the location where the flow separated from the surface. They
supported their hypothesis with time-dependent simulations [77] showing that this
dissimilarity also existed for the instantaneous friction and surface heat transfer.

More or less similar observations were made by Natarajan and Chyu [123, 33] for
distributions of the mass transfer coefficient of a cube in a thin boundary layer flow
at Reynolds numbers between 3.1 x 104 and 1.1 x 10°. The horse vortex region
was also indicative for lower mass transfer coefficients while the remainder of the
front face exhibited a more or less constant mass transfer rate.

Rear face

The convective heat transfer from the leeward face of the cube was more or less
uniform, except for the local maximum at the cube upper edge (see partition DA
and AB in Fig. 5.38 and Fig. 5.39, respectively). The flow on this face is dominated
by the arch-shaped vortex. Although the turbulence intensity was rather low in the
core of the vortex, the outer region of the vortex was rather turbulent because of
the vortex shedding and the turbulence production due to large averaged velocity
gradients. This is seen in the plot of the turbulent kinetic energy & in the plane
y/H = 0.5, given in Fig. 5.24. While the velocity fluctuations were moderate in the
vortex core, an increased activity was found in the free shear layer behind the side
faces of the cube. Further downstream, the flow recovers to an equilibrium state,
as is seen from the decreasing turbulence intensity. Partially due to the mechanism
of vortex shedding, the fluid recirculation in the wake caused an efficient mixing
which Jed to higher and more uniformly distributed local fluid temperatures. This
in turn caused uniform distributions of the local heat transfer coefficients. The local
maximum at the cube upper edge of the leeward face is explained by entrainment of
cold air from the shear layer over the top face which locally benefits the convective
heat transfer. The heat transfer coefficient increases somewhat in the direction of
the channel floor. This is explained by the rotation of the wake vortex and the
impingement of low-temperature fluid which entered the wake recirculation from
the bottom.

Natarajan and Chyu (123, 33] reported different local mass transfer coefficients
at the rear face of the cube. While in the present study the distributions of k were
more or less uniform except in the vicinity of the upper top and side edges, where
a local maximum and sharp minima were respectively found, the distributions of
the mass transfer coefficient showed uniform coefficients across the entire face with

=== [PDF page 155] ===
5.4. Convective heat transfer 153

exception of a maximum towards the channel floor. They argued that close to the
channel floor, high-rotational fluid from the horseshoe vortex entrains in the wake
region and caused the mass transfer to become locally maximum. The maximum
close to the upper edge was not present in their case. The shear layer reattachment
at the top face of the cube, as found to be an important mechanism at the top face
in the present study, caused the flow to be directed from the top wall and, therefore,
such a pronounced entrainment was not detected by Natarajan and Chyu.

Side faces

The profiles of the heat transfer coefficient at the side faces are shown between
partitions AB and CD of path ABCDA in Fig. 5.38. A marked minimum was
found at the front half of the side face, a marked maximum existed at the back half
of the side face, close to the trailing side edge. The variation in h along the face is
up to 100 %. The flow analysis showed that the flow separated at the leading side
edge and reattached downstream at the side face at approximately z/H = 0.9. The
flow separation resulted in a side vortex, which is oriented nearly in parallel to the
leading side edge. The ’artist’s impression’ shown in Fig. 2.1 is a long-term averaged
illustration of the flow structure. Flow instabilities will cause somewhat a-symmetric
flow patterns in time: in one cycle, a vortex is shed from the left side face while it is
shed from the right face in the next cycle. This alternating vortex shedding resulted
in quasi-periodic separation and reattachment. The side vortex, therefore, vibrates
somewhat around an equilibrium state. But despite of this vibration, the side vortex
was dominantly present. From the h-profiles in Fig. 5.38 it is seen that the location
of minimum heat transfer coefficients corresponded to that of the vortex core. This
centre of the vortex is associated with a region of high wall shear stress and low
static surface pressure. According to Reynolds analogy, regions of high wall shear
stress should imply large heat transfer coefficients and this observation thus suggests
that Reynolds’ analogy locally fails. An explanation is again found in the local fluid
temperature in the vortex. Heat is convected in the recirculation region, leading
+o an increased fluid temperature and the vortex is, therefore, acting as a kind of
insulation layer. This buffer prevents beneficial convective heat transfer. On the
contrary, the intermittent flow reattachment convects fresh and relatively cold fluid
to the surface. And together with strong velocity fluctuations normal to the wail,
this mechanism caused the convective heat transfer intensification observed from the
local maximum of the heat transfer coefficient in the proximity of the reattachment
point (c/H ~ 0.9). Areas of flow reattachment are usually characterised by low wall
shear stresses, and contrary to Reynolds analogy, it is the large velocity fluctuations
normal to the wall as well as the low-enthalpy fluid which intensify the local removal
of heat.

The mass transfer coefficients of Natarajan and Chyu [123, 33] showed a slight
increase in the streamwise direction for the side face. This was caused by the flow
recirculation which enveloped the entire side face. Due to the oncoming flow condi-
tions, the shear layers which separated from the leading side edges did not reattach
on the side faces. Therefore, the gradients in the local mass transfer coefficients re-

=== [PDF page 156] ===
154 Chapter 5. Single cube

mained moderate in contrast to the present results which were primarily dominated
by flow separation and subsequent reattachment.

Top face

Profiles of the heat transfer coefficients for the top face are shown in Fig. 5.39,
partition BC. These profiles show also large spatial streamwise gradients: the heat
transfer coefficient drops to a minimum where after is recovers downstream to a
plateau of maximum convective heat transfer. Similarly to the less extensive side
vortices at the side faces, the top vortex dictates the local convective heat transfer
at the top face. It was concluded from LDA measurements that no detectable vortex
shedding occurred from the top trailing edge. Instabilities in the flow over the top
face did not lead to vortex shedding since the necessary second shear layer was
absent. Further, it is believed that due to the vicinity of the opposing channel wall
(roof) and the channel floor possible flow oscillations were suppressed. Despite that,
the separated top shear layer reattached intermittently at approximately z/H = 0.9
at the top face. This caused local heat transfer intensification and led to the local
maximum in the convective heat transfer towards the trailing edge. Again, a local
heat transfer minimum is observed in an area of high wall shear stresses (on the wall
directly below the bound vortex core) and a maximum heat transfer in an area of
low wall shear stresses {the reattachment region).

Natarajan and Chyu [123, 33] observed monotonically increasing mass transfer
coefficients in the streamwise direction at the top face. They attributed this to
the recirculation of the top vortex which enveloped the entire top face. Again, the
significant differences between the local heat and mass transfer distributions were
caused by the different oncoming conditions as encountered in both studies.

Correlations

Due to the coupling between velocity and pressure field and between velocity and
convective heat transfer, a correlation between the local static surface pressure and
the surface heat transfer was sought. This attempt is shown in Fig. 5.42 where
the local heat transfer coefficients are plotted versus the local static pressure coef-
ficient C,, as given in Fig. 5.16. Only the results for one typical Reynolds number
are shown since it was proven that for the different Reynolds numbers studied the
scaled heat transfer coefficients coincided with each other. The results shown were
measured along the cross-sections ABCDA and ABCD, at locations for which the
local surface static pressure was measured (see Fig 4.50 for the locations in ques-
tion). The results for the top (x) and side (0) faces show marked similarities. The
most negative pressure coefficients were found underneath the centre of the top and
side vortices. Areas of low static pressure are usually associated with high velocities.
These locations corresponded to areas of minimum convective heat transfer. Fur-
ther downstream, both the pressure and the local heat transfer coefficient increase
monotonically. Flow reattachment is subsequently characterised by maxima in both
the heat transfer coefficient and surface static pressure. The strong gradient dh/dC,
supports the hypothesis that the strong differences in the heat transfer coefficients

are partly caused by temperature effects which are associated with the rotating vor-

the cold flow caused large values of the heat transfer coefficient.

If the values for the rear face are omitted, the data can be fitted with a function,

line in Fig. 5.42.

5.4.3 Time-dependent heat transfer

As discussed earlier, the oncoming main flow separated at the sharp leading side
edges of the cube, and intermittently reattached further downstream at the side
faces of the cube. To investigate a possible coherence between the dynamics of
reattachment of the side shear layers and the time-dependent convective heat flux,
complementary time-dependent heat flux measurements were performed with the
active heat flux sensor as described in chapter 3. The sensor was placed in the back
half of the side face of the cube as illustrated in Fig. 3.13. If periodicities were
present in the flow, it was expected that these also show up in the heat flux signal
due to the direct coupling. From power density spectra of the time-dependent heat
flux measurements no increased activity was detected at the frequency of vortex
shedding. The fluctuations in the reattached flow were probably too small to have
a measurable impact on the heat transfer from the side faces. Further, a kind of
spatial averaging was caused by the rather large effective sensor area (4 x 4 mm)
which damped the heat flux fluctuations a bit.

Differences between both averages appeared to be only a couple of percent (1-3%)

=== [PDF page 159] ===
§.4. Convective heat transfer 157

more related to literature work in which the cube-averaged heat transfer coefficients
are usually directly determined from the averaged convective heat flux and the
isothermal cube temperature, we will only present these averages.

The cube-averaged heat transfer coefficients h are given on log-log scale in
Fig. 5.41 as a function of Rey. On logarithmic scale, h is linearly dependent on
the Reynolds number. A linear regression suggests a fit of the type h = A(Rey)®,
where A and B denote constants. These kind of expressions are commonly found
for forced convective heat transfer situations. A least squares fit through the data
suggests an exponent of B = 0.65. From the naphthalene sublimation experiments
of Natarajan and Chyu [123, 33] a coefficient of B = 0.63 was found. Although the
Reynolds number range considered in their study differed approximately one decade
from the present Reynolds number range, the exponents are in good agreement.

The face-averaged heat transfer coefficients, also given in Fig. 5.41 for the differ-
ent faces, are also linearly dependent on Rey. The high heat transfer coefficient at
the front face (indicated with x) is indicative for the impinging flow at the front face.
The low-temperature oncoming flow caused high rates of convective heat transfer.
The averaged heat transfer at the remaining faces is of comparable order of magni-
tude but markedly smaller (about 50 %) than that at the front face. These faces
suffer all from strong flow recirculations which have apparently a reducing effect on
the convective heat transfer. Among these faces, the top face has the lowest aver-
aged heat transfer coefficient (indicated with o in Fig. 5.41). Besides the strong top
vortex found above this face, it is assumed that a blockage effect was present. The
channel roof imposes a significant constraint since the channel-height-to cube-height
ratio was D/H = 3.4. Although flow was trapped over the top face of the cube, the
corresponding mass flow rate is somewhat smaller than that along the side faces.
A further effect which intensified the convective heat transfer at the side faces was
vortex shedding from the trailing side edges. Natarajan and Chyu {123, 33] found
exponents between B = 0.54 (front face) and B = 0.67 (side face) for the different
faces of the cubical obstacle, which are in good agreement with the present results.

The face-averaged heat transfer coefficients are plotted versus the face-averaged
static pressure C,, as given in Fig. 5.16, for five different values of the Reynolds
number. It was already mentioned that the pressure measurements corresponded
to the whole range of Reynolds numbers of 2500 < Rey < 5000. As expected,
the windward face (C, = 0.74) had the largest convective heat transfer due to
flow impingement. Further, this windward face had the largest face-averaged static
surface pressure coefficient as the result of the pressure build-up of the stagnant flow.
The correlation for the other faces is not ambiguous. The convective heat transfer
for the leeward face (C, = -0.13) is significantly lower than that at the front face.
But the heat transfer for the other faces (top face, C, = -0.59, and side face, C, =
-0.13) tends to increase for even more negative pressure coefficients.

identified as a developing laminar boundary layer flow. Both the flow field and the

the laminar case, the top and the side vortices covered these cube faces entirely (see
the imprints in Fig. 5.43) and shear layer reattachment did not occur at these faces.
This is in contrast with the turbulent case for which the top and side shear layers
reattached at the back half of the top and side faces. This flow reattachment caused
that the vortices were much smaller and that they were located close to the leading
edges of the cube.

tions, is directly reflected in the convective heat transfer. Infrared surface tempera-

The Reynolds number was Rey = 4440 for both cases. Significant differences in
the surface temperature are observed, in particular at the top and side faces which
were covered by the top and side vortices. Because these vortices were much smaller
in the turbulent case compared to the laminar case, the corresponding temperature
peaks are, therefore, much thinner. Further, flow reattachment downstream on these
faces in the turbulent case intensified the convective heat transfer which is reflected
in a decrease in the surface temperature. The high surface temperatures for the
laminar untripped case are explained by the absence of flow reattachment. The
differences in the surface temperatures and, therefore, in the surface heat transfer,
are very small for the rear face. Differences diminish even within the experimental
uncertainty for the front face of the cube. Apparently, the heat transfer in the front
stagnation region is hardly effected by the oncoming flow conditions.
Face-averaged heat transfer coefficients are given in Fig. 5.45 for the Reynolds
number range 533 < Reg < 4973. It is noted that the results for the five largest
Reynolds numbers are reproduced from Fig. 5.41. Some interesting features appear
from the figure. A clear jump in the heat transfer coefficient for the top and side
faces occurred at a critical Reynolds number of about Reg ~ 2500. It is obvious
that this jump in the heat transfer is caused by the tripping strip which becomes
affective for a certain Reynolds number. A laminar boundary layer thickness scales
with Rez!/> while a turbulent boundary layer varies with Rez'/? (Rez denotes a
local Reynolds number, ie. Re, = Ux/v). When the boundary layer is too thick
compared to the tripping strip, which is the case for the lower values of Rey, the
strip is not effectively tripping the flow and, therefore, no transition to a turbulent

boundary layer flow will occur. Only the convective heat transfer from the top and
side faces of the cube were significantly affected by the transition to a turbulent
channel flow. The averaged heat transfer coefficient at the front face increased
gradually without a significant jump. This was already concluded from the surface
temperatures shown in Fig, 5.44.

Additional heat transfer measurements were performed for the isolated cube
in the channel fow without the tripping strip (a developing laminar channel flow
resulted) for the Reynolds number range of 2000 < Rey < 5000. From these mea-
surements, face-averaged heat transfer coefficients were determined. These are given
in Fig. 5.46 together with the previous results. The results for Reynolds numbers

=== [PDF page 164] ===
162 Chapter 5. Single cube

smaller than the critical number (Rey ~ 2500) collapse pretty well. This illustrates
the good reproducibility of the experiments. While the profiles of the averaged
heat transfer coefficient of the front and the rear faces collapsed more or less for
the tripped and untripped cases for Reynolds numbers larger than Rey ~ 2500,
significant differences are observed between those of the top and side faces. The
monotonically increasing laminar results for the top and the side faces illustrate
that the top and side vortices enveloped the entire faces and that flow reattachment
was not present.

The jump in the convective heat transfer at Rey ~ 2500 can be traced back to an
increased power supply P. The measurement assemblies were heated with an electri-
cal resistance heat source in the core of the cube. The power supply was determined
from the measured voltage drop V and the electrical current I. The supplied power
for the heat transfer measurements in the developing turbulent (tripped) and the
laminar (not tripped) developing channel flow are given in Fig. 5.47. The transition
around Rez = 2500 is clear from the results for the tripped case. In case that the
flow is not tripped, the supplied power increased gradually.

Array of 9 cubes

The in-line array of nine cubical protrusions, size H = 15 mm, was placed at half the
channel width on the channel floor. The centre-to-centre spacing between the cubes
was S,/H = 2. A schematic of the configuration is given in Fig. 6.1. A boundary
layer flow developed along the test-section walls. This resulted in a boundary layer
thickness of approximately half the cube height at the location of the row. The first
six cubes in the in-line row were used to determine local heat transfer coefficients.
Two additional cubes enabled the in situ calibration (cube 7) and the location
identification of the obstacles (cube 9). Cube 8 was used to establish an appropriate
environment for the in situ calibration. This nomenclature is used in the sketch in
Fig. 6.1 (right plot).

Although the general flow direction in this thesis is from left to right, in some
exceptions flow results (images) are presented in the opposite direction, from right
to left. It will be explicitly mentioned if this is the case.

FIGURE 6.1: Schematics of the in-line row of cubes. The left sketch is a side view of the test-section
with the infrared camera in position. The right plot shows a three-dimensional view of the set-up.

6.1 Flow field

The flow field around the cubes was derived from LDA-measurements, smoke and
oil-film visualisations. The ’artist’s impression’ given in Fig. 6.2 shows the flow field
around the first three cubes in the in-line row. Cross-sections at the symmetry
plane z/H = 0 and a plane parallel to the channel wall at y/H = 0.5 are shown
in Fig. 6.3, the upper and lower plot, respectively. The LDA-measurements were
performed at a bulk velocity of ug = 4.47 m/s which corresponds to a Reynolds
number of Rey = 4440. Both the smoke and oil-film visualisations were carried out
for Reynolds number in the range 1000 < Rey < 8000. It is noted that no significant

6.1. Flow field 165

FiGuRE 6.4: Oil-film visualisation of the surface flow pattern of an in-line row of cubes in a

patterns on and around all but the first obstacle are qualitatively similar. However,
the flow patterns are not fully developed (i-e. no spatial periodicity in the streamwise
direction) even after six cubes as can be seen from the nearly linear growth of the
region bound by the pigment line A (see Fig. 6.4). The rather complex surface
pigment patterns directly upstream of the leading cube are due to the horseshoe
vortex (this vortex is marked with A in Fig. 6.2). The cross-section of this vortex
structure upstream of the leading cube at z/H = 0 can clearly be observed in the
instantaneous smoke visualisations given in Fig. 6.5, obtained with a laser light sheet
technique. The smoke experiments presented here were performed at a Reynolds
number of Rey = 1000. For the experiments at larger Reynolds numbers, the flow
became more turbulent and the smoke traces diffused in the turbulent flow field.
The flow structure appeared then only from-the recorded movie and not from the
single shots as presented here. But as already stated, no severe differences were
observed between the lower and the higher Reynolds number experiments. It is
explicitly mentioned that the flow visualisations were performed in an isothermal
situation without powering of the cubes which excluded buoyancy effects. While
in the upper two images the smoke traces only elucidate the horseshoe vortex in
front of the leading cube, the lower two images also elucidate the flow structure in
the inter-obstacle spacings. The horseshoe vortex structure found upstream of the
leading cube is similar to the one found for the single cube case (chapter 5). The
flow separation caused by the primary horseshoe vortex resulted in two separation
saddles, denoted as S, and §, in Fig. 6.4. The critical lines through S, and 8, mark
the location of the horseshoe vortex system as it is deflected downstream along the
lateral sides of the array. These join approximately 1.54 downstream of the leading
obstacle trailing edge indicating that the horseshoe vortex structure is consolidated
in the primary vortex. From the instantaneous smoke visualisations it was observed

=== [PDF page 168] ===
166 Chapter 6. Array of 9 cubes

FIGURE 6.5: Four smoke visualisation images of the instantaneous flow pattern around the in-line
row of cubical protrusions at the symmetry plane z/H = 0. Flow is from right to left.

that the horseshoe vortex was unstable. Such a behaviour was also observed for the
single cube in the developing turbulent channel flow.

The appearance of two counter-rotating foci on top of the leading cube (marked
with N in Fig. 6.4), the imprint of the bound vortex at the top face, indicates flow
reversal, This top vortex is indicated with B in Fig. 6.2. The vortex was confirmed
by LDA-measurements of the streamwise velocity component at the centreline of the
cube, shown in Fig. 6.6. The profiles of the mean velocity and the Reynolds normal
stresses at the centreline are plotted for five different locations in the downstream
direction: «/H = 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7 and 0.9. The dashed line, at y = 15 mm, refers
to the solid top face of the leading cube. Strong peaks in the Reynolds normal
stresses were found in the vicinity of the recirculation region, at approximately y =
17 mm, which is at 2 mm distance from the top wall of cube. This is a result of
strong shearing at locations of large velocity gradients. Similarly as for the single
cube case, flow instabilities caused the flow to intermittently reattach at the back
half of the side faces which also contributed to the large values of wu’.

This flow pattern differs markedly from those on the top faces of the downstream
cubes, where the lack of pigment at the leading edge suggests attached flow. The
profiles of U/ug and u?/u?, for five locations at the top face of the second cube,

The significance of the pigment accumulation at the trailing edge of the obstacle
top face is not clear. It may be a result of either surface adhesion (evacuation of the
pigment being blocked by the return flow up the leeward face) or due to a local flow
reversal. Fortunately, since both structures are topologically closed (i.e. removable
mathematical singularities), their interpretation will not affect the deduced flow
pattern for the remaining field (see Figs. 6.13 and 6.14).

In the wake of the leading and the subsequent cubes, two vorticity concentrations
are denoted by the counter-rotating foci (indicated with N in Fig. 6.4). These are
the imprints of an arc-shaped vortex, confined in the inter-cube spacing. This arc
vortex is indicated with C in Fig. 6.2. An instantaneous smoke visualisation along
the plane y/H = 0.3, given in Fig. 6.9, shows the horseshoe vortex structure in
front of the leading cube and the cross-section of the arc-shaped vortex in the gap
between the leading and the second cube. Two saddles existed in the inter-cube
area (S in Fig. 6.4) suggesting that fluid is exhausted from the wake region between
these points and the downstream windward face. Hence, the gap area does not form
a closed recirculation bubble. This is dictated by conservation of mass since fluid is
