entrained by the top and the side shear layers.

The existence of a vortex tube extending up the cube side faces was inferred from
its footprint on the channel floor (marked with N in Fig. 6.4) directly downstream
of the leading side edge. The side vortex is marked with D in Fig. 6.2.

Vortex shedding was not detected in the wake of the leading and the subsequent
cubes. This is in agreement with the observations made for the in-line tandem
arrangement of cubes, where the onset to vortex shedding was detected at a minimal
streamwise distance of 1.4 H between the cubes (see chapter 7).

Measurements of the mean velocities and the Reynolds normal stresses in the xz-

The solid side cube wall is indicated with the dashed line. The side vortex appeared
from the negative velocities found at the side face of the first cube. Further, the arc-
vortex in the inter-obstacle spacing was demonstrated from the negative streamwise
velocities.

The Reynolds normal stresses u” and w? become maximum at the location z/H
= 0.5-0.7 for the leading cube where the separated shear layer reattached, similarly
as for the top face. The maximum occurred at approximately 2-3 mm from the solid
walls. Again these maxima were attributed to the strong shearing at locations of
large velocity gradients and to the intermittent flow reattachment.

Far away from the cube surface, all lines asymptote to the undisturbed boundary
layer values. The Reynolds shear stresses u’w’ in the xz-plane are shown in Fig. 6.12
for the same traverses as discussed above. Marked maxima in u’w’ are found at
locations with relatively large velocity gradients, as appears from the maxima at
roughly «/H = 0.5-0.7. The correlation u’w’ remains relatively large in the free

6.2 Convective heat transfer

The heat transfer measurements were performed for Reynolds numbers of Rey =
795, 2086, 3278 and 5066. The aforementioned flow field applied to the three largest
Reynolds numbers studied, while the flow deviated significantly from that for the
lowest Re-number studied (Rey = 795). .

6.2.1 Surface temperature measurements

A comparison between liquid crystal and infrared temperature measurements is
given in Fig. 6.15 for the fifth cube along paths ABCD and ABCDA. Path ABCDA
denotes the cross-section of a plane at half the cube height, parallel to the mounting
plate; path ABCD is the cross-section of the plane at the centreline of the cube ori-
ented perpendicularly to the base plate. The liquid crystal measurements (isotherms
at the surface and, therefore, only available at certain temperature levels) are indi-
cated by the symbols (©) and (A) for Rey = 2086 and Rey = 5066, respectively.
Infrared temperature measurements, based on images which were processed with the
restoration filter and the in situ calibration, are indicated as lines with symbols (4)
and (0) for the corresponding Reynolds numbers. The temperatures of both methods
coincide within the experimental uncertainty of the LC measurements. The good
agreement provides an accuracy estimate of 0.4 °C’ for the infrared temperatures.
The small deviations between the results of each of the techniques are attributed to
experimental uncertainty in both the spatial coordinate and the temperatures.
Isotherm maps of the five faces exposed to air flow for the first 5 subsequent
cubes in the packed array are given in Figs. 6.16 and 6.17 for Reynolds numbers
of Rey = 5066 and Rey = 795, respectively. The cube faces are folded out and
projected in one plane. It is important to note that the isotherm maps for Rey
= 5066 are all symmetric. Asymmetric effects caused by buoyancy induced fluid
motion are not observed from these plots. Even the inter-obstacle regions which
have flow recirculations, places where the local velocity drops to zero and where

6.2.2 Local convective heat transfer

The normalised adiabatic cube temperatures ((Taa—Tin)/Tin, with Tag the adiabatic
temperature and T;, the inlet temperature) are depicted in Fig. 6.18 for the first six
cubes in the array as a function of the Reynolds number. This adiabatic temperature
was measured in a different experiment. The increased air temperature, as a result
of the heat generation in the upstream cubes, caused a monotonic increase in the
adiabatic temperature. Larger Reynolds numbers caused larger rates of cooling and
convective transport and thus, caused lower adiabatic temperatures. The remarkable
shape of the curve for Rey = 795 was caused by mixed convection. The lower
adiabatic temperature for cube 6 compared to that for cube 5 is because of the
vertical motion of thé fluid directing cold fluid to the cube. The normalised adiabatic
cube temperature for natural convection (Rey = 0) was 0.23 for all cubes in the
row.

The heat transfer coefficient distribution along path ABCDA is given in Fig. 6.19
as a function of the six cube positions and parametric in the Reynolds number. Local

=== [PDF page 177] ===
6.2. Convective heat transfer 175

SIDE (N) FRONT SIDE (8) REAR

A

location on path

FIGURE 6.19: Heat transfer coefficients along cross-section ABCDA for cubes 1-6 parametric in
the Reynolds number: o Rey = 795, x Rey = 2086, A Rey = 3278 and O Rey = 5066.

=== [PDF page 178] ===
176 Chapter 6. Array of 9 cubes

heat transfer coefficients along path ABCD are given in Fig. 6.20. The uppermost
plot corresponds to the leading cube, the lowest plot to the sixth cube. Buoyancy
effects, induced by the temperature difference between the heated cubes and the
surrounding air, are of negligible importance for the three largest Reynolds numbers
studied. For these situations, symmetric profiles were found on the faces of all cubes.

The local heat transfer phenomena at the top and the side faces of the leading
cube are dominated by the top and the side vortices. These features are most
prevalent for the largest Reynolds number. These vortices were caused by the flow
separation at the leading top and side edges. Similarly as for the single cube case, a
minimum was found at the front half of the top and side faces underneath the vortices
in question. Flow reattachment at the back half of these faces close to the trailing
edges caused the heat transfer enhancement. The low-temperature oncoming flow
impinged at the front face (partition BC) of the leading cube, consequently leading
to a high level of heat transfer. Close to the channel wall, the local minimum at
this front face is explained by the rotating horseshoe vortex, see Fig. 6.20, similarly
as for the single cube case.

The convective heat transfer from the top and side faces of the second cube,
governed by the attached boundary layer flow, is characterised by a strong decay
in the direction of the rear edge. The relatively high heat transfer coefficients are
caused by the reattaching horseshoe vortex and the main flow. The concave profiles
in the heat transfer coefficients at the east (front) face are explained by the counter-
rotating extensions of the arch-shaped vortex which envelopes the gap between the
leading and the second cube. The heat transfer at the rear face is uniform at the
cross-section at z/H = 0.5, however, a local minimum, accompanied by a small
local maximum, is found at the upper edge. These local extrema are caused by the
rotating arch-shaped vortex. The convective heat transfer at the lateral side faces
for the subsequent cubes is dictated by boundary layer flow. Along the surface,
the heat transfer decays in the downstream direction. Aliaga et al. [5, 6] observed
similar behaviour at the top face of multiple wall-mounted ribs in a fully developed
channel flow. The heat transfer at the front face is still characterised by concave
profiles, caused by the arch vortex. The local extrema at the rear face also remain.
No significant differences are observed downstream of the third protrusion.

Significant buoyancy effects appear for Rey = 795, classifying the situation as
mixed convection. The temperature-driven buoyancy flow is relatively strong in the
corridors between the subsequent cubes. The arch-shaped vortex, which was found
for the forced convection dominated flow situations (the three largest Reynolds num-
bers studied), broke up because of the induced flow motion directed perpendicular
to the main flow. The resulting flow pattern caused an increase in the heat transfer
at the south-front-edge, decaying in the downstream direction along C-D (south
face) and in the opposite direction of gravity along C-B (back face), see Fig. 6.19.
The opposite behaviour occurs at the opposing face, although the effects are weaker.
This can also be seen in Fig. 6.19. These buoyancy effects become more pronounced
for the downstream protrusions. The induced buoyancy flow appears also from the
isotherm plots, shown in Fig. 6.17. Differences in temperature gradients indicate
the direction of the flow.

6.2.3 Averaged convective heat transfer

The surface-averaged heat transfer coefficients, derived similarly as for the single
cube experiments, for the five separate faces are shown in Fig. 7.22 as a function of
the cube location and parametric in the Reynolds number. As is usually the case,
the largest Reynolds number caused the largest average convective heat transfer
coefficients and vice versa. The oncoming flow impinges at the front face of the
leading cube and, hence, results in a large heat transfer coefficient. The front of
the second cube is exposed to the arch-shaped vortex. This vortex is continuously
supplied with cold air from the horseshoe vortex and the main stream with the
consequence of relatively high cooling rates. This also applies to the subsequent
cube downstream in the array. The convective heat transfer at the rear face of the
first cube is relatively high because of the prevalent arch-shaped vortex between the
first two cubes. The heat transfer at the rear faces of the second and third protrusion
is much smaller, which is explained by the decreasing impact of the bounded vortices
further downstream. The strong flow reversals at the lateral faces of the first cube
cause a notable minimum in the. convective heat transfer. In contrast, the second
cube is exposed to the reattached flow from the horseshoe vortex and main stream
and, therefore, its convective heat transfer rate is significantly higher. 'This feature
appears at the three highest Reynolds numbers. The impact of the horseshoe vortex
diminishes for the downstream cubes, which results from the lower heat transfer

Substantial variation in the averaged heat transfer coefficients is found for the

=== [PDF page 183] ===
6.2. Convective heat transfer 181

first three protrusions in the array. After the third cube, the flow becomes grad-
ually fully developed and no noticeable variation in the convective heat transfer is
observed. This is the consequence of using the concept of the adiabatic heat transfer
coefficient. The small deviations for the remaining cubes, as appear from the plots,
are attributed to experimental uncertainty.

The average convective heat transfer for the mixed convection case, Rey = 795,
differs somewhat from the others. In particular, for the cubes further downstream
in the array, the heat transfer benefits from the buoyancy-induced vertical fluid
motion, which results in an enhancement of the heat transfer coefficient.

The heat transfer coefficients averaged over all five cube faces are given in
Fig. 6.22 as a function of the cube location. Even at cube level, the recirculative flow-
field around the leading cube dictates a pronounced minimum. The second cube has
a maximum cube-averaged heat transfer coefficient because of the interaction with
the horseshoe vortex and the main flow. Anderson and Moffat [8] performed cube-
average heat transfer experiments for a regular matrix of wall-mounted aluminium
cubes in a channel flow. The spacing between the cubes was 2 cube widths, ie.,
S,/H = 3. The results of their study are also plotted in Fig. 6.22 for Rey = 2980
and Rey = 6656 at a channel-height-to-cube-height ratio of D/H = 4.6. Although a
somewhat different geometry was studied, their results are in good agreement with
the present findings. They also found that the convective heat transfer is minimum
for the leading cube and maximum for the second cube.

Fig. 6.23 gives the Reynolds number dependence on the heat transfer coefficient
in log coordinates. The cube-averaged heat transfer coefficients versus the Reynolds
number, based on the cube height and bulk velocity, are shown pararnetric in the
cube location. The correlation Nu « Re”, which usually represents forced convec-
tive heat transfer problems, seems to be applicable only to the two largest Reynolds
numbers studied.

An integral analysis was conducted to estimate the conductive heat losses through
the interface between the channel wall and cube and through the lead wires. The
supplied power, determined from the measured voltage and current, is depicted in
Fig. 6.24. The power supply to the second cube is maximum for the three largest
cooling rates, which confirms the observations made above. Since the main stream
is preheated by upstream cubes, a continuous decrease in the supplied power is en-
countered in the downstream direction. The power supply for Rey = 795 differs
from that of the other cases. It is maximum for the leading cube and, after a de-
crease in the downstream direction, a slight increase is observed. The power supply
for Rey = 1589 is added to illustrate the transition regime between Rey = 795 and
Rey = 2086. Also for this cooling rate, the power supply was slightly larger for the
second cube, but the transition is obvious. The heat losses in terms of percentage
of the power supply range between approximately 25% for Rey = 795 and 10% for
Rey = 5066.

184 Chapter 7. Tandem of two cubes

The fully developed channel flow was achieved by tripping the flow at 2 m up-
stream of the cube. Fully development of the flow was demonstrated from the
velocity profiles across the channel height at different streamwise locations. Profiles
of the mean velocity % and the Reynolds normal stresses wu? are given in Fig. 7.2
for different bulk velocities and different streamwise locations in the channel. The
cubes were temporarily removed during these measurements. The four velocity pro-
files at subsequent streamwise locations (z/H = -13.3, -6.7 0 and 13.3) but for a
constant Reynolds number show that the channel flow was fully developed. The
other two profiles refer to a lower and a higher bulk velocity. The negligible dis-

FIGURE 7.2: Profiles of the mean velocity and the Reynolds normal stresses. for a bulk velocity
of ug = 3.95 m/s: (+) 2/H =0, (0) 2/H =-13.3, (x) 2/H = 13.3, (A) 2/H = -6.7. Profile for
ug = 4.77 m/s: (O) 2/H = -6.7. Profile for ug = 2.83 m/s: (0) 2/H = -6.7.

crepancies between the velocity data for the different locations confirmed the fully
developed character of the flow. The integration of the four coinciding velocity pro-
files provided the average flow rate from which the bulk velocity of ug = 3.9 m/s
was derived. The corresponding Reynolds number was Rey = 3900.

All the flow measurements with the tandem in the fully developed channel flow
were performed at Rey = 3900. Some additional frequency measurements were
performed for a tandem configuration on a flat plate in a developing turbulent
boundary layer flow. The Reynolds number for these measurements was based
on the free stream velocity, U,,, and had a value of Rey = 21,500. To avoid
misinterpretation, we will explicitly indicate in the text when these measurements
are discussed.

The local structure of the mean flow and the turbulence fields will have a sig-
nificant effect on the distributions of the local convective heat transfer. The aero-
dynamic interference between the two cubes gives rise to quasi-periodic phenomena
which may lead to an augmentation or degradation of the local and the averaged
convective heat transfer. -The vortex shedding at the lateral faces of the leading
cube caused flow periodicities in its wake that may either be attenuated or ampli-
fied by the interaction with the flow structure around the downstream cube. The
distance between the cubes is an important parameter for this time-dependent flow
behaviour. Flow interference may occur over a range of distances. When the spac-

=== [PDF page 187] ===
7.1. In-line tandem 185

ing approaches zero, the mutual flow interaction vanishes and the resulting obstacle
is considered to be an extended cube of size 2H. In the opposite limit, when the
distance approaches infinity, flow interference also vanishes and the cubes behave
like two aerodynamically independent cubes. The convective heat transfer from
both cubes may either be augmented or degraded by the flow field interaction. The
oscillatory behaviour of the reattaching flow may locally augment the convective
heat transfer. On the other hand, flow interference may cause heat to be convected
slowly away in areas where the time-mean velocities are low.

7.1 In-line tandem

7.1.1 Mean flow structure

Oil-film visualisations were performed for various streamwise distances to qualita-
tively examine the influence of the distance on the flow structure around the cubes.
The results of these visualisations showed that the three distances S,/H =: 2, 4
and 8 incorporated the most significant aerodynamic aspects. It was decided to
document only these three cases with the LDA system.

S,/H = 2

An oil-film visualisation of the surface flow pattern is given in Fig. 7.3 for 5,/H
= 2. The Reynolds number was Rey = 7000, based on the cube height and the
bulk velocity. It was confirmed that this Reynolds number was representative for
the Reynolds number range of interest.

FIGURE 7.3: Oil-film visualisation of the surface flow pattern of an in-line tandem arrangement of
cubes for S;/H = 2 (Rey = 7000).

A horseshoe vortex formed upstream of the front face of the leading cube and was
deflected downstream along the sides. The shear layer separated at the top leading
edge of the upstream cube and reattached at the top face of the downstream cube.
The pigment pattern at the top face of the leading cube clearly shows the imprint
of the resulting bound vortex. The inter-obstacle wake is characterised by an arc-
shaped vortex, confined by the side flow and the separating shear layer. Its imprint
appeared as the nodal pigment concentrations in the inter-obstacle spacing. Such
a wake vortex was also observed for the downstream cube. The oncoming flow
impinged on the front face of the leading cube, separated at the side leading edges
and caused vortex tubes to exist along the side edges of the leading cube. Their
imprints appear as the nodal pigment concentrations at the channel floor, close
to the leading edge. The corresponding LDA measurements are shown in Fig. 7.4

S,/H = 8

The LDA results for S,/H = 8 are given in Fig. 7.9 and Fig. 7.10 for respectively the
flow around the first and second cube in the tandem arrangement. The shear layer
reattached at the channel floor in the inter-obstacle spacing at approximately 2/H
= 2.5. The flow around the leading cube is again characterised by the vortex tubes
at the side, the bound vortex at the top (observed from the pigment concentrations
at the top face in Fig. 7.11) and the wake vortex. The relative large distance between
the cubes allowed the flow to recover in a time-averaged sense as is observed from
the mean flow field after the flow reattachment at the channel floor. However,
the upstream protrusion disturbed the flow which led to a rather high level of the
turbulence intensity as experienced by the downstream obstacle. The mean flow
and turbulence fields around the downstream cube exhibit a large similarity with
those around the upstream cube, but the recirculations were generally smaller. This
is in agreement with earlier observations made in chapter 5 for the single cube test.
case.

diminishes for a large value of S,/H, asymptoting to a single cube situation in the
case of an infinite distance. These measurements have already been discussed.

is given in Fig. 7.12. The visualisation was performed with three-dimensional light
illumination from a stroboscope. The pulsed illumination freezes in fact the time-
dependent flow pattern. [umination with a stroboscope can be regarded as a kind
of phase-averaging to detect coherent structures in the flow. In particular in the
wake of the leading cube, the flow appeared to be very instable. The technique

thin turbulent boundary layer at Rey = 21,500 are indicated with A and OQ, for a
cube size of respectively H = 2.5 and 5.0 cm. Although these measurements also
show a minimum frequency at a gap size of S,/H = 3.0-3.2, the Strouhal numbers
are significantly higher than those for the fully developed channel flow. The small
shift in the critical distance, for which the shedding frequency becomes minimum,
is most likely due to flow structure differences around the cube, for example the
curvature of the separated shear layer. A minimum in frequency was also observed
by Takeuchi and Matsumoto [163, 164, 165] who studied the interference between
two square cylinders suspended in a uniform free stream. They found a critical gap
space of S,/H = 3. It is interesting to note that despite totally different oncoming
flow conditions were encountered, the agreement is remarkably good, suggesting
that the mechanism of vortex shedding is dominated by inviscid flow effects.
Non-dimensionalised shedding frequencies measured in the wake of the second
cube (with H = 2.5 cm) are indicated with (©) in Fig. 7.13. It is observed that
the shedding frequencies in the wake of the first and the second cube are the same,
at least within the experimental uncertainty in determining the shedding frequency.
We note that the frequencies behind the two cubes were not measured simultane-
ously. Observed differences in the frequencies as seen in Fig. 7.13 are most likely
due to experimental artifacts, for example duplicating S,, between two separate ex-
periments. These small differences are amplified in the vicinity of the critical region
because of the strong sensitivity of the frequency to spacing. The similarity never-

=== [PDF page 195] ===
7.1. In-line tandem 193

theless indicates that the vortex shedding of both cubes is frequency locked. At the
moment it is not clear if a phase shift exists between the two shedding mechanisms.

7.1.3 Convective heat transfer

The heat transfer measurements were performed at Rey = 3900. The streamwise
distance S,/H was varied between S,/H = 0.5 and oo while the spanwise distance
was kept at zero (S,/H = 0). Both cubes were separately powered to enable the
use of the inlet flow temperature as the reference temperature (if both cubes were
powered, the effect of upstream heating needed to be taken into account which
involved a different experiment to determine the actual adiabatic temperature of
the cube in question).

Distributions of the local convective heat transfer

Distributions of the heat transfer coefficient for the leading cube in the in-line tan-
dem are given in Fig. 7.14 for S,/H = 2, 4 and 8. The distributions along two paths
are shown: path ABCDA denotes a plane parallel to the channel floor at half a cube
height y/H = 0.5; path ABCD denotes a plane perpendicular to the channel floor
at z/H = 0.5. It is noteworthy that the h-profiles are symmetric around the plane
of symmetry, indicated with the dotted lines in the upper plot in Fig. 7.14.

Although a large spatial variation in the local heat transfer coefficient is observed,
the differences between the results for the different distances (S,/H = 2, 4 and 8)
are too small to attribute to physical phenomena. The profiles of the heat transfer
coefficient at the top and side faces show a marked minimum and maximum, located
at the front half and the back half of these faces, respectively. For all three values
of S/H, a vortex tube was observed to exist at the side faces and a bound vortex
on the top of the cube. The heat removed from the hot cube faces recirculated
in the vortex and caused the local fluid temperature to increase. This caused the
convective heat transfer to decrease which led to the observed minimum in h. The
intermittent reattachment of the cold shear layer close to the trailing edges benefited
the convective heat transfer.

From the previous discussion of the mean flow and turbulence fields it can be
concluded that only minor differences existed in the flow structure in the vicinity of
the leading cube. The distributions of the heat transfer coefficients for S,/H = 2,
4 and 8 support this conclusion. Since these three cases are assumed to cover the
different types of flow, it is unlikely that distributions of the heat transfer coefficient
for other spacings will differ greatly from those presented here for S,/H = 2, 4 and
8.

Distributions of h along path ABCDA for the second cube in the in-line tan-
dem arrangement are given Fig. 7.15 as a function of S,/H. The distributions are
separated in two plots to increase the clarity of the results. It is seen from the
figures that the convective heat transfer at the back (west) face is hardly affected
by a variation in S,/H. This is expected since the wake vortex of the second cube
was more or less similar for all the considered in-line tandems. Further, the shape
of these heat transfer coefficient distributions is also very similar to the those of

in the minimum in A, close to the leading edge, followed by a slight increase in h.

reattached more or less at the side edge of the downstream cube; it reattached in the
inter-obstacle spacing at c/H = 2.5 for S,/H = 4 and larger. The flow structure
around the upstream and downstream cube were more or less similar for S,/H > 8.
The wake flow of the upstream cube showed oscillatory behaviour due to the shed
vortices behind its side faces. The frequency of vortex shedding was modulated by
the aerodynamic interaction with the downstream cube resulting in a dependency
on the streamwise distance S,/H. The vortex shedding frequency was minimum
for S,/H = 2.9. Dependent on S,/H, the shed vortices reattach more or less
periodically at the side faces of the downstream cube, presumably causing the time-
dependent heat flux to fluctuate also periodically. This coupling was demonstrated
for geometries other than wall-mounted cubes [146, 147, 148, 155, 99, 142] and,
therefore, expected in this case to be present as well.

Measurements of the time-resolved convective heat flux were performed with the
active heat flux sensor (see chapter 3}. The two dynamic calibrations described in
chapter 3 ensured a sufficiently high frequency-response in the order of magnitude
of at least 200 Hz. The absolute value of the heat flux signal was not calibrated
because of the time-dependent heat losses which were significantly large but difficult
to estimate correctly. Therefore, we will only focus on the frequency information
contained by the time-dependent convective heat flux. The sensor was flush mounted
in the side face of a cube of formaldehyde. Formaldehyde was chosen since it was
easy to machine and it had a low thermal conductivity of A = 0.33 W/mK (see
appendix A). The data acquisition and the sensor controlling were similar to that
of the frequency-response calibration, see chapter 3.

Simultaneously, a hot-wire anemometer was used to measure the spanwise ve-
locity in the wake of the upstream cube. The vortex shedding frequency was then
obtained from the corresponding power density spectra. A typical example is shown
in Fig. 7.18 for S,/H = 3 and ug = 2 m/s. The spectrum was based on 500,000 data

A periodic heat flux is only present when the sensor is subject to oscillatory

Strouhal number for this case was St = 0.095. This value is also in good agreement
with shedding frequency results, as shown in Fig. 7.21.

The noise level in the hot-wire signals was rather high due to the limited num-
ber of samples used to calculate the spectrum. Further, the hot-wire probe was
positioned in an area where the amplitude of the oscillating flow was small. Since
the vortex shedding frequency was clear from the spectrum, we did no attempt to
improve the signal-to-noise ratio. However, the noise level in the heat: flux signals
was large due to the small signal. The periodic variation in the heat flux signal

=== [PDF page 202] ===
200 . Chapter 7. Tandem of two cubes

was only millivolts while the dc-component was in the order of magnitude of volts.
Further the sensor area was not infinitely small but 4 x 4 mm? which caused a
spatial averaging of the event.

7.1.4 Averaged heat transfer coefficients

In an attempt to correlate the average heat transfer coefficients with the observed
minimum in the wake shedding frequency of the leading cube, the face-averaged heat
transfer coefficients of the downstream cube, determined from the face-averaged con-
vective heat flux and surface temperature, are plotted versus S,/H in Fig. 7.22. The
average heat transfer coefficient of the side and top faces exhibits a small minimum
around S,/H = 3. This minimum occurred at the critical distance for which the
shedding frequency was minimum and it strongly suggests that this minimum is
correlated to the shedding frequency and/or mean flow structure. At the moment,
it is not clear if the minimum in A is directly caused by the frequency of vortex
shedding or that both minima (in the frequency and the heat transfer coefficient)
are caused by the local flow structure. A plausible argument for the former pos-
sibility, a direct correlation, is that the quasi-periodic flow to and from the walls
augments the convective transport of heat, as for example occurs in intermittent
flow reattachment. It is noted that the causality between the minimum in the shed-
ding frequency and the convective heat transfer is very weak as appears from the
minor differences. Further, the cube-averaged heat transfer coefficient, which was
determined from the cube-averaged heat flux and surface temperature, shows also
a small minimum around S,/H = 3. The face-averaged heat transfer at the east
and west face is more or less constant around S,/H = 3 and apparently, the side
and top faces contribute markedly to the cube-averaged heat transfer. The dip in h
at S,/H = 5 is explained by experimental uncertainty and a possible variation in
the averaged flow rate. The latter explanation was made plausible by plotting the
supplied power, the product of the voltage and current, versus S,/H. The results
indicated with (0), given in Fig. 7.23, clearly show a dip in the supplied power at
S,/H = 5. The second measurement series of the supplied power, obtained from an
independent measurement and indicated with (A) in Fig. 7.23, shows a monotonic
increase up to S,/H = 6, indicating that the local minimum around S,/H = 5 is an
experimental artifact. The figure further shows a minimum in the supplied power
around S,/H = 3, which supports the previous conclusion. In general, the varia-
tions in the face-averaged and cube-averaged heat transfer coefficient as a function
of S/H are rather small compared to the variations in the local heat transfer coef-
ficient. Besides the minimum around S,/H = 3, the only marked variation is found
for the leeward face (west) for which h slightly increases with increasing distances.
The averaged heat transfer coefficient for the other faces decreases slightly.

The off-axis arrangements of two cubes were also placed in the fully developed
channel flow. While the long-term averaged flow field and the convective heat

transfer distributions were symmetric in the in-line tandem cases, the results for
the staggered arrangements were significantly asymmetric for some combinations of
the streamwise and the spanwise distance. For either a large streamwise or spanwise
distance, no significant asymmetry of the long-term averaged flow field is expected
and, therefore, these arrangements represent more or less the flow around a single
cube. But for relatively small streamwise and spanwise distances, the flow will be
significantly directed by the downstream staggered cube which results in asymme-
try of both the averaged flow pattern and the convective heat transfer. Oil-film

=== [PDF page 204] ===
202 Chapter 7. Tandem of two cubes

and smoke visualisation experiments were performed for a large range of streamwise
and spanwise distances (S,/H and S,/H) to qualitatively study the effect of off
axis arrangements on the averaged flow structure. From these observations, three
cases were selected to be documented quantitatively with the LDA-system, namely:
S,/H =2and S,/H =1; S,/H =4 and S,/H =1; 8,/H =4 and S,/H = 2. The
results showed that the flow structures around both cubes generally resembled much
of the features found for the in-line tandem arrangements of cubes. However, for
some configurations the flow asymmetry caused significant departures from these.
Heat transfer measurements were performed for a larger series of streamwise and
spanwise distances, ie. S,/H = 2 and S,/H = 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2 and 3; $,/H = 4 and
S,/H = 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2 and 3; S,/H = 8 and S,/H = 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2 and 3. Both
the convective heat transfer and the flow field measurements were performed at the
Reynolds number of Rey = 3900, which corresponded to the bulk velocity of ug =
3.9 m/s.

For the interpretation of the off-axis results, we will distinguish between the
inner and outer side face of the leading and the downstream cube. The outer side
face is the face pointing outwards the tandem, both inner side faces (of the upstream
and downstream cube) point to each other. See the sketch of the plan view of the
off-axis tandem arrangement given in Fig. 7.1.

7.2.1 Mean flow structure

The first off-axis configuration discussed had a streamwise and spanwise distance
of S,/H = 2 and S,/H = 1, respectively. An oil-film visualisation of the surface
streaklines is given in Fig. 7.24. The overall pigment pattern indicate the flow asym-
metry for this slightly staggered configuration. The horseshoe vortex in front of the
leading cube can clearly be seen from the pigment pattern. The symmetric pigment
pattern in this upstream region suggests that the flow impinges perpendicularly on
the front face. This more or less undistorted oncoming flow can also be seen from
the long-term averaged vector plot of the velocity field in a plane at half the cube
height (y/H = 0.5), given in Fig. 7.25. The downstream off-axis obstacle caused
an extra blockage effect which imposed additional flow resistance. While the sep-
arated side shear layer reattached at the outer side wall of the leading cube, and
thus causing a small side vortex to exist close to the leading edge, the inner side
face is covered entirely by a side vortex without flow reattachment at the back half
of the face. This is obviously caused by the direction of the flow as oppressed by
the downstream cube. The extra downstream blockage caused the oncoming main
flow to be more directed towards the inner side face of the first cube. This caused a
large recirculation and, therefore, the absence of flow reattachment at the back half
of the side face. The flow accelerated through the corridor between the cubes. Since
the flow was about 45 ° inclined with respect to the system centre-line, it separated
at the inner side face of the downstream cube and caused the rather large side vor-
tex at this side face (see Fig. 7.25). Further, a big flow recirculation in the wake
of the upstream cube resulted from the inclined oncoming flow. The arch-shaped
vortex, as found for the symmetric flow cases, was then replaced by a one-centre

=== [PDF page 205] ===
7.2. Off-azis tandem arrangement 203

FIGuRE 7.24: Oil-film visualisation of the surface flow pattern of the staggered tandem with S,/H
= 2and $,/H = 1 (Rey = 7000).

wake vortex. The wake of the downstream cube exposed a weird recirculation area.
Here also, due to the asymmetry of the flow, the arch-shaped vortex was replaced
by a one-centre recirculation.

From the oil-film visualisation it is seen that the extension of the horseshoe
vortex, induced in front of the first cube, interacts with the vortex in front of the
second cube. This caused a strong intensification of the turbulence production which
subsequently will cause heat transfer enhancement.

Strong peaks in the turbulence intensity were found at the interfaces between the
side vortices and the shear layers for both cubes. Both the intermittent flow reat-
tachment due to flow instabilities (at the outer and inner side face of the upstream
and the downstream cube) and strong shearing are mentioned as causes for the large
values of the turbulent kinetic energy. This is observed from the iso-value plot of
k, given in Fig. 7.26 for a plane at half a cube height y/H = 0.5. Further, the free
mixing layers behind the lateral faces have relatively large values of the turbulent
kinetic energy. This is also due to the strong shearing and the vortex shedding from
the side faces. The vortices in the wake of both cubes are rather stable, as is seen
from the low values of the turbulence intensity. The region in front of the cubes
have the lowest level of &. Since the plane considered (y/H = 0.5) does not intersect
with the horseshoe vortices found upstream of both cubes, an increased turbulence
level as usually found in the horseshoe vortex is not visible from the plot.

The influence of the off-axis placed downstream cube on the time-averaged flow
field diminishes for a larger streamwise distance. This can clearly be seen from the
oil-film visualisation given in Fig. 7.27 and from the vector plot of the velocity field in
the plane at y/H = 0.5, shown in Fig. 7.28 for the tandem with a larger streamwise
distance (S,/H = 4), but the same spanwise distance (S,/H = 1). The flow around

7.2.2 Distributions of the local heat transfer coefficient

Because the local heat transfer characteristics and their associated flow features
were already discussed in detail for the in-line tandem, the discussion in this section

FIGURE 7.33: Heat transfer coefficient distributions along path ABCD (upper plot) and along path

will primarily be on the asymmetry of the local convective heat transfer. Significant
asymmetry effects were detected in the distributions of the heat transfer coeffi-
cients for both the upstream and downstream cube for small values of $,/H and
S,/H. Profiles of the local heat transfer coefficient are given in Fig. 7.33 along paths
ABCDA and ABCD for the downstream cube in a typical off-axis arrangements with
severe asymmetry effects, i.e. S,/H = 2 and S,/H = 0.5. The dotted lines refer to
the centreline of the cube and denote the eventual symmetry lines (x/H = 0.5). It
is seen that the profiles are significantly asymmetric. The flow acceleration through
the corridor between the two cubes caused the distinct peak in h at the front face.
This flow acceleration was shown for S,/H = 2 and S,/H = 1 (see Fig. 7.25), but
is more pronounced for smaller spanwise distances. The inner side face of the down-
stream cube, indicated with BC in the upper plot in Fig. 7.33, shows a marked
minimum close to the leading edge, caused by the side vortex. The maximum lo-
cated at the middle of this side face is due to flow reattachment. Although these
flow features were observed from the flow field analysis for 5, /H = 2 and S,/H
= 1, similar flow features apply for this case. The heat transfer coefficient profiles
at the outer side is caused by the flow recirculation which enveloped more or less

=== [PDF page 211] ===
7.2. Off-atis tandem arrangement 209

the entire face. The convective heat transfer at the back face of the downstream
cube decays monotonically towards the outer side face of the cube. This direction is
opposite to the local air speed of the rotating wake vortex. The heat removal from
the surface caused an increase in the fluid temperature and, therefore, reduced the
convective heat transfer. The top face (shown in the lower plot in Fig. 7.33) exhibits
more or less similar heat transfer features as for the single cube. The heat transfer
coefficient decays strongly up to approximately 2/H = 1/4, where a local minimum
is found. This is the location of the top vortex. The subsequent flow reattachment
caused the heat transfer to become maximum at about «/H = 1/2.

small which suggests that the overall cooling of the cube is dominated by the bulk
flow. Although severe differences were observed in the distributions of the local heat
transfer coefficient, the cube-averaged heat transfer coefficient apparently is not a
function of the location of this cube with respect to its upstream neighbour. A
similar observation was already made for the leading cube.

=== [PDF page 213] ===
CHAPTER 8

Matrix of cubes

The experimental work discussed in this chapter served as reference dataset for the
validation of numerical simulations at ERCOFTAC workshops held at the Delft
University of Technology, at June 6-7, 1997 [62] and at UMIST, May 28-29, 1998,
Manchester [88]. The usefulness of this test case is directly related to the well
defined periodic boundary conditions involved which can easily be implemented in
numerical simulations. Periodic boundary conditions make simulations much more
reliable, reproducible and comparable. For accurate experimental results and for a
fair validation of the results, it is of crucial importance that the experimental flow
boundary conditions are periodic. In order to achieve this periodicity, we needed at
least 15 rows of cubes to make the flow fully periodic.

An equidistantly-spaced matrix of wall-mounted cubes was placed on the channel
floor of the rectangular windtunnel section of 51 mm height (D = 51 mm, D/H =
3.4) and 600 mm width. A schematic of the configuration is given in Fig. 8.1. The
matrix consisted of a total of 25 x 10 cubes in respectively the streamwise and the
spanwise direction. The inflow conditions were controlled by tripping the boundary
layer approximately 1 m upstream of the first column of cubes. The cube size was
H = 15mm. The local origin of the cube system is defined as shown in Fig. 8.1.
The x, y and z direction are in the streamwise, normal and spanwise direction,
respectively. The streamwise and spanwise distances between the cubes were 45
mm, giving centre-to-centre distances of S,/H = S,/H = S/H = 4.

The flow and heat transfer measurements were carried out around the 18th row
from the inlet, at mid-height of the channel. This location ensured a spatially
periodic flow in both the streamwise and the spanwise direction independent of the
inflow conditions, as was confirmed by velocity measurements at several locations
across the entire matrix (see below). The velocity and Reynolds stress data were
duplicated for several stations to verify the reproducibility of the measurements.
The discrepancies between the different measurements were less than 1%.

The mounting base was machined from formaldehyde which had a low thermal
conductivity of 0.33 W/mK. This low value minimised the conductive heat losses
through the base plate. The flow field was measured with the LDA system for a
Reynolds number of Rey = 3854, based on the cube height and the bulk velocity.
Smoke and oil-film visualisations were performed in a broader range of Reynolds
numbers (1000-8000) to complement the LDA measurements. The convective heat
transfer measurements were performed for the range: 2380 < Rey < 5280.

8.1 Flow field

8.1.1 Spatial periodicity

A typical example which demonstrates the spatial periodicity in the streamwise di-
rection is given in Fig. 8.2. Shown are profiles of the mean streamwise velocity

211

=== [PDF page 214] ===

FicurE 8.1: Three-dimensional sketch of the matrix configuration of cubes on the channel wall
(upper plot} and the side view of the configuration (lower plot).

U/up and the Reynolds normal stresses u?/u?, and w?/u2,, scaled with the bulk
velocity in the channel ug, through the centre of the streamwise corridor at z/H =
2 and at height y/H = 2. It is clearly observed that both the mean velocity and
Reynolds stresses asymptote to a maximum value. After roughly 15 cube rows, the
flow appeared to be spatially periodic which is now referred to as fully developed.
Similar experiments were performed to confirm the spatial periodicity in the span-
wise direction. Profiles of U/ug, U/us, w/uz and u’w'/u?, are given in Fig. 8.3
for a traverse in the z-direction at z/H = 2 and y/H = 0.5. The flow symmetry is
apparent from the profiles.

8.1.2 Consistency validation

A consistency validation was performed from mass conservation through four differ-
ent sub-planes at periodic up- and downstream locations of the cube. These planes
(2/H =-1.5and 2.5, z/H > 0 and 2/H =-1.5 and 2.5, z/H < 0) are centred around
the local symmetry plane z/H = 0 at the middle of the cube in question. The spa-
tially periodic and symmetric situation requires a necessarily equal mass flow rate
through the four sub-planes. For each sub-section, seven velocity profiles in normal
direction were measured at equidistant locations. The profiles were measured at the
following locations: r/H = - 1.5 and 2.5, z/H = 0, + 0.33, + 0.66, + 1, + 1 .33,
+ 1.66 and + 2. The axial mass flow rate was determined from a numerical inte-
gration. The distance between the individual stations (5 mm) was sufficiently small

=== [PDF page 216] ===
214 Chapter 8. Matrix of cubes

to ensure a reasonably accurate estimate of the velocity gradients in the spanwise
direction. The integrations of the velocity profiles yielded an averaged mass flow
rate of 6.85 10-° with deviations less than 1%. These small discrepancies showed
the good quality of the spatial periodicity and the symmetry of the matrix flow. The
averaged velocity, derived from the ratio of the mass flow rate and the integration
area, yielded a value of wg = 3.86 m/s. The Reynolds number based on the bulk
velocity and the cube size was Rey = 3854.

z/H

FIGURE 8.5: Profiles of U/ug (first) and @/ug (second) in the spanwise direction at #/H = 1.3
and y/H = 0.5. The markers (0) refer to the experiments (EXP), the solid and dashed lines refer

June 6-7, 1997 [62] and at UMIST, May 28-29, 1998, Manchester [88]) for two dif
ferent stations. The periodic flow boundary conditions and the different flow types
involved (flow recirculations, separation and reattachment) made this test case very
suitable for an objective evaluation of different turbulence models and computa-
tional methods. Verstappen and Veldman [170, 171] performed a kind of Direct
Numerical Simulation (DNS) which was based on a high-order, spectro-consistent
discretisation method. The results of Mathey et al. [106] are large eddy simulations
(LES). These methods were allowed due to the relatively low Reynolds number of
the flow. Fig. 8.4 shows profiles of Z/ug, w?/u2, and w/u% in the normal direction
at z/H = 0 and 2/H = 0.3. Profiles of @/ug, U/ug, u?/uz, w?/u% and uw! /ur,
are given in Figs. 8.5 and 8.6 for a traverse in the spanwise direction at 2/H =
1.3 and y/H = 0.5. It is observed that the numerical results, the averaged veloci-
ties as well as the Reynolds stresses, are in good agreement with the experimental
results. Even the strong peaks in the Reynolds stresses are well predicted by the
numerical simulations. Besides that this good agreement is a complimenting result
for the computers, it also indicates that the LDA measurements were accurately
performed. The numerical results show that LES and DNS are promising methods
to accurately resolve this kind of bluff-body flows.

8.1.4 General flow structure

A three-dimensional impression of the flow field around the cube was derived from
the LDA-measurements and flow visualisations (see Fig. 8.7). Distinct vortex struc-

tures were only observed in the immediate proximity of the obstacle, while the core
flow in the region above the cubes and in the streamwise corridors between them
remained almost undistorted. The flow in the inter-obstacle space is characterised
by a two-cell structure arising from the intermittent reattachment of the shear layer
separated at the leading top edge of the upstream cube. The long-term averaged
flow reattachment occurred at a distance of approximately 1.5 cube heights (2/H
= 2.5) from the leeward face. This shear layer bounds the arc-shaped vortex in the
wake of the upstream cube. A horseshoe type of vortex was formed at the windward
face of the cube and was deflected downstream along both sides of the cube, weak-
ening further downstream through the interaction with the counter-rotating vortex
along the neighbouring cubes.

A vector plot of the velocity field in the symmetry plane z/H = 0 is given in
Fig. 8.8. The plot is a collection of results obtained from two different measure-
ment techniques. The vectors, located in the two triangular areas upstream and

geometrical constraints imposed by the prism-cube, the normal velocities could only
be measured in the limited triangular regions. The other vectors were obtained with
the reconstruction technique from two separate velocity measurements acquired at
different inclination angles of the laser probe. Although the results obtained with
the reconstruction technique were less accurate than those acquired with the prism-
cube, the agreement between the results in the overlapping region provides sufficient
evidence of the reliability of the reconstruction technique. The vortex structures can
be readily identified from the vector plot in Fig. 8.8. A downwash of the flow, orig-
inating from the horseshoe vortex, is characterised by negative velocities in the

=== [PDF page 220] ===
218 Chapter 8. Matriz of cubes

FIGURE 8.9: Oil-film visualisation of the surface streaklines of the flow in the matrix flow. It looks
like that the oil-film pattern is not fully developed but this was caused by the lack of pigment
upstream of the cube when the experiment was performed.

proximity of the windward cube wall. The wake flow is dominated by the arch vor-
tex, causing a strong upwash close to the leeward face. The velocity vectors close to
the top leading edge, Fig. 8.8, indicate that the flow separated and caused a recir-
culation bubble to exist in the vicinity of the leading top edge. Although it was not
possible to measure this with the used LDA set-up, the flow reversal was apparent
from the surface streak lines of the oil-film visualisations given in Fig. 8.9. The top
face shows pigment removal! close to the leading edge which originated from the re-
circulating flow. Flow reattachment was found at approximately 4 mm downstream
of the leading edge, as appears from the more or less smeared pigment pattern.

A further analysis of the visualisation in Fig. 8.9 reveals the imprint of the
horseshoe-type vortex in front of the obstacle as the white line, bending around the
cube along the separation line. This vortex joined the horseshoe vortex generated
upstream in the corridor region, see Fig. 8.9. The influence of the extensions of other
horseshoe vortices, induced in front of the cubes more upstream, has diminished as
is seen from the oil-film visualisation. At 2/H = 0.5, both co-rotating horseshoe
vortices interacted with each other but did not fully merge. If they are of comparable
strength, the interaction at this location leads to negative normal velocities close
tothe obstacle side face, positive values at the symmetry line z/H = 2 and zero
values at the interface. This hypothesis is supported by the v-measurements given
in Fig. 8.10 in the yz-plane at z/H = 0.5. Note that the traverses shown were
measured with the prism cube. Also for this orientation, the measurement was
geometrically constrained. Negative velocities were found around z/H = 0.5 to 1
due to the first horseshoe vortex, which decreased gradually with an increase in
height y/H. Positive velocities are observed around z/H = 2 due to the extensions
of the upstream horseshoe vortex with zero velocities around z/H = 1.4 due to the

superposition of the two co-rotating vortices. Because of the flow symmetry, the
effects of the two extensions of the upstream horseshoe vortex are superimposed,
resulting in an intensification of the effect.

Vector plots of the streamwise and spanwise velocity components u and w with
superimposed trajectories are given in Fig. 8.11 for one half of a spatial! period in the
matrix for y/H = 0.25, 0.50 and 0.75. The flow symmetry was verified with velocity
measurements across the entire xz-plane at different stations showing negligible
differences. This symmetry allowed us to concentrate on only one symmetric half of
the spatially periodic domain. An almost undistorted corridor region between the
cube arrays is obvious from the plots. The oncoming flow is bended towards the cube
sides to separate at the leading side face, creating a small recirculation bubble close
to the leading side edge. As a consequence, the separated shear layer reattaches at
approximately 5 mm downstream from the side edge. This was demonstrated from
measurements of the streamwise component at 0.5 mm distance from the wall (z/H
= 8/7.5). These measurements show a negative velocity up to 5 mm distance (2/H
= 1/3) from the leading edge, Fig. 8.12. The side vortices are aligned along the
leading side edges with their origins at the channel floor. Their imprints are seen
from the oil-film picture as the nodal pigment accumulation close to the leading side
edge (see Fig. 8.9). The oil-film pattern at the side faces (not shown in the thesis)
showed a white feather-type imprint of the side recirculation close to the leading
edge. This structure is qualitatively similar to that found for a single cube in a
developing turbulent channel flow (see Fig. 5.18).

The wake flow shows the recirculation of the arc-type of vortex. The results
at the three different levels y/H show a gradual growth of the recirculation area
towards the channel wall. An extrapolation of the separation line, where the velocity
changes sign, gives a reattachment point at 2/H = 2.5. The oil-film visualisation
in the wake of the cube indicates two pigment concentrations which refer to two
counter-rotating foci. These vorticity concentrations are the imprints of the wake

Figure 8.12: Profile of the mean velocity @ at y/H = 0.5 and z/H = 8/7.5.

vortex and its location agrees reasonably well with the LDA measurements at y/H
= 0.25, extrapolated towards the channel wall. The shear layer reattached in the
inter-obstacle spacing at x/H = 2.5, as observed from the pigment accumulation.
This location is in good agreement with the LDA observations. The three vector
plots show that the centres of the two recirculations are at about the same location.
If the wake vortex was closed, the centres of the recirculations would shift towards
the symmetry line for cross-sections at increasing height y/H. This shift is not
observed which suggests that the wake vortex is not closed at its top. The results
indicate that the wake vortex consists of two separate spirally-winded vortices with
origins at the channel floor and that their endings are diffused in the downstream
free shear layer.

The vector plots further show that the streamlines in the inter-obstacle space
upstream of the cube are more or less parallel and undistorted for y/H = 0.75,
with exception of the wake vortex region and close to the windward face, whilst
the streamlines are significantly curved at the plane y/H = 0.25. This curvature
indicates that much air is advected away along the side whilst at higher locations,
much more air flows over the obstacle top.

The co-rotating horseshoe vortices can hardly be seen from the u-component at
y/H = 0.25 since no local acceleration in the streamwise direction is generated by
this vortex structure. This means that the vortex is advected away with the velocity
of the bypassing flow.

8.1.5 Dynamic behaviour: vortex shedding

Flow instabilities which are initiated at the side faces caused the flow to fluctuate
in a periodic manner. These coherent structures in the wake flow were detected
from power density spectra of the time series of the spanwise velocity component.
Since the flow was highly turbulent, vortex shedding occurred at a broader range
of frequencies. The dominant characteristic frequency was derived from the lo-
cation in the spectrum which corresponded to the maximum energy. Long-time
averaged spectra were obtained from time durations of 500,000 data points which
corresponded to about 20,000-40,000 vortex shedding cycles. This number of real-
izations was required to ensure a sufficiently large signal-to-noise ratio. A slotting,

=== [PDF page 224] ===
222 Chapter 8. Matrix of cubes

sample-and-hold and re-sampling method were compared to justify the accuracy of
the calculated power density spectra, and thus the accuracy of the wake frequen-
cies {1]. The three methods reproduced the wake frequencies within 0.5 Hz. This
value also pertains to the uncertainty of estimating the frequencies from the spectra
and the experimental uncertainty involved. A typical example of a power density
spectrum is given in Fig. 8.13. The coherent structure in the flow (vortex shedding)
is apparent from the increased activity around f = 27 Hz. The dashed line, the
turbulent power density spectrum, indicates the contributions from the incoherent
structures in the flow. The corresponding autocorrelation function calculated

incoherent part (the decaying profile from 1 for a zero time delay to 0 for large time
delays). Typical values of the integral time scale Tu, obtained from the integration
of the autocorrelation function, were of the order of 15 msec. This value was also
derived from the characteristic length (the cube H = 15 mm) and the characteristic
velocity (about 1 m/s). The second time derivative at t=O gives the value for the

Rex

FIGURE 8.15: Strouhal numbers (St = Hf /ug) versus the Reynolds number Rey measured in the
wake of a cube in the spatially periodic matrix flow.

Taylor micro scale which was in the order of magnitude of 1 msec. These values
were more or less similar to the ones found for the isolated cube in a channel flow
(chapter 5).

Wake frequencies were measured for a range of mass flow rates. These frequencies
were made dimensionless with the bulk velocity and the cube size to yield the
Strouhal number, St = Hf/ug. These Strouhal numbers are plotted versus the
Reynolds number in Fig. 8.15. All shown results were determined from 500,000
data points. When an experimental uncertainty of 5% is taken into account, a
constant Strouhal number of St = 0.109 is found for the Reynolds number range
of 2000 < Rey < 6000. The deviations for Rey < 2000 are explained by larger
experimental uncertainty or possible Reynolds number effects.

The value of St = 0.109 agrees well with the Strouhal number measured in the
wake flow of the leading cube in the tandem configuration of cubes placed in a
turbulent boundary layer flow for S,/H = 4 (see Fig. 7.13). The tandem of cubes
placed in the fully developed turbulent channel at the much lower Reynolds number
of Rey = 3900, subject of study in chapter 7, was characterised by a significantly
lower Strouhal number, i.e. about St = 0.08 for 5,/H = 4. The large turbulence
intensity of the oncoming flow in the matrix configuration of cubes is a possible
explanation for this good agreement with the Strouhal number for the turbulent
boundary layer flow. This observation is supported by the conclusion drawn in
chapter 5 that, among others, the increased turbulence level of the oncoming flow
caused a higher shedding frequency for the single cube placed in the high-Reynolds
number fully developed channel flow: compare St = 0.145 for Rey = 40000 [101]
and St = 0.095 for Rey = 3900, chapter 7.

It is noted that the power density spectrum in Fig. 8.13 decays with a power
of —5/3 for the inertial subrange which is usually found in turbulence spectra for
high Reynolds numbers away from the wall. Also note that the noise level becomes
important around the truncation of the spectrum, @ * 0.03.

quantitative comparison. While the streamwise fluctuations u become largest in
the shear layer and horseshoe vortex region, the fluctuations in the spanwise velocity
w” exhibit maxima where the side shear layers interfere, which is approximately
around the reattachment point 2/H = 2.5. The vortex shedding from the lateral
faces caused significant fluctuations in the z-direction. However, the fluctuations
w’ in the wake vortex region are rather small. Apparently, the unstable shedding
behaviour is only prevalent in the shear layer enveloping the arc vortex and is almost
diminished at the centreline z/H = 0. A qualitatively similar maximum in the
spanwise velocity fluctuations around the reattachment point was also observed for
a single wall-mounted cube in a fully developed turbulent channel flow [101]. For
this single cube configuration, the mechanism of vortex shedding contributed also
to the production of turbulence.

The maxima in w? in the vicinity of the top face, where the shear layer sepa-
rates and Subsequently reattaches, are accompanied by a strong streamwise velocity
gradient & , See Fig. 8.8, leading to large values of the streamwise normal stress pro-
duction term u? &. Although the velocity gradient diminishes further downstream
in the shear layer, wu? contributes still significantly to the streamwise normal stress
production.

Measurement of the Reynolds normal stresses v’? with the prism-cube provided
the third component for the turbulent kinetic energy k = 0.5 (wu + v® + w?) in the
triangular region. It is noted that the Reynolds normal stresses v? obtained with the
reconstruction technique were not accurate enough and are therefore, not considered
in this thesis. Values of k are given in Fig. 8.18 in the two triangles in front and in
the wake of the obstacle. In the remaining part of the spatial period, v” is assumed
as v? = 0.5 (u® + w?) leading to kop = 0.75 (w? + w?) where u® and w® were

and 8.17) the agreement between k and kp is sufficiently good (differences are at
maximum 10 %) to allow at least a qualitative interpretation of the turbulent kinetic
energy field with ken.

The turbulent kinetic energy above the cubes (the core region of the channel) is
rather small and increases gradually towards the opposing channel wall. The large
Reynolds normal stresses found in the shear layer region and around the reattach-
ment point lead to local maxima in the turbulent kinetic energy. A maximum in k
was found downstream in the shear layer at approximately one cube distance be-
hind the leeward face where the shear layer is broadening and the turbulence level is
decaying due to turbulent advection. The large values of k in the horseshoe vortex
region at the windward face of the obstacle indicates that this vortex structure is
unstable and highly turbulent. Unstable behaviour of the horseshoe vortex was also
observed for a single cube in a fully developed channel flow [101, 86]. For this single
cube, bimodal but non-periodic behaviour of the velocity components was observed
which was attributed to the flipping between two flow modes. In the case of a spa-
tially periodic matrix flow, no bimodal behaviour was detected from the measured
probability density functions in the horseshoe region.

0.50 and 0.75. The flow impingement at the windward obstacle face caused local
maxima in k, in particular at y/H = 0.50. The increased turbulent kinetic energy
at y/H = 0.25 close to the front face of the cube is attributed to the horseshoe

vortex. Similarly to the top face, the local maxima close to the sides were caused by
the intermittent reattachment of the shear layers which were separated at the sharp
leading side edges. Flow instabilities at these faces are amplified and initiate the
vortex shedding behind the trailing side edges. The high turbulence levels directly
behind these faces are, therefore, explained by the periodically fluctuating velocities.

to the strong curvature of the flow in that region.

8.2 Convective heat transfer

Heat transfer was measured at one powered cube in the spatially periodic matrix
flow, while all other cubes remained unpowered. The inlet temperature was used as
a reference which, in this case, equals the adiabatic surface temperature [113, 114].
The heat transfer measurements were reproduced at different cubes in separate ex-

=== [PDF page 232] ===
230 Chapter 8. Matriz of cubes

periments and the results were well within the 10% uncertainty limit. Heat transfer
experiments were performed in the Reynolds number range of 2380 < Rey < 5280.
The temperature of the copper core was kept at 75 °C’ for all the heat transfer
measurements. The inlet temperature varied between 19-21 °C (the real inlet tem-
perature was measured for each experiment and used for further processing of the
data).

coefficients were derived in a similar way, but the convective heat flux and surface
temperature are then an average over all the five faces of the cube exposed to air

flow. The results are shown in Fig. 8.24 as a function of the Reynolds number
Rey. Despite a small experimental uncertainty, the data follow an almost linear
regression. A large heat transfer coefficient found at the front face is indicative for
the impinging flow at this face. The side and top faces typically have the lowest
averaged convective heat transfer coefficient which is due to the flow reversal, as
shown later. The face-averaged heat transfer at the rear face is the lowest of all
faces. The small deviations in the face-averaged heat transfer coefficients can be
traced back to deviations in the power supply. The sum of the total convective and
radiative heat transfer from the five faces exposed to the air flow is denoted as (+)
in Fig. 8.25 as a function of the Reynolds number Rey. The total power supplied to
the heat source in the cube, which is product of the voltage drop and the current, is
given in the same figure, indicated with (o). The difference between both values is
the heat leakage through the sixth face, the interface between the wall-mounted cube
and the channel wall, and consists of conduction losses through the base plate and:
lead wires. For all considered Reynolds numbers, this heat loss was approximately
10-15 %, where the smaller values pertain to the higher Reynolds numbers and vice
versa.

8.2.2. Local convective heat transfer

FIGURE 8.26: Heat transfer coefficient distributions along paths ABCDA and ABCD as a function
of the Reynolds number Rey: (*} 2851, (0) 3348, (x) 3854, (A) 4321, (CG) 4722 and (©) 5279.

Distributions of the heat transfer coefficient are shown along the paths ABCDA
and. ABCD in Fig 8.26 for different Reynolds numbers. The profiles are normalised
with the cube-averaged heat transfer coefficient for the corresponding Reynolds num-
ber, as given in Fig. 8.24, and collapse within the experimental uncertainty. This
suggests that the local heat transfer characteristics are similar throughout the range
of Reynolds numbers considered (2380 < Rey < 5280). Because of this similarity,
the discussion can be focussed on one characteristic distribution of the local heat
transfer coefficient, related to the characteristic flow field. Fig. 8.26 reveals that
the heat transfer coefficient distributions show large spatial gradients at a relatively
small surface which reflects a complexity of the flow structure around the matrix
with flow recirculations and reattachment. Despite this large variation, the heat
transfer displays a full symmetry indicating that local buoyancy effects are negligi-
ble. The flow is, therefore, classified as forced convection for all Reynolds numbers
considered.

As mentioned earlier, the heat transfer at the windward face (FRONT) benefits

=== [PDF page 235] ===
&.2. Convective heat transfer 233

from the turbulent flow impingement and is maximum compared to the other faces.
The peaks towards the side edges, see Fig. 8.26, are due to a local acceleration of
the fluid, and thus causing a local increase in the heat transfer coefficient. Close to
the mounting wall edge, the heat transfer coefficient shows a local minimum (the
front face in the lower figure of Fig. 8.26) which is traced back to the presence of the
horseshoe vortex. Heat convected away from the hot cube surface recirculates in the
vortex causing the local vortex temperature to rise. This increased temperature level
prevents a beneficial removal of heat at this location. A similar observation was made
for the side faces (SIDES) of the cube. The side recirculations acted as tnsulation
layers and, therefore, caused local minima close to the leading edge. On the contrary,
the fresh air from the intermittent reattaching shear layer at approximately 5 mm
downstream towards the trailing edge caused the local maxima in the heat transfer
coefficient. Similar features were found at the top face, shown in the lower plot
in Fig. 8.26 (TOP). The bound vortex, located in the vicinity of the top leading
edge, caused the heat transfer to become minimum at about the centre of the vortex
core. The subsequent maximum is due to the reattachment of the separated shear
layer. The convective heat transfer from the leeward face is worst compared to the
other faces which is mainly attributed to the wake vortex. The recirculating heat
prevented also a beneficial cooling for this face.
