Part IV

Conclusion

230

=== [PDF page 238] ===


=== [PDF page 239] ===
CHAPTER 9

Concluding discussion

The convective heat transfer from wall-mounted cubes placed in a turbulent channel
flow has been studied in detail for four different test cases which included an isolated
cube, a tandem of cubes, a row of cubes and a matrix of cubes. The research was
mainly based on the experimental results obtained with Laser Doppler Anemom-
etry and infrared thermography. In this chapter, the usefulness and accuracy of
both measurement techniques are briefly discussed. Further, the major flow and
convective heat transfer results of the four different test cases are discussed.

9.1 Infrared surface temperature measurements

Infrared thermography was used to measure the surface temperature distributions
of wall-mounted cubes placed in a turbulent channel flow. A major difficulty in
measuring surface temperature distributions of small three-dimensional bodies was
maintaining a high accuracy and large depth-of-field without reducing the spatial
resolution. This problem was solved by using an infrared image restoration tech-
nique with a Wiener filter which was based on the measured optical transfer function
(OTF). For the application considered, it has been proven that the measured OTF
was independent of the temperature of the scanned targets (temperatures in the
range 30-70 °C’). Further, a depth-of-field of at least + 5mm was ensured by mea-
surements of small out-of-focus step-edge responses. An in situ calibration was
used to account for miscellaneous environmental contributions to the total intensity
received by the camera.

Based on the surface temperature measurements from the independent liquid
crystal technique, it was demonstrated that the temperature measurements which
were not processed with the restoration filter showed systematic discrepancies up to
3 °C. The restored infrared temperatures coincided with the liquid crystal temper-
atures within the claimed experimental uncertainty of 0.4 °C. This result illustrates
the necessity of image restoration in order to ensure a temperature accuracy of 0.4
°C’ for the entire surface of the cubes in question.

If infrared thermography is used without proper calibration and detailed anal-
ysis of the situation in which the infrared system is employed, substantial error
may be encountered in the temperature reading. It is very important to validate
measurements with a detailed accuracy analysis and preferably with additional mea-
surements from a second independent measurement technique.

9.2 Laser Doppler Anemometry measurements

A standard single sample uncertainty analysis was performed to evaluate the ac-
curacy of one velocity realisation with the laser probe in the standard orientation.

237

=== [PDF page 240] ===
238 ; Chapter 9. Concluding discussion

Additional contributions to the error, like misalignment and bias effects, were re-
ported. From this analysis, it was conclided that the averaged velocities were
accurate within 5% while the higher order moments were less accurate (between
7-10%). The results obtained with the prism cube had errors of comparable mag-
nitude. However, the normal velocities obtained with the reconstruction of inclined
velocity measurements had a somewhat larger uncertainty due to additional exper-
imental error (10% and 20% for the averaged velocity and second order moments,
respectively).

The claimed accuracy of the results was confirmed by several means, for example
measurements in a laminar flow to verify the alignment of the system (absence
of fringe gradients and chirps), comparison with literature results, comparison of
similar results obtained from different techniques and so on.

Although LDA is a mature measurement technique widely used in flow dynamics
research, it can never be considered as a black box. It is of major importance to
evaluate all steps carefully, from alignment of the optics, to data acquisition and
processing. Even if all steps are performed accurately, one should interpret the
results with care. ;

9.3 Single cube

The time-averaged flow field around an isolated cube consisted of the horseshoe
vortex, the top and the side vortices and the arch-shaped wake vortex. Some dif-
ferences were detected as compared to the fully developed high-Reynolds number
channel flow with a channel-height-to-cube-height ratio of 2, studied by Martinuzzi
[101], which case is the closest to the present situation. Shear layer reattachment at
the top and side faces of the cube was found for the present low-Reynolds number
case while this was absent in the fully developed high-Reynolds number case from
literature. Further, the flow reattachment at the channel floor downstream of the
cube occurred at a larger distance than in the fully developed channel flow. The
vortex shedding frequency was also different: the Strouhal number was St = 0.095
in the present case while it was St = 0.145 in the fully developed high Reynolds
number flow.

The local flow structure had a significant effect on the local convective heat
transfer from the cube. At the windward face, it was observed that the heat trans-
fer coefficient is uniform about the stagnation point, commonly found for impinging
flows. A local heat transfer minimum was found close to the base corner correspond-
ing to the position of the horseshoe.vortex core. Although this location coincided
with an area of high wall shear stresses it was argued that the local increase in the
fluid temperature prevented a beneficial removal of heat. Flow separation occurred
at the leading side edges and flow instabilities caused significant vortex shedding
behind these faces. Maxima of the heat transfer coefficient occurred in the proxim-
ity of the reattachment points, where the velocity fluctuations normal to the wall
are large. Minima in A were found below the recirculation vortex cores, where the
wall shear stress is high but where the local fluid temperature is also locally higher.
The increased temperature of the vortex acted as an insulation layer and prevented

=== [PDF page 241] ===
9.4. Row of cubes 239

beneficial convective removal of heat. Similar features were found at the top face.
The arch vortex in the wake of the cube caused rather uniform distributions of the
local heat transfer coefficient.

Measurements of the static surface pressure were in agreement with the LDA-
observations. A suction peak was found underneath the top and the side vortices
while the reattachment region was characterised by a pressure maximum. This
again illustrated the dissimilarity between surface heat transfer and the local flow
structure.

A laminar developing channel flow was achieved by removing the tripping wire
upstream of the cube such that a smooth entrance length remained. This situation
enabled us to study the effect of the oncoming flow condition on the convective heat
transfer. The vortices in the proximity of the isolated cube were generally larger in
the laminar case. In particular the convective heat transfer at the top and sicle faces
of the cube was significantly lower than for the turbulent case for a similar Reynolds
number. Differences between the tripped and untripped results were negligible for
Rey < 2200. Rey ~ 2500 was identified as a transition point for which the tripping
wire became effective.

9.4 Row of cubes

The flow field study revealed that a horseshoe vortex structure originated directly
upstream of the leading obstacle base. Flow separation from the sharp leading
edges caused the forming of vortices at the top and side faces of the leading cube.
Ordinary shear flow was found for the second and downstream cubes in the row.
The complex vortex structure of the flow-field around the individual cubes caused
a large non-uniformity in the convective heat transfer distributions. In particular,
the flow separation and reattachment at the top and side faces of the first cube and
the strong arch-shaped vortices between subsequent cubes gave rise to significant
differences in the local heat transfer coefficients. It was shown that regions of intense
flow separation are usually associated with a poor convective heat transfer and, in
contrast, regions with flow reattachment exhibited the opposite behaviour.

A substantial variation in the averaged convective heat transfer was found along
the array of cubes, in particular for the first three subsequent cubes. The leading
cube appeared to have the lowest averaged heat transfer coefficient for the three
highest Reynolds numbers studied. This was a consequence of the strong vortices at
both the lateral and top faces. The second cube, however, is situated in the wake of
the first cube and experiences a direct interaction with the horseshoe vortex and the
separated shear layer which flows over the top of the leading cube. This caused the
highest averaged heat transfer coefficient. Fully developed convective heat transfer
was found starting from the third cube.

Buoyancy effects imposed a noticeable effect on the convective heat transfer only
at the lowest Reynolds number studied, Re = 795. Especially in the corridors be-
tween subsequent cubes, the vertical fluid motion caused an asymmetric distribution
of the local heat transfer coefficient.

=== [PDF page 242] ===
240 Chapter 9. Concluding discussion

9.5 Tandem of cubes

Aerodynamic interference between two cubes and the effect of neighbouring cubes
was studied for in-line and off-axis tandem arrangements of cubes. It was shown for
the in-line tandem that variation of S/H had only a significant effect on the mean
flow and turbulence fields around the downstream cube, while the flow around the
leading cube was hardly affected. The flow around the leading cube was more or less
similar to that around the wall-mounted isolated cube. For S,/H = 2, the separated
shear layer reattached at the side edge of the downstream cube; it reattached in the
inter-obstacle spacing at 2/H = 2.5 for S,/H = 4 and larger. Large similarities
were found to exist in the flow structure around the upstream and the downstream
cube for S,/H > 8. The distinguishable flow features, for example, flow separation
and reattachment, and recirculation led to marked differences in the distributions
of h.

The frequency of vortex shedding appeared to become minimum for a critical
distance of S,/H = 2.9 which was dictated by the local flow structure. Although
small, a minimum in the face-averaged heat transfer for the top and side faces was
found for this critical streamwise distance. It was assumed that both the convective
heat transfer and the dynamics of vortex shedding were a direct consequence of the
flow structures which were dictated by the streamwise distance.

Further, the time-dependent convective heat transfer was measured with a fast-
responding active heat flux sensor. The wake flow of the upstream cube was sub-
jected to flow periodicities caused by vortex shedding. Dependent on S,/H, the
periodically oscillating shear layer reattached on the side faces of the downstream
cube, causing the convective heat flux to vary periodically as well. This was demon-
strated from power density spectra of the heat flux signal while the vortex shedding
frequency was simultaneously measured with a hot-wire anemometer. These results
were in good agreement with the LDA frequency measurements.

The flow structures detected around the cubes in the off-axis tandem arrange-
ments were apparently similar to those measured around the cubes in the in-line
tandem. Typically, the horseshoe, top, side and wake vortices were detected from
LDA measurements and flow visualisations. Only for the tandems with small stream-
wise and spanwise distances, significant asymmetric flow structures were observed.
These had comparable, but somewhat different consequences on the local convective
heat transfer.

While large differences in the distributions of the local heat transfer coefficient
existed between the different in-line and off-axis tandem arrangements, the cube-
averaged heat transfer coefficients appeared to be insensitive to both the streamwise
and spanwise distance between the cubes. This result suggests that the bulk ve-
locity of the flow rather than the local flow structures dictates the overall cooling
performance of the cubes.

=== [PDF page 243] ===
9.6. Matrix of cubes 241

9.6 Matrix of cubes

It was shown that the flow in the immediate proximity of the cube in the matrix
configuration is characterised by a two-cell structure which was caused by the shear
layer reattachment in the inter-obstacle space. A horseshoe type of vortex originates
at the front face; an arc-shaped vortex exists in the wake. Flow separation at the
sharp leading top and side edges caused flow recirculations to exist close to the edges
and subsequent reattachment at the faces more downstream. Vortex shedding in the
wake of the cube was detected from LDA-measurements. A typical dimensionless
shedding frequency of St = 0.109 was found. Both the periodic fluctuations caused
by vortex shedding and the large velocity gradients were responsible for large values
of the turbulent kinetic energy in the free shear layers in the wake of the cube.

The pronounced differences in the flow structure around the cube caused large
gradients in the distributions of the local heat transfer coefficient. Similarity of these
distributions, scaled with the cube-averaged heat transfer coefficient, indicated that
no Reynolds number effects existed in the range of Reynolds numbers investigated
(2380 < Rey < 5280). Regions of intense flow recirculations with subsequent flow
separation showed relatively small values of the local heat transfer coefficient. In
contrast, areas where the flow reattached were generally characterised by large values
of the heat transfer coefficient.

9.7 Cube-averaged convective heat transfer

FIGURE 9.1: Cube-averaged heat transfer coefficient for all geometries studied as a function of the
bulk velocity ug.

For the tandem configurations, it was shown that the cube-averaged convective
heat transfer was insensitive to both the streamwise and spanwise distance between

=== [PDF page 244] ===
242 Chapter 9. Concluding discussion

the cubes. A similar observation can be made for the other configurations studied.
To do so, the cube-averaged heat transfer coefficients are plotted as a function of the
bulk velocity in Fig. 9.1 for all different configurations considered in this thesis. The
off-axis tandem results are omitted since the large number of data will reduce the
ability of interpreting the figure. In particular for the larger bulk velocities, the heat
transfer data collapse pretty well for all considered cases. In log-log coordinates, the
cube-averaged heat transfer coefficient obeys a linear regression which suggests that
the overall convective cooling characteristics for this turbulent region are dominated
by the bulk flow through the channel. The results for the in-line row of cubes differ
somewhat from the general trend. This deviation is largest for the upstream cubes
in the row, while the deviations diminish for the cubes further downstream in the
row. A sharp transition in the heat transfer coefficient is observed at ug = 2.5
m/s which is attributed to the transition from a laminar developing to a turbulent
developing channel flow as facilitated by the tripping wire upstream of the cubes.

It is noted that the results given in Fig. 9.1 were obtained for one channel-height-
to-cube-height ratio, for one level of the free stream turbulence, one viscosity (that
of air), and so on. It is, therefore, not known if the linear correlation is generally
applicable for situations with different flow and fluid properties.

As mentioned before, the convective heat transfer characteristics in a spatially
fully developed matrix flow was one of the test cases of the ERCOFTAC workshop
on refined flow modelling, held at the Delft University of Technology on June 6-7,
1997. Bornoff and Parry [21] and Badie and Lasance [12] were the only participants
who simulated the convective heat transfer in this matrix flow. They used the
commercial code Flotherm [49, 50]. Although the velocity field around the cubes
was wrongly predicted (with all the turbulence models incorporated in Flotherm)
the cube-averaged convective heat transfer predictions corresponded remarkably well
with the experimental data (deviations of maximum 10-20%). The explanation for
this agreement is found in Fig. 9.1. Although large differences in the local heat
transfer distributions were detected, the averaged heat transfer coefficient scales
directly with the bulk flow.

=== [PDF page 245] ===
CHAPTER 10

Conclusions and recommendations

In this last chapter, conclusions of the major findings of the research are drawn and
possible directions for future research are highlighted.

10.1 Major conclusions

The major feature of the local heat transfer distribution for all four different test
cases discussed in this thesis is a large spatial variation and steep gradients. Vari-
ations in h were up to 100% at a relatively small area of 15 x 15 mm. Farther,
marked differences were also observed between the face-averaged heat transfer co-
efficients. The large variation in A, i.e. the intensification and degradation of the
local convective heat transfer, was caused by the vortex structure in the proximity of
the cubes. A minimum in the surface heat transfer was typically found at locations
where the fluid undergoes a recirculation, such as the top and side vortices. On the
contrary, flow reattachment caused large convective heat transfer rates. Although
the time-dependent heat transfer measurements were far from perfect, the results
provided interesting additional information of the convective heat transfer dynamics
involved.

The detailed accuracy analysis of the experimental techniques indicated that
the acquired convective heat transfer and flow data appeared to be very reliable.
The LDA measurements for the matrix configuration were in good agreement with
numerical simulations which provided additional evidence of the carefully conducted
experiments. In the development of advanced turbulence models and numerical
solution methods, the need for accurate flow and heat transfer data is indispensable
in order to make progress. The accurate results collected in this thesis are very
suitable to validate further developments.

Although the configurations of wall-mounted cubes placed in a low-Reynolds
number turbulent channel flow are considered to be representative test cases for re-
alistic printed circuit boards (PCB), the experimental data presented in this thesis
can not directly be translated to the practical heat transfer problems encountered on
such boards. The study provides the design engineer at least sufficient background
information how to solve these thermal problems and it makes the designer aware of
the complexity of the convective heat transfer in question. An interesting result of
the study is that for all considered configurations, the cube-averaged heat transfer
coefficient was a linear function of the bulk velocity when plotted in log-log coor-
dinates. Although this observation suggests that global parameters can be used to
describe global convective heat transfer, such a relation will not provide information
about local convective heat transfer phenomena which is of crucial importance to
avoid local hot spots on electronic components. Further, the data acquired for the

243

=== [PDF page 246] ===
244 Chapter 10. Conclusions and recommendations

four test cases can be used for benchmark purposes of commercial Computational.
Fluid Dynamics (CFD) codes, commonly used in the thermal design of PCBs.

In conclusion, the objectives of the research have been met but additional re-
search is recommended to complement some of the aspects reported in this thesis.

10.2. Recommendations for future research

The time-dependent convective heat transfer is an issue which needs much more
attention.. The results reported in this thesis were a first attempt to analyse the
dynamics in the convective heat transfer from wall-mounted cubes. A more ad-
vanced, a second generation sensor, is needed to accurately measure absolute values
of the heat transfer coefficients. It was suggested that minimisation of the heat
losses by means of isothermal conditions is a realistic option. This can be achieved
by a guard heater, underneath the sensor. Another alternative is to determine
the time-dependent heat losses from an experimental and numerical analysis. By
a proper calibration of the sensor resistance versus the surface temperature, the
actual sensor temperature is evaluated more accurately. Further, by embedding
several thermocouples in the cube underneath the sensor, the conductive heat losses
can be estimated. Numerical simulations, for example with Flotherm, can serve to
estimate the three-dimensional conductive heat losses which can serve to guide the
interpretation of the experimental results.

In order to elucidate the mechanisms which contribute to the degradation and
the intensification of the local convective heat transfer, the time-dependent correla-
tion between the flow structure and the surface heat flux needs to be investigated.
Particle image velocimetry (PIV) is an almost mature technique to measure in-
stantaneous vector fields. Synchronised simultaneous heat transfer measurements
of the local surface heat flux can be performed with fast-response liquid crystal or
infrared thermography. The simultaneous measurements should help in explaining
the mechanisms that cause the observed dissimilarities between surface heat transfer
and local flow structure in vortical flows.

Structure identification of the flow around the cube can be performed when in-
stantaneous velocity data is available in planes. PIV is very suitable for providing
data for this kind of analysis. With different methods based on flow topology and
critical point theory, the vortex structure can then be identified. The structure iden-
tification based on the streakline patterns obtained from the oil-film visualisation
only allowed a qualitative analysis. Since oil-film visualisations visualise the wall
shear stress at the solid surface of the obstacle, and indirectly the local velocity field
just above the surface, it is well suited for correlating the streakline structure with
the local heat transfer coefficient. The only difficulty is to quantify the streakline
patterns at the surface but at least a qualitative interpretation can be performed.

Direct measurement of the turbulent heat fluxes wu’ can be performed with Laser
Doppler Anemometry and a cold wire positioned close to the measuring volume
of the LDA-system. These results will also help in explaining the dissimilarities
between the convective heat transfer from the cubes to the immediate flow. However,
this is a very difficult measurement since the temperature-velocity correlation is

=== [PDF page 247] ===
10.2. Recommendations for future research 245

dependent on the local velocity and, therefore, on the location of the cold wire with
respect to the measuring volume.

It is useful for engineering applications to relate the cube-averaged heat transfer
coefficients to quantities which are easy to be measured in practical situations. One
can think, for example, of the local static surface pressure in the channel or the free
stream turbulence of the flow.

Another issue which deserves attention is how to deal with the definition of the
local heat transfer coefficient. If the true local adiabatic reference temperature can
be measured, temperature effects will be eliminated. In the case of epoxy cubes,
strong temperature gradients exist at the cube surface due to the low thermal con-
ductivity of epoxy. Since it is impossible to locally make the cube surface adiabatic,
numerical simulations will help in estimating the order of magnitude of the effect.
Further, the effect of the recirculation in the proximity of the cube can be investi-
gated. Experimentally, one can think of measuring the response of a point source on
the epoxy surface, for example by means of laser illumination. If the temperature
distribution is in that case measured with infrared thermography, the temperature
distribution in the epoxy layer can then be solved, giving the heat fluxes involved.
In that way, the effect of a point source can be studied which may elucidate the
concept of the adiabatic convective heat transfer.

This brief summation of recommendations for further research illustrates that there
are still many issues to be addressed. The availability in the near future of highly
accurate and more sophisticated measurement techniques as well as more advanced
numerical methods and turbulence models facilitate the investigation of the time-
dependent interaction between convective heat transfer and turbulence. Considering
the complexity of the problem and the interesting transport phenomena involved, it
is a big challenge to proceed with the investigation to address the questions which
are still open.

APPENDIX A

Thermal conductivities

A.1 Thermal conductivity of the epoxy substrate

A transient and a steady-state method were developed to measure the thermal
conductivity of the two-compound epoxy substrate. In addition to the thermal con-
ductivity, the transient method provided also an estimate of the thermal diffusivity.
The steady-state method provided the possibility to investigate the temperature
dependency of the epoxy substrate.

A.1.1 Transient hot-wire method

The hot-wire method is based on the conductive heat transfer in a cylinder-symmetric
infinite medium (cylinder) with a line source at the centreline. When the medium
is considered to be isentropic and homogeneous, the Fourier equation for heat con-
duction expressed in cylindrical coordinates becomes [29]:

Design of the test-sample

The test-sample consisted of a cylinder of epoxy in which a high electrical resistance
wire was embedded at r = 0. A sketch of the sample is given in Fig. A.1. Thermo-
couples were also embedded in the epoxy at two fixed radial positions R, and Rp.
It is noted that the two-compound liquid epoxy substrate was poured into a cylin-
drical mall with the dissipating wire and thermocouples already in position. After
the solidification of the epoxy, it was ensured that no interface resistance existed
between the wires and the surrounding epoxy. When a voltage drop is maintained
across the wire, heat is diffused into the cylinder causing the temperature T(r, f)
to increase. The thermal conductivity was then derived from the temperature-time
gradients at the fixed positions R, and Rp.

FicurE A.1: Sketch of the cylindrical test-sample for the transient hot-wire method to measure
the thermal conductivity of the epoxy substrate. Dimensions are given in mm.

The resistance wire had a diameter of 0.01 mm and a length of / = 80 mm.
The power supply to the wire, the product of the voltage drop V and the electrical
current I, gives then the heat per unit length: Q; = P/l = VI/l. The cylindrical
test-sample had a length of 100 mm and a radius of 20 mm. The two thermocouples
were mounted in parallel to the centreline at distances of AR; = 1 and Ry = 3
mm. The temperature of the outer wall (at r = 20 mm) was also monitored with
thermocouples to ensure that this surface temperature was not increased due to the
power dissipation at the centre, and that the half-infinite approach was still valid.
The whole cylinder mantle was thermally insulated with foam to minimise the heat
losses to the environment.

The uncertainty in the measured thermal conductivity consisted of both experi-
mental and structural errors. An estimate of the experimental uncertainty is based
on the single sample uncertainty analysis, see {109, 110]. Structural errors were due
to, for example, interface resistances, a not-infinite medium, heat losses through
the leading wires, heat conduction at the lateral endings of the cylinder, and so on.
It was verified that the dimensions as given in Fig. A.1 satisfied the requirements
of an infinite medium. The temperature at the outer radius (r = 20 mm) of the
test-sample did not increase during the experiment. The connecting leading wires,

=== [PDF page 251] ===
A.1. Thermal conductivity of the epoxy substrate 249

to which the resistance wire was welded, were made of a Nickel alloy and had a
low thermal conductivity to minimise conductive heat losses. Further, it had a high
electrical conductivity to prevent power dissipation in the lead wires itself. The re-
sistance wire (heat source) was fully embedded in the epoxy (the length of the wire
was 8 cm while the length of the total epoxy cylinder was 10 cm, see Fig. A.1). The
low thermal conductivity of epoxy prevented thus lateral heat losses. The thermal
insulation at the lateral faces further minimised these conductive heat losses. From
a detailed analysis, it was estimated that the heat losses were less than 1%. The
thermocouples were welded such that the hot junction was in the centre and that
both thermocouple wires were guided through the epoxy in opposite direction. This
construction is shown in Fig. A.1. Since these thermocouple junctions were located
at half of the cylinder length, a symmetric thermal situation inside the epoxy cylin-
der was guaranteed. The length of the cylinder was much larger than the radius
R, and Ry were the actual temperatures were measured and, therefore, the situa-
tion could be considered as one-dimensional, at least in a substantial part of the
test-sample. Based on a detailed analysis, the accuracy of the thermal conductivity
measured with the transient method was estimated to be 5%.

orientations of long molecules.

Test-sample

A test-sample was designed and constructed which consisted of an annulus of epoxy
with copper annuli adjacent to both the inner and outer side of the epoxy material.
A sketch of this test-sample is shown in Fig. A.4. The copper annuli were positioned
in a mall such that an inner annulus of air resulted. The liquid epoxy mixture was
poured into this vertical annulus where after the solidification process started. This
vertical orientation prevented the occurrence of interface resistances between copper
and epoxy since eventual shrinkage of the epoxy was compensated for with fresh
epoxy (gravitation forces caused a constant pressure on the hardening epoxy, thus

enabling a good contact between the epoxy and copper). This construction ensured
that the annulus width (Rz - R,) was constant within 0.2 % along the entire length

The length of the model was 10 cm. The three radii Ri, Re and Rs were 100,
150 and 200 mm, respectively. The epoxy annulus had a radius Ry - Ay = 50 mm.
The axial edges were insulated with a low thermal conductivity foam ( was less
than 0.1 W/mK). This insulation and the sufficiently large aspect ratio (the ratio
of length-to-width of the annulus was 100:5 = 20) prevented severe heat losses in
the axial direction. This resulted in a one-dimensional axis-symmetric situation
(the temperature was only a function of the radius). This one-dimensionality was
confirmed with experiments and is discussed later.

A heat source made from a spirally-winded resistance wire was placed at the
centreline of the inner copper annulus. The connecting lead wires were again made
from a Nickel alloy to minimise conductive heat losses and to prevent the power
dissipation in the connecting pins. The high thermal conductivity of copper resulted
in a uniform temperature of the inner annulus, even for relatively large heat fluxes.
This temperature was monitored with 6 thermocouples distributed in the inner
annulus. The average of these 6 temperatures gave the inner temperature at the
radius Ry: T; = T(R,). The standard deviation was about 0.1 °C. The entire
sample was placed in a thermostated water bath. The temperature of the outer

=== [PDF page 255] ===
A.l. Thermal conductivity of the epoxy substrate 253

copper annulus was dictated by the water temperature due to the good heat transfer
coefficient of water. Again 6 thermocouples were used to measure the averaged
copper temperature which represented the temperature at Ry: Tz = T(Rt2). By
varying the water temperature (and thus T(R2)) and the power supply to the heat
source in the core of the sample, the temperature difference AT = Ty - 7, was
variable.

This one-dimensional model was derived to determine spatially- and temperature-
independent thermal conductivities of the test material (epoxy). If the thermal
conductivity is temperature-dependent, an averaged value is measured across the
annulus radius which corresponds to the averaged temperature (Tz + T,)/2. If the
temperature differences AT’ are kept relatively small, the temperature dependence
of the thermal conductivity can then be examined by varying the temperature level
(I, + T,)/2. Experiments were conducted with temperature differences of 5, 10 and
15 °C at different temperature levels between 25 and 85 °C’. While the results for
the smaller temperature difference (AT = 5 °C’) gave a better temperature resolu-
tion, the results for the larger differences (AT = 10 - 15 °C’) were more accurate
since the experimental uncertainty was then relatively smaller.

A.1.3. Concluding remarks

In conclusion, a transient and a steady-state method were explored to measure the
thermal conductivity of the epoxy substrate. With the assumption of constant ther-
mal properties of the epoxy material, a thermal conductivity of \ = 0.237 W/mK
was measured with the transient technique. This assumption was confirmed by
the measured logarithmic temperature profiles. A small temperature dependence
was measured with the steady-state method. However, this variation was about
4% which is smaller than the 5% experimental uncertainty involved and, therefore,
this dependency was not taken into account. The averaged thermal conductivity
measured with the steady-state method was \ = 0.235 W/mK. The thermal con-
ductivities obtained with both methods are in very good agreement. It is, therefore,
concluded that the epoxy substrate had a constant thermal conductivity of A =

=== [PDF page 257] ===
A.2. Thermal conductivity of the formaldehyde base plate 255

0.235 W/mK which was claimed to be accurate within 5%.

A.2 Thermal conductivity of the formaldehyde base plate

The base plate was fabricated from a low thermal conductivity formaldehyde. An
accurate value of the thermal conductivity is required to estimate the heat losses
through the base during the heat transfer measurements. Only a steady state con-
duction method was applied which was based on the so-called sandwich method.

A.2.1 Experimental method

The formaldehyde material was machined to plates of exactly 100 x 100 x 10 mm.
The precise thickness of 10 mm was essential for an accurate measurement of the
thermal conductivity. A copper plate was equipped with a commercial electrical
resistance heater, operated between 5 and 100 Watts. The heat source was fully
embedded in the copper inner plate of the sandwich. This inner plate had dimensions
120 x 120 x 10 mm. The formaldehyde plates were placed on each side of the
copper inner plate. Thermal heat compound ensured negligible thermal contact
resistances between the copper and formaldehyde plate. Subsequently, two outer
copper plates were placed at both outer sides of the formaldehyde material and
again, thermal compound was used to minimise thermal resistances between the
different materials. The outer plates consisted of machined channels through which
water of adjustable temperature was recirculated. These heat exchangers enabled

=== [PDF page 258] ===
256 Appendiz A. Thermal conductivities

that the outer temperature was kept at a prescribed level. The entire sandwich,
constructed in this way, was pressed between two clamps. A sketch of the sandwich
is given in Fig. A.7. Thermal insulation around all sides of the sandwich was applied
to minimise heat losses to the environment. Thermocouples were embedded in
both the copper inner and outer plates. Due to its large thermal conductivity, the
copper plates were constant in temperature. The thermocouple measurements were
averaged to obtain the averaged copper temperature.

The total heat (P = VI, where V is the voltage drop and J is the electric
current) dissipated in the electrical heat source in the inner copper plate of the
sandwich should equal the conductive heat through both sides of the inner core
(Qcond) plus some additional heat losses (Qioss):

P=VxI= Qeond + Qtoss- (A.12)

Since a relative small thickness-to-width ratio was selected for the formaldehyde
substrate (10:1), the situation was considered to be one-dimensional. The heat
through one half of the sandwich is Q.ona/2 and equals:

A.2.2 Results

The temperature of the outer copper plates was fixed at a constant temperature
between 19.5 and 20.0 °C’. The power supply to the electrical heater was gradually
increased which led to an increase in the temperature of the inner copper plate.

Since the temperature of the outer copper plate was kept at room temperature, the
temperature decay across the formaldehyde plate increased also. The results, given
in Fig. A.8 show a perfect linear increase in the temperature difference as a function
of the power supply. The thermal conductivity was directly determined from its
slope, according to: Oninal

of the heat losses to the environment.

SUMMARY

Experimental study of heat transfer in turbulent flows over
wall-mounted cubes

Erwin R. Meinders

The turbulent heat transfer and the flow field characteristics of heated wall
mounted cubes placed in a channel flow are experimentally studied in this thesis.
This investigation is of both fundamental scientific and practical interest. Sharp-
edged obstacles on flat walls give rise to complex flow patterns with regions of flow
separation, reattachment and recirculation. Further, periodic vortex shedding can
occur in the wake of the obstacles because of flow instabilities. The fundamental
interest lies in understanding the interaction between these large-scale vortex struc-
tures and the local surface heat transfer. Further, because of the complex convective
heat transfer and turbulence involved, these configurations are suitable test cases for
the benchmarking of turbulence models and numerical methods. From the applica-
tion point of view, these geometries have a diagnostic value of practical relevance to
the electronics industry, where local overheating is an important cause of technical
failure of printed circuit boards.

Four different test cases were considered, namely a single cube, an in-line array
of cubes, a spatially fully developed matrix flow and a tandem configuration of cubes
with various streamwise and spanwise spacings between the cubes. The configura-
tions were mounted on one of the walls of a low-Reynolds number closed windtunnel.
Distributions of the time-averaged local convective heat transfer were determined
from the internally controlled heat flux and the surface temperature distributions of
special measurements cubes. Infrared and liquid crystal thermography were used to
measure the surface temperature distributions. A high accuracy in the infrared tem-
perature measurement (0.4 °C’) was achieved by the application of image restoration
of the infrared images and an in situ calibration. The image restoration technique
was based on the measured optical transfer function. The infrared measurements
processed with the image restoration filter agreed very well with independent liquid
crystal measurements. In addition to the time-averaged measurement method, some
time-dependent heat transfer measurements were performed with a fast-responding
heat flux sensor. Although the technique was far from perfect, some interesting
results have been obtained for the tandem configuration of two cubes.

The three-dimensional flow structures around the cubes were deduced from oil-
film surface patterns, smoke visualisation and detailed Laser Doppler Anemometry
data. The two-dimensional LDA-system allowed the direct measurement of two
velocity components. With two additional methods, information of the third com-
ponent was obtained. A direct measurement of the normal velocity component was
possible by integrating a prism in one of the cubes while the second method was
based on a modification of the set-up to allow the reconstruction of the third velocity
from inclined measurements.

273

=== [PDF page 276] ===
274 Summary

A highly turbulent flow structure was typically observed for all configurations
studied. For example, it was found that for the single cube or other configuration
with a sufficiently large distance between the cubes, the flow was characterised by
high vorticity recirculations at both the top and side faces, as well as the horseshoe
vortex and the wake vortex. Flow instabilities caused the separated shear layers to
fluctuate in a sinusoidal manner, the so called vortex shedding, which led to a highly
turbulent wake flow. If cubes are located in the vicinity of other cubes, the flow
structures are somewhat altered. This was clearly shown for a tandem of cubes for
which the streamwise distance was varied. While the flow around the first cube was
hardly affected by the downstream cubes, the flow around the latter was significantly
influenced by the leading cube. Boundary layer flow was typically found for small
streamwise distances while this generally changed to recirculative flow for the larger
streamwise distances.

These, in nature, different flow types caused large variations in the local con-
vective heat transfer for all configurations considered in this study. The steepest
gradients in the distributions of the surface heat transfer were found at locations
characterised by flow separation and reattachment. The resulting flow recirculations
were typically accompanied by a minimum in the heat transfer coefficient. It was
suggested that due to the local temperature rise of the air in the recirculation bub-
ble, the bubble acted as an insulation layer and, therefore, prevented the removal
of heat from the surface of the cubes. In contrast, the intermittent reattachment of
the low-temperature shear flow was found to cause large heat transfer coefficients.

Despite the fact that a large variation in the local convective heat transfer existed
for all configurations studied, dominated by the local vortex structure around the
protrusions, the cube-averaged heat transfer appeared to scale with the bulk velocity
of the flow. Although this is an interesting result for global thermal analysis, local
hot spots still may occur which makes a detailed analysis of the flow features and
related heat transfer characteristics indispensable.

=== [PDF page 277] ===
SAMENVATTING

Experimenteel onderzoek van de warmteoverdracht in turbulente
stromingen rond kubussen geplaatst op een wand

Erwin R. Meinders

De turbulente stromingsverschijnselen en de convectieve warmteoverdracht van
kubussen geplaatst in een kanaalstroming zijn experimentee! onderzocht in dit ver-
slag. Het beschreven onderzoek is zowel van fundamenteel wetenschappelijk als van
praktisch belang. De stroming rond kubussen is gekarakteriseerd door een com-
plex vortex systeem met grenslaag loslating, aanhechting en stromingsrecirculaties.
Daarnaast kunnen instabiliteiten in de stroming aanleiding geven tot periodieke
wervelafschudding. Het fundamentele karakter heeft vooral betrekking op het begri-
jpen van de koppeling tussen grootschalige vortex structuren en de lokale convectieve
warmteoverdracht. Kubusvormige obstakels zijn verder uitermate geschikt voor het
valideren van turbulentie modellen en numerieke oplosmethoden door de complex-
iteit van zowel de stroming als de lokale convectieve warmteoverdracht. Vanuit een
praktisch oogpunt bezien hebben deze configuraties een diagnostische waarde met
betrekking tot de koeling van elektronische printkaarten waarvoor lokale oververhit-
ting nog steeds een belangrijke oorzaak van technisch falen is. Grondige kennis van
de warmteverschijnselen van elektronische componenten en systemen is van vitaal
belang om reeds tijdens het ontwerpstadium eventuele “hot spots’ te elimineren.
De onderzochte configuraties van kubussen zijn geidealiseerde representanten van
complexe printkaarten zoals die in computers en ander elektronische apparatuur te
vinden zijn.

In deze studie zijn 4 configuraties onderzocht, namelijk een vrijstaande kubus,
een rij kubussen, een tandem configuratie waarbij zowel de longitudinale als transver-
sale afstand tussen de kubussen is gevarieerd en tenslotte een matrix van kubussen.
De kubusconfiguraties zijn geplaatst in een kanaalstroming met een relatief laag
Reynolds getal. De lokale tijdsgemiddelde warmteoverdracht is gemeten met spe-
ciaal daarvoor ontworpen meetkubussen. De verdeling van de oppervlakte tem-
peratuur van deze kubussen is gemeten met infrarood thermografie en vloeibare
kristallen (liquid crystals). Een hoge nauwkeurigheid, te weten 0.4 °C, is verkre-
gen door de infrarood metingen te bewerken met een beeldreconstructie filter en
verder door de metingen in situ te kalibreren. Het beeldreconstructie filter was
gebaseerd op gemeten overdrachtsfuncties die geconstrueerd zijn uit responsies op
perfecte thermische stapfuncties. De gemeten oppervlakte temperatuur is gebruikt
als randvoorwaarde voor het bepalen van de lokale convectieve warmtefluxen door
het opperviak van de kubus. Daarnaast is deze temperatuur ook nodig om warm-
teoverdrachtscoéfficienten te definieren. De tijdsafhankelijke warmteoverdracht is
gemeten met een zelf-ontwikkelde actieve censor met een zeer snelle responsie. On-
danks dat de censor nog in een ontwikkelfase verkeerde, zijn er toch interessante
resultaten mee verkregen. De met deze censor gemeten tijdsafhankelijke warmteflux
door het zijvlak van een kubus geplaatst in het zog van een andere kubus bleck

275

=== [PDF page 278] ===
276 Samenvatting

een periodieke component te bevatten waarvan de frequentie overeen kwam met de
frequentie van wervelafschudding, zoals gemeten met het LDA systeem.

Het driedimensionale tijdsgemiddelde en instantane stromingsveld is gemeten
met verscheidene technieken, te weten Laser Doppler Anemometrie, ’Hot-wire’ Ane-
mometrie en stromingsvisualisaties (waaronder rook en olie-film visualisaties). Om-
dat het gebruikte LDA systeem alleen maar geschikt was om de twee snelheidscom-
ponenten parallel aan de grondplaat te meten, zijn er twee methodes ontwikkeld
om de derde component, loodrecht op het grondvlak, te meten. Een directe meting
van deze snelheid was mogelijk door gebruik te maken van prisma geintegreerd in
één van de kubussen waarin de laser bundels 90 graden gespiegeld werden door een
spiegelend diagonaalvlak (de hypotenusa van de kubus). In de tweede methode werd
de loodrechte snelheidscomponent gereconstrueerd uit metingen onder verschillende
hoeken.

De stroming rond een vrijstaande kubus, of een configuratie met een voldoende
grote onderlinge afstand tussen de kubussen, bestaat typisch uit een hoefijzerachtige
vortex ("horseshoe’ vortex), die stroomopwaarts van de kubus ontstaat en door de
hoofdstroming langs beide kanten wordt afgebogen. Verder bestaat de stroming
uit een boogachtige vortex (’wake vortex’) in het zog en stromingsrecirculaties op
de top- en zijvlakken (’top’ en ’side’ vortex). Wervelafschudding van de zijvlakken
veroorzaakt een oscillerende stroming in het zog van de kubus wat een verhoogde
turbulentie intensiteit tot gevolg heeft. Enigszins afwijkende stromingsverschijnse-
len werden waargenomen indien de kubus in de nabijheid van een andere kubus was
geplaatst. Dit is duidelijk te zien voor een tandem configuratie van twee op een lijn
geplaatste kubussen waarvoor de longitudinale afstand was gevarieerd. Terwijl het
stromingsveld rond de stroomopwaartse kubus nauwelijks door de stroomafwaartse
kubus werd beinvloed, blijkt het tegengestelde te gelden voor de stroomafwaartse
kubus. De stroming langs de top- en zijvlakken van het desbetreffende objekt was
een grenslaagstroming voor kleine onderlinge afstanden tussen de kubussen ter-
wijl voor grotere separaties grenslaag loslating, en dus een recirculatieve stroming,
plaatsvond.

De verschillende typen stromingen veroorzaakten grote verschillen in de lokale
convectieve warmteoverdracht. De steilste gradienten zijn gevonden op locaties waar
de stroming recirculeerde. De lokale warmteoverdrachtscoéfficient vertoonde lokale
Minima op plaatsen ongeveer ter hoogte van de kern van deze recirculaties. Dit
verschijnsel werd deels verklaard door een temperatuurstijging van de wervel dat
daardoor als een soort isolatie laag fungeerde. Daarentegen veroorzaakte het op-
nieuw aanhechten van de relatief koude luchtstroming maxima in de lokale warm-
teoverdrachtscoéfficient op plaatsen waar de stroming aanhechtte.

Ondanks het feit dat de lokale warmteoverdrachtscoéfficient grote verschillen
vertoonde voor alle onderzochte configuraties, bleek dat de kubusgemiddelde warm-
teoverdrachtscoéfficient schaalt met de gemiddelde snelheid in het stromingskanaal.
Hoewel dit een interessant resultaat is voor een globale thermische analyse van
printkaarten, zal een nauwkeurige karakterisatie van het turbulente stromingsveld
en de daaraan gerelateerde lokale convectieve warmteoverdracht onontbeerlijk zijn
om lokale oververhitting van componenten te voorkomen.

ACKNOWLEDGEMENT

Het is een illusie om experimenteel onderzoek zonder de technische, wetenschappe-
lijke en mentale ondersteuning van anderen te verrichten. In dit dankwoord wil ik
iedereen bedanken die heeft bijgedragen aan het onderzoek en de totstandkoming
van dit proefschrift. In het bijzonder wil ik graag stil staan bij een aantal personen
die een grote bijdrage heeft geleverd.

Ten eerste ben ik mijn promotor prof. K. Hanjali¢ zeer erkentelijk voor zijn
stimulerende en enthousiaste begeleiding. Uw fysische inzichten en kennis hebben
mij veel inhoudelijk bagage meegeven. Verder heeft uw internationale opvatting van
onderzoek verrichten mij in contact gebracht met andere professionele onderzoekers
en mij in de gelegenheid gesteld om deel te nemen aan internationale conferenties
en workshops.

Theo van der Meer heeft vooral in de eerste jaren van het onderzoek een be-
langrijke bijdrage geleverd aan de dagelijkse begeleiding. Ook in een later stadium
had je altijd tijd voor inhoudelijke discussies. In een turbulente groep was je een
grote steun en toeverlaat. Ik heb de samenwerking als zeer plezierig maar vooral als
erg nuttig ervaren waarvoor mijn grote dank.

Het onderzoek werd financieel gesteund door Philips, Nederlandse Philips Be-
drijven bv., en vanaf het Nat.Lab. begeleid door Clemens Lasance. Ik ben je
dankbaar voor de nuttige sturing, waar nodig, en de inhoudelijke discussies maar
vooral ook voor de grote vrijheid die ik had om het onderzoek te verrichten.

De helderheid en de technische kwalificaties van Bart Hoek zullen mij nog jaren
heugen. Ik zal nooit je meest afschuwelijke ’worst case’ scenario’s vergeten die,
dankzij je analytische denkvermogen, alleen maar in je gedachten bestonden. Voor
elk probleem, hoe eenvoudig ze in het eerste opzicht ook leken, had je wel tien
catastrofale *doom’-scenario’s, maar ook tien ’mijnbouw’-voorbeelden. Ik ben je
zeer dankbaar voor je onmisbare hulp tijdens mijn onderzoek, natuurlijk ook voor
de fraaie autocad tekeningen.

Veel dank ben ik ook verschuldigd aan Jaap Beekman. Zijn kennis van systeem
beheer, plus alles wat daarmee samenhangt, elektrotechnische problemen en data-
acquisitie en verwerking en zijn onontbeerlijk gebleken. Je positieve inzet gaf mij het
groene licht om je ook in je schaarse vrije uren lastig te vallen. Maar ik zou je maar
half beschrijven als ik niet je sociale activiteiten belicht. TP-cafe, promotie-stunts,
sinterklaas, etc, woorden waarmee je sterk geassocieerd kan worden. Bedankt voor
je onmisbare hulp.

Lucas van Vliet en Geert van Kempen hebben een onmisbare bijdrage geleverd
aan de totstandkoming van de beeld reconstructie techniek gebruikt in dit onder-
zoek om infrarood temperatuur metingen nauwkeuriger te maken. Ik kijk met veel
genoegen terug op de plezierige samenwerking en jullie enthousiaste inzet die tot
mooie resultaten hebben geleid.

Ondanks dat studenten vaak geassocieerd worden met tijd- en energie-rovend
(niet onterecht denk ik nu 4.5 jaar en 6 studenten later) kijk ik met plezier terug op
de begeleiding van een zestal van hen tijdens hun afstudeerwerk. De samenwerking

ABOUT THE AUTHOR

Born in Hengelo, The Netherlands

Secondary school VWO, at "Lyceum de Grundel’,
Hengelo (O)

Study Mechanical Engineering at the University
of Twente, Enschede

Graduation work at KSEPL, the exploration and
production research laboratory of Shell, Rijswijk,
during the period April 1993 - December 1993.
The theoretical/experimental research involved the
sound propagation in drilling fluids and, in relation
with it, the development and testing of an acoustic
detection system for the fast detection of possible
gas influxes in drilling wells.

Ph.D. student (assistent in opleiding) at the Heat
Transfer section of the faculty of Applied Physics,
Delft University of Technology, The Netherlands,
under supervision of Prof. dr K. Hanjalic

Research scientist at Philips Research (Nat.Lab.)
Nederlandse Philips Bedrijven B.V.
